# Finite-Time Concentration and Convergence Rates for Projected Two-Time-Scale Stochastic Approximation with Markov Noise

Rahul Singh¹, Vivek S. Borkar2, and Eric Moulines1,3

1Laboratoire de Recherche de l’EPITA, Paris, France

2Indian Institute of Technology Bombay

3Mohamed bin Zayed University of Artificial Intelligence, Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates

## Abstract

We study projected two-time-scale stochastic approximation algorithms driven by a controlled Markov chain. The fast recursion is governed by an averaged contractive map, while the slow recursion is constrained to a compact convex polyhedron through a projection step. The limiting slow dynamics are described by a projected differential equation, whose vector field may be discontinuous on the boundary of the constraint set. This feature prevents a direct application of standard finite-time stochastic approximation arguments based on Lipschitz limiting vector fields.

We establish explicit blockwise high-probability bounds showing that the fast iterate tracks the moving fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ of the averaged fast recursion and that the step embedding of the slow iterate tracks the associated projected ODE. The bounds separate fast and slow martingale deviations, Poisson residuals generated by Markovian sampling, and the deterministic twotime-scale bias. Under a Lipschitz Lyapunov function satisfying a uniform one-block decrease condition for the projected ODE, these estimates yield almost-sure convergence of the lastiterate Lyapunov error and explicit rates when the decrease modulus has a power lower bound near zero. Uniform one-block contraction is the linear-decrease special case.

A uniform decrease condition for a Lipschitz Lyapunov function over fixed time intervals yields almost-sure convergence of the slow Lyapunov error, with explicit last-iterate rates when the decrease admits a power lower bound near zero. Under uniform Lyapunov contraction, suitably chosen polynomial step sizes give joint fast-tracking and slow Lyapunov-error exponents arbitrarily close to 1/3, while a slow step size of order 1/n attains the fast-tracking exponent $1 / 3$ When the reduced slow update map is also a Euclidean contraction, logarithmically separated step sizes improve the joint rate to $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 / 2 } \log n )$ , including when the equilibrium lies on the boundary. The same rate holds under a distinct geometric condition: the slow equilibrium set is a strictly attracting face of a box, and the fast equilibrium is constant on that face.

We apply the results to an actor-critic algorithm, obtaining an almost-sure value-gap rate of $O ( n ^ { - 1 } \log n )$ relative to the optimum within the constrained policy class. Further applications include projected TD(0) and projected stochastic gradient descent. We also extend the analysis to projection of the fast recursion under Euclidean contractivity.

Keywords: Stochastic approximation, two-time-scale methods, projected dynamical systems, controlled Markov chains, Skorokhod map, finite-time concentration, reinforcement learning

## 1 Introduction

Stochastic approximation algorithms are iterative schemes for solving fixed-point, root-finding, and optimization problems when only noisy observations of the underlying operator are available. They play a central role in stochastic control, reinforcement learning, simulation-based optimization, and adaptive systems. Many modern applications involve coupled recursions evolving on different time scales, with one component adapting rapidly and another changing more slowly. In addition, constraints on the iterates are often enforced through projection steps. This paper studies finitetime behavior of a projected two-time-scale stochastic approximation scheme driven by a controlled Markov chain.

The algorithm consists of a fast recursion $x _ { n }$ and a slow projected recursion $\theta _ { n } .$ The slow iterate is projected onto a compact convex polyhedron Θ, while the transition probabilities of the controlled Markov chain are determined by the current value of $\theta _ { n }$ . Because the slow variable changes on a smaller time scale, the fast recursion is expected to equilibrate near the fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ of the averaged fast-time-scale map. We make this intuition quantitative by proving high-probability bounds on the tracking error $x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$

The slow recursion is then compared with the solution of an associated projected ODE. This comparison is nonstandard because the projection may make the limiting vector field discontinuous on the boundary of Θ.

We overcome this difficulty by representing each projected path through the Skorokhod map. The Lipschitz property of this map then allows us to control differences between projected paths by differences between their unconstrained driving paths. This yields finite-time concentration bounds for the slow iterate around the projected ODE trajectory. If the associated projected ODE admits a Lipschitz Lyapunov function satisfying a uniform one-block decrease condition along its trajectories, these bounds imply almost-sure convergence of the last-iterate Lyapunov error to zero. Explicit rates follow when the decrease modulus has a power lower bound near zero, with uniform one-block contraction as the linear-decrease special case.

Consider the coupled stochastic recursions

$$
x _ { n + 1 } = x _ { n } + \alpha _ { n } [ h ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - x _ { n } + M _ { n + 1 } ] ,\tag{1.1}
$$

$$
\theta _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \{ \theta _ { n } + \beta _ { n } [ f ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) + M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime } ] \} .\tag{1.2}
$$

where the step sizes satisfy $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }  0$ as $n \to \infty$ , Θ is a compact convex polyhedron, and $\mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta }$ denotes projection onto Θ. This separation of step sizes gives the two-time-scale structure: the $x _ { n } \cdot$ -recursion evolves on the faster time scale, while the θ-recursion evolves on the slower time scale. The processes $\{ M _ { n } \}$ and $\left\{ M _ { n } ^ { \prime } \right\}$ are martingale-difference noise terms. The sequence $\left\{ Y _ { n } \right\}$ is a controlled Markov chain taking values in the finite set $\mathcal { V } ,$ and its transition kernel depends on the current slow iterate $\theta _ { n }$

For each $\theta \in \Theta$ , let $\mu ^ { ( \theta ) }$ be the stationary distribution of the controlled Markov chain induced by $\theta ,$ and define

$$
h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ; \theta ) : = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) h ( y , x , \theta ) .
$$

The unique fixed point of $h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \cdot ; \theta )$ is denoted by $x ^ { \star } ( \theta )$ . We prove that the fast iterate tracks the moving fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ with high probability. We then show that the right-continuous step embedding of the slow iterate tracks the solution of the projected ODE

$$
\dot { z } ( t ) = \Pi _ { \Theta } \left( z ( t ) , \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( z ( t ) ) } ( y ) f ( y , x ^ { \star } ( z ( t ) ) , z ( t ) ) \right) .
$$

Here $\Pi _ { \Theta } ( z , v )$ denotes the projected vector at z in direction $v ;$ its formal definition is given in (4.1). Using these concentration bounds, we obtain iteration-wise almost-sure last-iterate rates for   
$\alpha _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - { \mathfrak a } }$ and $\beta _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 6 }$ , where $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { a } } < { \mathfrak { b } } \leq 1$ . Under uniform one-block contraction   
and ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ , Corollary 8.1 gives joint fast-tracking and slow Lyapunov-error rates with polynomial   
exponent arbitrarily close to $1 / 3 { \mathrm { . } }$ up to a $\sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) }$ factor. At the harmonic endpoint ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ 2   
for which $\beta _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 }$ , taking ${ \mathfrak { a } } = 2 / 3$ gives $\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 3 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } )$   
The polynomial exponent governing the slow Lyapunov error is then min $\{ \lambda _ { T } , 1 / 3 \}$ , with the precise   
logarithmic factors given in the same corollary; here $\lambda _ { T }$ is defined in (8.7).

More generally, suppose that the decrease in the Lyapunov function over one fixed block of projected-ODE time is bounded below by a positive constant times its value at the start of the block raised to a power $p _ { J } > 1$ , whenever that value is sufficiently small. Corollary 8.2 gives the optimized ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ rate

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / ( 4 p _ { J } - 1 ) } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { 1 / ( 2 p _ { J } ) } \Big ) ,
$$

whereas its harmonic-endpoint rate is $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { - 1 / ( p _ { J } - 1 ) } )$ . In Appendices S.1-S.3 of the Online Companion, we specialize the framework to the case where the fast recursion is absent, obtain sharper rate bounds for the remaining projected recursion, and give applications to projected TD(0) and projected SGD.

Additional equilibrium geometry improves these polynomial exponents. If the slow equilibrium set is a strictly attracting box face and the fast equilibrium is constant on that face, Proposition 8.1 gives a joint rate $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 / 2 } \log n )$ with $\alpha _ { n } = A \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) / ( N _ { 0 } + n )$ and $\beta _ { n } = B / ( N _ { 0 } + n )$ . Actorcritic algorithm from reinforcement learning satisfies these conditions and also achieves a value-gap rate of $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 } \log n )$

The projection step introduces a difficulty that is absent in standard finite-time analyses of stochastic approximation. The limiting dynamics are described by a projected differential equation, whose vector field need not be continuous at the boundary of the constraint set. Consequently, standard perturbation arguments for stochastic approximation with Lipschitz limiting vector fields do not directly apply. We therefore follow the ODE method (Borkar [3]): on each finite block of accumulated slow time, we compare the slow step embedding with the projected-ODE solution initialized at the block entrance. We make this projected-ODE comparison quantitative: the Lipschitz continuity of the Skorokhod map on finite time intervals yields explicit path-tracking concentration bounds, and a separate one-block Lyapunov decrease condition converts those bounds into last-iterate rates, with contraction as its linear special case.

## 1.1 Contributions

Our main contributions are as follows.

(i) We establish explicit finite-time high-probability concentration bounds showing that the fast iterate $x _ { n }$ tracks the moving fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ in projected two-time-scale stochastic approximation driven by an iterate-controlled finite-state Markov chain. Only the averaged fast map is assumed contractive; no contraction is imposed on the slow recursion, which is projected onto the compact convex polyhedron $\Theta .$ The bounds separate the effects of martingale noise, Markovian sampling, movement of the fast equilibrium, and time-scale separation; see Proposition 6.1.

(ii) We prove a finite-horizon supremum-norm concentration bound for the slow iterates around the associated projected-ODE trajectory. We use the Skorokhod map to handle the possible discontinuity of the projected vector field at the boundary; see Theorem 7.1.

(iii) For the slow iterates, we prove almost-sure convergence to the equilibrium set of the associated projected ODE, assuming that this ODE admits a Lipschitz Lyapunov function that is nonincreasing along trajectories and satisfies a uniform decrease condition over a fixed time horizon $T > 0$ The horizon can be chosen to establish this decrease, without requiring contraction or nonexpansiveness of the reduced slow update map. This goes beyond the reduced slow-map contraction assumptions of Chandak [6], Chandak et al. [9] and the nonexpansiveness assumption of Chandak [5]. The actor-critic application in Section 9 illustrates this distinction: its reduced slow update map need not be nonexpansive, while its projected ODE reaches the restricted-optimal equilibrium face in uniformly bounded time and therefore satisfies one-block Lyapunov contraction for a sufficiently large T. To quantify this convergence, we assume that, for sufficiently small Lyapunov errors, the decrease over time T is bounded below by a positive constant times a power of the current Lyapunov error. This yields explicit optimized rates, with one-block Lyapunov contraction as the linear-decrease special case. Under this contraction condition and polynomial step sizes, the joint fast-tracking and slow Lyapunov-error exponent can be taken arbitrarily close to $1 / 3 ;$ see Theorems 8.2 and 8.4 and Corollaries 8.1 and 8.2. When the slow equilibrium set is a face of a box constraint, the averaged slow drift points strictly outward in each coordinate fixed on that face, and $x ^ { \star } ( \theta )$ is constant on the face, Proposition 8.1 gives the joint rate $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 / 2 } \log n )$ with logarithmically separated step sizes.

(iv) We apply our results to an actor-critic algorithm in which the current policy controls the Markov sampling process. With logarithmically separated step sizes, the actor value gap relative to the optimum within the constrained policy class is $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 } \log n )$ , and the critic error is $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 / 2 } \log n )$ . The Online Companion contains the proof and applications to projected $\mathrm { T D } ( 0 )$ and projected SGD. It also gives a saddle-point application in Appendix S.18, including examples with vanishing curvature and rotational coupling from a bilinear term. These examples fail both nonexpansiveness of the reduced forward map in any fixed norm and uniform quadratic dissipation, but their projected ODEs satisfy a power Lyapunov decrease condition; see Remark S.18.1. Finally, we extend the analysis to algorithms that project the fast iterate onto a compact convex polyhedron at every step. This makes the fast iterates bounded by construction, removing Assumption 3.7, while retaining contraction of the averaged fast map in the Euclidean norm and bounded noise before projection.

## 2 Related work

Representative finite-time results include mean-square, and high-probability concentration bounds due to Borkar [2], Chandak et al. [8], Chen et al. [10, 11]. In two-time-scale stochastic approximation (TTSA), one first studies the fast averaged dynamics with the slow variable frozen. The corresponding reduced slow dynamics are then obtained by replacing the fast variable with its equilibrium $x ^ { \star } ( \theta )$ . Existing analyses often impose stability or contraction both on this frozen-slow fast subsystem and on the resulting equilibrium-reduced slow subsystem; see, for example, Chandak [6]. In the linear setting with i.i.d. noise, Konda and Tsitsiklis [17] characterize the asymptotic covariance of two-time-scale stochastic approximation, and establish asymptotic normality. Dalal et al. [12| derive a concentration bound for linear two-time-scale stochastic approximation, with applications to reinforcement-learning algorithms. Doan [13] prove almost-sure convergence and an $O ( k ^ { - 2 / 3 } )$ mean-square bound for nonlinear TTSA under strong-monotonicity assumptions on the frozen-slow fast operator and the equilibrium-reduced slow operator. Chandak et al. [9] obtain an $O ( n ^ { - 2 / 3 } )$ mean-square rate under arbitrary-norm contractions and Markov noise. Chandak [6] obtains $O ( k ^ { - a } )$ mean-square error for the two-time-scale schedule $\alpha _ { k } \asymp k ^ { - a } , \beta _ { k } \asymp k ^ { - 1 }$ , for $a \in ( 1 / 2 , 1 )$ . These rates use contraction on both reduced operators. Chandak [5] allows a nonexpansive slow map and a projected fast recursion, with a residual mean-square rate $O ( k ^ { - 1 / 4 + \varepsilon } )$ where $\varepsilon > 0$ is arbitrarily small. Classical works on projected stochastic approximation focus on asymptotic convergence, weak convergence, or large-deviation principles; see, for example, Dupuis and Kushner [15], Kushner and Yin [19]. Borowski and Miasojedow [4] repair the gap in Kushner and Yin [19, Theorem 5.2.1] for single-time-scale SA with projection onto a hyperrectangle, proving almost-sure convergence to the stationary set of its projected ODE.

Notation Throughout, $\| \cdot \|$ denotes the Euclidean norm for vectors and the induced Euclidean operator norm for matrices. For vectors, $\| \cdot \| _ { \infty }$ denotes the sup norm. For matrices,

$$
\| A \| _ { \infty } : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { i } \sum _ { j } | A ( i , j ) |
$$

denotes the operator norm induced by the vector sup norm, equivalently the maximum absolute rowsum norm. Boldface 0 and 1 denote, respectively, the zero and all-ones vectors of the appropriate dimension. If E is an event, then $\mathbf { 1 } \{ \mathcal { E } \}$ is its indicator. If B is a finite nonempty set, then $\Delta ( B )$ denotes the probability simplex on B, viewed as a subset of R $| B |$ . For a set X and point x, dist $( x , X )$ denotes the distance from x to $X$ . For a positive deterministic sequence ${ \boldsymbol { a } } _ { n } ,$ we write $Z _ { n } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( a _ { n } )$ if, on an event of probability one, there exist a finite nonnegative random constant C and a finite integer-valued random index N such that $| Z _ { n } | \le C a _ { n }$ for every $n \geq N$ , with absolute value replaced by the Euclidean norm for vector-valued $Z _ { n }$ . For positive deterministic sequences $\left\{ a _ { n } \right\}$ and $\left\{ b _ { n } \right\}$ , we write $a _ { n } \asymp b _ { n }$ when they are comparable up to positive multiplicative constants for all sufficiently large $n ;$ that is, when $c b _ { n } \leq a _ { n } \leq C b _ { n }$ eventually for some $0 < c \le C < \infty$ . We write $\mathbb { N } : = \{ 1 , 2 , \ldots \}$ and write $\mathbb { R } _ { + }$ for the set of nonnegative reals. For $d \geq 1$ , define

$$
\kappa _ { d } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| u \| _ { \infty } \leq 1 , \ u \in \mathbb R ^ { d } } \| u \| .
$$

Thus $\| z \| \leq \kappa _ { d } \| z \| _ { \infty }$ for every $z \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$

Sections 3-5 give the model, notation, and proof strategy; Sections 6–8 establish concentration and last-iterate rates; Section 9 presents the actor-critic application; and Section 10 concludes. Selected proofs appear in Appendix $\operatorname { A } ;$ the remaining proofs, the one-time-scale specialization and applications, the fast-projection extension, and auxiliary results are collected in the Online Companion.

## 3 Problem Formulation

We study the recursions (1.1)-(1.2) introduced in Section 1. Here $x _ { n } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } }$ is the fast iterate, $\theta _ { n } \in \Theta \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { \theta } }$ is the slow iterate, and Θ is a nonempty compact convex polyhedron. The map $\mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } ( \cdot )$ denotes Euclidean projection onto Θ. The initial slow iterate satisfies $\theta _ { 1 } \in \Theta$ almost surely. The process $\left\{ Y _ { n } \right\}$ is a controlled Markov chain taking values in the finite state space , and its transition law depends on the current slow iterate $\theta _ { n } .$ The functions $h : \mathcal { V } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } } \times \Theta  \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } }$ and $f : \mathcal { V } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } } \times \Theta \to \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { \theta } }$ define the fast and slow updates, respectively. The processes $\{ M _ { n } \}$ in $\mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } }$ and $\left\{ M _ { n } ^ { \prime } \right\}$ in $\mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { \theta } }$ are noise processes.

In addition to the time-scale separation stated in Section 1, the step sizes satisfy

$$
\sum _ { n } \alpha _ { n } = \sum _ { n } \beta _ { n } = \infty , \qquad \sum _ { n } ( \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } + \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } ) < \infty .
$$

We impose the following standing assumptions.

Assumption 3.1 (Controlled Markov chain). The process $\left\{ Y _ { n } \right\}$ is a controlled Markov process taking values in the finite state space . Let

$$
\mathcal { F } _ { n } = \sigma ( x _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 1 } , \{ Y _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } , \{ M _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } , \{ M _ { i } ^ { \prime } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } ) , n = 1 , 2 , \ldots .
$$

Then

$$
\mathbb { P } ( Y _ { n + 1 } = y ^ { \prime } | \mathcal { F } _ { n } ) = p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } , y ^ { \prime } ) , \quad n \in \mathbb { N } , \quad \mathrm { a . s . } ,
$$

where $\{ p ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y , y ^ { \prime } ) \} _ { y , y ^ { \prime } \in \mathcal { V } }$ are the controlled transition probabilities of the Markov chain induced by θ. For each $\theta \in \Theta$ , the Markov chain with transition probabilities $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ has a unique stationary distribution, denoted by $\mu ^ { ( \theta ) }$

Assumption 3.2 (Lipschitz transition kernel). There exists $L _ { p } > 0$ such that, for all $i \in \mathcal { V }$ and all $\theta , \theta ^ { \prime } \in \Theta$ 2

$$
\sum _ { j \in \mathcal { V } } | p ^ { ( \theta ) } ( i , j ) - p ^ { ( \theta ^ { \prime } ) } ( i , j ) | \leq L _ { p } \| \theta - \theta ^ { \prime } \| .
$$

Thus, the controlled transition kernel is Lipschitz in $\theta .$

This regularity condition is standard in finite-time analyses of stochastic approximation; see, e.g., Chandak et al. [8], Metivier and Priouret [20].

Assumption 3.3 (Martingale-difference noise). The sequences $\{ M _ { n } \}$ and $\{ M _ { n } ^ { \prime } \}$ are martingaledifference sequences with respect to $\{ \mathcal { F } _ { n } \}$ . Thus,

$$
\mathbb { E } ( M _ { n + 1 } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ) = \mathbf { 0 } , \qquad \mathbb { E } ( M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ) = \mathbf { 0 } , \quad \mathrm { ~ a . s . }
$$

Moreover, there exist constants ${ \mathfrak { c } } _ { 1 } , { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 2 } > 0$ such that, almost surely,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| M _ { n + 1 } \| \leq \mathfrak { c } _ { 1 } + \mathfrak { c } _ { 2 } \| \mathfrak { x } _ { n } \| , \qquad \| M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime } \| \leq \mathfrak { c } _ { 1 } + \mathfrak { c } _ { 2 } \| \mathfrak { x } _ { n } \| , \quad \forall n \in \mathbb { N } . } \end{array}\tag{3.1}
$$

Martingale-difference noise assumptions of this form are standard in stochastic approximation; see, $\mathrm { e . g . }$ , Borkar [3], Kushner and Yin [19].

Assumption 3.4 (Uniform contraction of the averaged fast map). For each $\theta \in \Theta$ define the averaged fast-time-scale map

$$
h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( x ; \theta ) : = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) h ( y , x , \theta ) , \quad x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } } .\tag{3.2}
$$

For a fixed $\theta , h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ; \theta )$ is the mean fast-update map obtained by freezing the slow parameter and averaging $h ( y , x , \theta )$ with respect to the stationary distribution $\mu ^ { ( \theta ) }$ of the resulting Markov chain. Thus, $h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ; \theta ) - x$ is the corresponding averaged drift of the fast recursion. There exists $\gamma \in [ 0 , 1 )$ such that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( { \boldsymbol x } ; { \boldsymbol \theta } ) - h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( { \boldsymbol x } ^ { \prime } ; { \boldsymbol \theta } ) \right\| \leq \gamma \| { \boldsymbol x } - { \boldsymbol x } ^ { \prime } \| , \quad { \boldsymbol x } , { \boldsymbol x } ^ { \prime } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } } , { \boldsymbol \theta } \in \Theta . } \end{array}
$$

The unique fixed point of $h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \cdot ; \theta )$ is denoted by $x ^ { \star } ( \theta )$ , so that

$$
h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) ; \theta ) = x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) .
$$

Remark 3.1 (Fast-scale contraction). Assumption 3.4 imposes a contraction condition only on the averaged fast-time-scale map. No contractivity assumption on a discrete slow update map is needed for the concentration and path-tracking results; the slow dynamics are instead characterized through the projected ODE studied later. The constant $\gamma$ may be replaced by a parameter-dependent contraction factor $\gamma ( \theta )$ , provided $\operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \gamma ( \theta ) < 1$

The extension to an arbitrary fixed norm for the fast coordinate is recorded in Remark S.5.1 of the Online Companion.

Assumption 3.5 (Lipschitz update functions on bounded fast-state sets). For every $0 \leq R < \infty$ 2 there exist finite constants $L _ { f } ( R ) , L _ { h , x } ( R )$ , and $L _ { h , \theta } ( R )$ such that, for all $y \in \mathcal { V } , \theta , \tilde { \theta } \in \Theta$ , and $x , \tilde { x } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } }$ satisfying $\| x \| , \| \tilde { x } \| \leq R$

$$
\| f ( y , x , \theta ) - f ( y , \tilde { x } , \tilde { \theta } ) \| \leq L _ { f } ( R ) \Big ( \| x - \tilde { x } \| + \| \theta - \tilde { \theta } \| \Big ) ,
$$

and

$$
\| h ( y , x , \theta ) - h ( y , \tilde { x } , \tilde { \theta } ) \| \leq L _ { h , x } ( R ) \| x - \tilde { x } \| + L _ { h , \theta } ( R ) \| \theta - \tilde { \theta } \| .
$$

The constants may depend on $R ,$ but not on $y , x , \tilde { x } , \theta .$ or $\tilde { \theta }$ within the displayed sets. The moduli may be chosen nondecreasing in R.

For $0 \leq R < \infty$ , Assumption 3.5, finiteness of , and compactness of Θ imply

$$
\mathsf { B } ( R ) : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \substack { y \in \mathcal { Y } , \theta \in \Theta } } \operatorname* { m a x } \{ \| h ( y , x , \theta ) \| , \| f ( y , x , \theta ) \| \} < \infty .
$$

Write $\mathsf { B } _ { 0 } : = \mathsf { B } ( 0 )$

Assumption 3.6 (Uniform hitting-time bound). There exists a reference state $y ^ { \star } \in \mathcal { V }$ and ${ \mathfrak { c } } _ { 5 } < \infty$ such that, for every $\theta \in \Theta$ and every initial state $y \in \mathcal { V }$ ，

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { y } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( \tau _ { y ^ { \star } } ) \leq \mathfrak { c } _ { 5 } ,
$$

where $\tau _ { y ^ { \star } } : =$ inf $\{ n \geq 1 : Y _ { n } = y ^ { \star } \}$ for the Markov chain with transition probabilities $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ . The superscript $( \theta )$ on $\mathbb { E } _ { y } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ indicates that the chain uses the fixed transition kernel $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ at every step, while the subscript y specifies the initial state $Y _ { 0 } = y$

This condition provides uniform control of the Poisson-equation solutions used to decompose the Markov noise; see Lemmas 6.1 and 7.1. The next lemma records two regularity consequences of the preceding controlled-Markov assumptions. Together, Assumptions 3.2 and 3.6 imply Lipschitz continuity of the stationary-distribution map. Combined with Assumptions 3.5 and 3.4, this also gives Lipschitz continuity of $x ^ { \star } ( \cdot )$

Lemma 3.1. The stationary distribution map $\boldsymbol { \theta } \mapsto \boldsymbol { \mu } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ and the fxed-point map $\theta \mapsto x ^ { \star } ( \theta )$ are Lipschitz on Θ. That is, there exist constants $L _ { \mu } , L _ { f p } \geq 0$ such that

$$
\left\| { \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } - \mu ^ { ( \theta ^ { \prime } ) } } \right\| _ { 1 } \leq L _ { \mu } \left\| { \theta - \theta ^ { \prime } } \right\| , \qquad \left\| { x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) - x ^ { \star } ( \theta ^ { \prime } ) } \right\| \leq L _ { f p } \left\| { \theta - \theta ^ { \prime } } \right\| , \qquad \theta , \theta ^ { \prime } \in \Theta .
$$

Here, for any signed measure ν on $\mathcal { V }$

$$
\| \nu \| _ { 1 } : = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { y } } | \nu ( y ) | .
$$

In particular, su $\operatorname { p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \| x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) \| < \infty$

The proof is given in Appendix S.4 of the Online Companion. We henceforth write

$$
\mathfrak { c } _ { 8 } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \| x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) \| \le \frac { \mathsf { B } _ { 0 } } { 1 - \gamma } < \infty .
$$

Assumption 3.7 (Stability of the fast iterate). There exists a deterministic constant $c _ { 6 } < \infty$ such that

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { n \in \mathbb { N } } \| x _ { n } \| \leq { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 6 } \ a . s .
$$

Contraction of the averaged fast map does not by itself keep the fast iterates bounded: individual sampled updates may still produce large excursions. Assumption 3.7 ensures that the martingale increments, Poisson-equation solutions, and drift perturbations used throughout the concentration proof are uniformly bounded. It can be verified by finding a deterministic bounded set that contains the initial iterate and is preserved by the recursion, as illustrated in the Online Companion. Alternatively, projecting the fast iterate onto a compact convex polyhedron at every step ensures boundedness by construction. Appendix S.9 develops this extension, removing the need for Assumption 3.7.

Set

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathsf { B } _ { 6 } : = \mathsf { B } ( \mathfrak { c } _ { 6 } ) , \qquad \mathsf { B } _ { 8 } : = \mathsf { B } ( \mathfrak { c } _ { 8 } ) , \qquad R _ { \mathrm { l o c } } : = \operatorname* { m a x } \{ \mathfrak { c } _ { 6 } , \mathfrak { c } _ { 8 } \} . } \end{array}
$$

Hereafter, $L _ { f } , L _ { h , x } .$ and $L _ { h , \theta }$ denote, respectively, the constants $L _ { f } ( R _ { \mathrm { l o c } } ) , L _ { h , x } ( R _ { \mathrm { l o c } } )$ , and $L _ { h , \theta } ( R _ { \mathrm { l o c } } )$ from Assumption 3.5. The ball of radius $R _ { \mathrm { l o c } }$ contains every fast iterate and every fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta )$

Unless an alternative schedule is stated explicitly, we use polynomial step sizes with a positiveinteger shift $N _ { 0 } \geq 1$

$$
\alpha _ { n } = \frac { 1 } { ( n + N _ { 0 } ) ^ { \mathfrak { a } } } , \beta _ { n } = \frac { 1 } { ( n + N _ { 0 } ) ^ { \mathfrak { b } } } , n \in \mathbb { N } ,
$$

where ${ \mathfrak { a } } \in ( 0 , 1 )$ and ${ \mathfrak { b } } \in ( 0 , 1 ]$ . The exponent a corresponds to the fast recursion and b to the slow recursion. Since the slow step size must decay faster, we require ${ \mathfrak { a } } < { \mathfrak { b } }$ . The hyperparameters a, b satisfy the following

Condition 3.1 (Step-size exponents). The step-size exponents satisfy

$$
\frac { 1 } { 2 } < a < { \mathfrak { b } } \leq 1 .
$$

The shift $N _ { 0 }$ is part of the algorithm. For the polynomial two-time-scale schedule above, we assume throughout, unless explicitly stated otherwise, that

$$
( N _ { 0 } + 1 ) ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak { a } } } \geq 4 .\tag{3.3}
$$

This choice ensures that the step-size bounds needed in the analysis hold from index $n = 1$ ; see Appendix S.16 of the Online Companion.

Alternatively, we can begin the analysis after a finite number of iterations. In this case, we take $N _ { 0 } = 1$ and run the recursion from $n = 1$ , but begin the block construction at the first index $n _ { 0 } \geq 1$ satisfying

$$
\left( n _ { 0 } + 1 \right) ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak { a } } } \geq 4 .
$$

Appendix S.16 of the Online Companion verifies that the analysis applies from $n = n _ { 0 }$ , with convergence rates expressed in terms of $n + 1$

## 4 Continuous-time step embeddings and projected dynamical systems

We first introduce the cumulative time grid and the right-continuous step paths used in the projected-ODE analysis. For $n \geq m$ , write

$$
a ( m , n ) : = \sum _ { k = m } ^ { n - 1 } \beta _ { k } , \qquad b ( n ) : = \sum _ { k \ge n } \beta _ { k } ^ { 2 } ,
$$

and

$$
\chi ( m , n ) : = \left\{ \prod _ { k = m } ^ { n } ( 1 - \alpha _ { k } ) , ~ m \leq n , \right.
$$

For $\ell \geq 1$ , define the cumulative time grid associated with the θ-recursion by

$$
t ( \ell ) : = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \ell - 1 } \beta _ { k } .
$$

The empty sum is zero, so $t ( 1 ) = 0$ . Moreover,

$$
t ( n + 1 ) - t ( n ) = \beta _ { n } , \qquad t ( n ) - t ( m ) = a ( m , n ) , \qquad n \geq m \geq 1 .
$$

Define the right-continuous step embedding of the slow iterate by

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \theta ^ { \circ } ( r ) : = \theta _ { n } , \qquad r \in [ t ( n ) , t ( n + 1 ) ) . } \end{array}
$$

Thus,

$$
\theta ^ { \circ } ( t ( n ) ) = \theta _ { n } .
$$

By construction, the path $\theta ^ { \circ }$ is right-continuous with left limits (càdlàg) and piecewise constant. Fix a slow-time block horizon $T > 0$ . Let $n _ { 0 }$ be the starting index of the analysis specified in Section 3, with $n _ { 0 } = 1$ under the default choice of $N _ { 0 }$ . Define recursively

$$
n _ { m } : = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ n > n _ { m - 1 } : t ( n ) \geq t ( n _ { m - 1 } ) + T \} , \qquad m \geq 1 .
$$

For $m \geq 0$ set

$$
T _ { m } : = t ( n _ { m } ) , \qquad K _ { m } : = \{ n _ { m } , n _ { m } + 1 , \ldots , n _ { m + 1 } - 1 \} ,
$$

and write

$$
K _ { m } : = n _ { m + 1 } - n _ { m } , \qquad H _ { m } : = T _ { m + 1 } - T _ { m } ,
$$

We call $\kappa _ { m }$ the mth discrete-time block; its associated slow-time interval is $[ T _ { m } , T _ { m + 1 } ]$ . A local index k measures displacement from the block entrance $n _ { m }$ , with $k = K _ { m }$ corresponding to the right endpoint $n _ { m + 1 }$

For $0 \leq \ell \leq K _ { m }$ , define the shifted slow-time grid by

$$
t ^ { ( m ) } ( \ell ) : = t ( n _ { m } + \ell ) - t ( n _ { m } ) .
$$

Then

$$
t ^ { ( m ) } ( 0 ) = 0 , t ^ { ( m ) } ( K _ { m } ) = H _ { m } ,
$$

and

$$
t ^ { ( m ) } ( \ell + 1 ) - t ^ { ( m ) } ( \ell ) = \beta _ { n _ { m } + \ell } .
$$

For any discrete-time sequence $\{ u _ { n } \} _ { n \ge 1 }$ , write

$$
u _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } : = u _ { n _ { m } + \ell } .
$$

For any sequence $\{ q _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \} _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } }$ indexed by the local position k relative to the entrance of block $m ,$ define its right-continuous block step path by

$$
\begin{array} { r } { q ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) : = q _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } , \qquad r \in [ t ^ { ( m ) } ( k ) , t ^ { ( m ) } ( k + 1 ) ) , \quad 0 \leq k < K _ { m } , } \end{array}
$$

and set

$$
q ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( H _ { m } ) : = q _ { K _ { m } } ^ { ( m ) } .
$$

Let Γ denote the normal-reflection Skorokhod map on Θ, and let $L _ { \mathrm { S k } }$ denote a finite Lipschitz constant for this map, as stated in Theorem S.13.1 of the Online Companion. When a block path is passed to the Skorokhod map, it is extended constantly beyond $H _ { m } ;$ this does not affect any block estimate. In particular,

$$
\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) = \theta ^ { \circ } ( T _ { m } + r ) , \qquad 0 \leq r \leq H _ { m } .
$$

We use parenthesized superscripts to label shifted objects. Thus, $u _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) }$ denotes a discrete-time sequence reindexed at the block entrance $n _ { m }$ , whereas $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) }$ denotes the projected-ODE trajectory initialized at the slow-time anchor $T _ { m }$

## 4.1 Projected dynamical systems

Following Dupuis and Nagurney [16], let $C \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ be a nonempty closed convex set, and let projc denote Euclidean projection onto C. For $z \in C$ and $v \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ , define

$$
\Pi _ { C } ( z , v ) : = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { \varepsilon \downarrow 0 } { \frac { { \mathrm { p r o j } _ { C } ( z + \varepsilon v ) - z } } { \varepsilon } } .\tag{4.1}
$$

For $\varepsilon > 0$ , also define

$$
\Pi _ { C , \varepsilon } ( z , v ) : = \frac { \mathrm { p r o j } _ { C } ( z + \varepsilon v ) - z } { \varepsilon } .\tag{4.2}
$$

We omit the set subscript when it is clear from the context.

Definition 4.1 (Dupuis and Nagurney [16]). Let $F : C \to \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ . A function $z : [ 0 , \infty ) \to C$ is a solution of $\dot { z } = \Pi _ { C } ( z , F ( z ) )$ if it is absolutely continuous and

$$
\dot { z } ( t ) = \Pi _ { C } ( z ( t ) , F ( z ( t ) ) )
$$

for almost every $t \geq 0$

For the slow recursion, we apply these definitions with $C = \Theta$ and $d = d _ { \theta }$

## 5 Concentration proof sketch and overview

The concentration arguments are developed in Sections 6 and $7 ;$ selected proofs appear in $\mathrm { A p \mathrm { - } }$ pendix A and supporting proofs in the Online Companion. Since the fast recursion is contractive after averaging, the first step of the analysis is to show that $x _ { n }$ tracks the moving fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ with high probability. This allows the slow recursion to be compared with an auxiliary projected recursion in which $x _ { n }$ is replaced by $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ . After averaging over the controlled Markov chain, the limiting slow dynamics are described by the differential equation

$$
\dot { z } = \Pi _ { \Theta } ( z ( t ) , f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( z ( t ) ; z ( t ) ) ) ,\tag{5.1}
$$

where, for $\theta , \theta ^ { \prime } \in \Theta$ , we define

$$
f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta ; \theta ^ { \prime } ) : = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ^ { \prime } ) } ( y ) f ( y , x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) , \theta ) .\tag{5.2}
$$

In $f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \theta ; \theta ^ { \prime } )$ , the first argument θ is substituted into both the fixed-point map $x ^ { \star } ( \cdot )$ and the parameter coordinate of $f ,$ while the second argument $\theta ^ { \prime }$ determines the stationary distribution used to average over $y .$ We refer to $f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } }$ as the two-parameter averaged field. Its evaluation is called diagonal when the two arguments agree and off-diagonal otherwise.

We first establish a high-probability bound on the fast tracking error $\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \|$ ; see Proposition 6.1. Using this estimate, we then obtain a concentration bound on the maximum deviation of the embedding of the slow iterates from the projected-ODE trajectory over each finite slow-time block; see Theorem 7.1

For $\tau \geq 0$ , let $z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( \cdot )$ denote the solution to (5.1) satisfying

$$
z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( 0 ) = \theta ^ { \circ } ( \tau ) .
$$

In particular, on the mth block, we compare

$$
\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) = \theta ^ { \circ } ( T _ { m } + r )
$$

with $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( r )$ for $0 \leq r \leq H _ { m }$

Our analysis has three main components. First, Section 6 establishes a high-probability tracking bound for the fast iterate $x _ { n }$ around the moving equilibrium $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ . Second, Section 7 compares the step embedding $\theta ^ { \circ }$ with the projected ODE over finite time intervals. Third, Section 8 combines this finite-time tracking estimate with a Lipschitz Lyapunov function satisfying a uniform one-block decrease condition for the projected ODE to obtain almost-sure convergence. Explicit rates follow when, for sufficiently small Lyapunov errors, the decrease over a fixed time horizon is bounded below by a positive constant times a power of the current error. One-block Lyapunov contraction is the linear-decrease special case.

The main technical obstacle is the projection. The projected vector field in the limiting ODE may be discontinuous on the boundary of Θ, so standard stochastic-approximation concentration arguments based on Lipschitz ODE fields do not apply directly. We instead use right-continuous step embeddings of the discrete projected recursions and their unconstrained drivers. More precisely, each projected step path is exactly the image of its corresponding unconstrained driving path under the Skorokhod map. The finite-horizon supremum-norm Lipschitz property of that map then controls the difference between two projected paths through the difference between their unconstrained drivers.

## 6 Blockwise concentration of the fast iterate

We analyze the fast recursion on the slow-time blocks introduced in Section 4. On block $m ,$ our goal is to bound the fast tracking error, the distance between the fast iterate and its moving equilibrium:

$$
\| x _ { n _ { m } + k } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n _ { m } + k } ) \| , \qquad 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } .
$$

We bound this error through two comparisons: first, between the original fast recursion and a blockrestarted averaged recursion; second, between this averaged recursion and the moving equilibrium.

Fix $m \geq 0$ . For $0 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ , write

$$
x _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = x _ { n _ { m } + k } , \qquad \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = \theta _ { n _ { m } + k } , \qquad \alpha _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = \alpha _ { n _ { m } + k } , \qquad \beta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = \beta _ { n _ { m } + k } .
$$

$\mathrm { O n }$ block $m ,$ we compare the original fast iterate with an averaged recursion initialized at the same value $x _ { n _ { m } }$ and driven by the same slow-parameter sequence $\{ \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \} _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } }$ . Consequently, the difference between these two recursions vanishes at the block entrance and records only the explicit martingale increments and the Markov noise terms

$$
h ( Y _ { n _ { m } + k } , x _ { n _ { m } + k } , \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n _ { m } + k } ; \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } )
$$

accumulated within the block. The discrepancy already present at time $n _ { m }$ enters through the distance between the averaged comparison recursion and the moving fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } )$ 1

## 6.1 Block-restarted averaged recursion

Define the block-restarted averaged recursion $\{ \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \} _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } }$ by

$$
\widetilde { x } _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } : = x _ { n _ { m } } ,
$$

and, for $0 \leq k < K _ { m }$

$$
\widetilde { x } _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } + \alpha _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \left[ h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) - \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right] .\tag{6.1}
$$

Since $\left\| h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( 0 ; \theta ) \right\| \leq \mathsf { B } _ { 0 }$ , contraction gives

$$
\left\| \widetilde { x } _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \leq \{ 1 - ( 1 - \gamma ) \alpha _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \} \left\| \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| + \alpha _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \mathsf { B } _ { 0 } .
$$

Since $\widetilde { x } _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } = x _ { n _ { m } }$ , induction gives

$$
\| \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \| \leq R _ { \mathrm { a u x } } : = \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ \mathfrak { c } _ { 6 } , \frac { \mathsf { B } _ { 0 } } { 1 - \gamma } \right\} .
$$

The auxiliary iterates need not lie in the ball of radius $R _ { \mathrm { l o c } }$ . At these iterates, we use the global contraction of the averaged map; no bounded-set estimate for the unaveraged maps h or $f$ is needed. The recursion (6.1) applies the averaged map $h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } }$ from (3.2) to its own state $\widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) }$ , while using the same slow-parameter sequence $\{ \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \} _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } }$ . Thus it contains neither the explicit noise increment $M _ { n _ { m } + k + 1 }$ nor the sampled Markov state $Y _ { n _ { m } + k }$ . It remains random only through its block initial value and the random sequence $\{ \boldsymbol { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \}$

To quantify the first comparison—the discrepancy between the original fast recursion and this averaged recursion—we define for $0 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ 2

$$
D _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = x _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } .
$$

By construction, $D _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } = \mathbf { 0 }$

## 6.2 Poisson decomposition and local fast fluctuations

The Markov noise

$$
h ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } )
$$

is generally not a martingale difference because $Y _ { n }$ is Markovian. The Poisson equation is used to decompose this error into a martingale-difference term and a difference between two successivestate evaluations of the Poisson solution. Since the sampling error enters the fast update multiplied by $\alpha _ { n }$ , iterating the recursion produces weighted partial sums of the two terms. We control the martingale contribution using a concentration inequality and bound the remaining Poisson residual by summation by parts, using the slow variation of $( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } )$

Definition 6.1 (Poisson equation for the fast recursion). Fix $\boldsymbol { x } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } }$ and $\theta \in \Theta$ . For the Markov chain on $\mathcal { V }$ with transition matrix $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ , consider the Poisson equation

$$
u ( y ) - \sum _ { i \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y , i ) u ( i ) = h ( y , x , \theta ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ; \theta ) , \qquad y \in \mathcal { Y } .\tag{6.2}
$$

We use the particular solution, as in Chandak et al. [8]

$$
u _ { h } ^ { ( x , \theta ) } ( y ) : = \mathbb { E } ^ { ( \theta ) } \left[ \sum _ { r = 0 } ^ { \tau _ { y } \star - 1 } ( h ( Y _ { r } , x , \theta ) - h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( x ; \theta ) ) \bigg | Y _ { 0 } = y \right] ,\tag{6.3}
$$

where $\tau _ { y ^ { \star } }$ is the hitting time of the reference state $y ^ { \star }$ from Assumption 3.6. The superscript (θ) on $\mathbb { E } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ indicates that the controlled Markov chain $( Y _ { r } )$ is run with transition matrix $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ ; the arguments x and θ in the summand are held fixed as the chain evolves.

The expectation in (6.3) is finite by Assumption 3.6. Moreover, by the definition of $h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } }$

$$
\sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) \left\{ h ( y , x , \theta ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ; \theta ) \right\} = \bf { 0 } .
$$

Lemma S.15.7 of the Online Companion, applied coordinatewise, therefore gives $u _ { h } ^ { ( x , \theta ) } ( y ^ { \star } ) = \mathbf { 0 }$ Conditioning on the first transition then shows that (6.3) solves (6.2).

Let $C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } < \infty$ denote the Poisson-sensitivity constant from Lemma S.10.1. In particular, whenever $\lVert x \rVert , \lVert x ^ { \prime } \rVert \leq \mathfrak { c } _ { 6 } , \theta , \theta ^ { \prime } \in \Theta$ , and $y \in \mathcal { V }$

$$
\left\| u _ { h } ^ { ( x , \theta ) } ( y ) - u _ { h } ^ { ( x ^ { \prime } , \theta ^ { \prime } ) } ( y ) \right\| \le C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } ( \| x - x ^ { \prime } \| + \| \theta - \theta ^ { \prime } \| ) .
$$

Lemma 6.1 (Poisson decomposition). Define

$$
M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime } : = u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) - \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } , y ) u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( y ) , \qquad n \ge 1 .\tag{6.4}
$$

Then $\{ M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime } \} _ { n \ge 1 }$ is a martingale-difference sequence with respect to $\{ \mathcal { F } _ { n } \}$ , and, almost surely,

$$
h ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } ) = M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime } + u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } ) - u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) .\tag{6.5}
$$

The sequence $\{ M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime } \}$ is defined on the original time axis and therefore does not carry a block index. On block m, the relevant increments are simply

$$
M _ { n _ { m } + k + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime } , \qquad 0 \leq k < K _ { m } .
$$

For later use, define

$$
\begin{array} { l l } { \Delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { h } : = u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } ) - u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) , } \\ { \Xi _ { n + 1 } : = M _ { n + 1 } + M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime } , \qquad n \ge 1 . } \end{array}
$$

Then (6.5) becomes

$$
h ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } ) + M _ { n + 1 } = \Xi _ { n + 1 } + \Delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { h } .
$$

Set

$$
C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( x ) } : = \mathfrak { c } _ { 1 } + \mathfrak { c } _ { 2 } \mathfrak { c } _ { 6 } + 4 \mathfrak { c } _ { 5 } \mathsf { B } _ { 6 } , \qquad c _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { x } : = \frac { 1 } { 4 \kappa _ { d _ { x } } ^ { 2 } ( C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( x ) } ) ^ { 2 } } .
$$

For $0 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ , define the local weighted martingale sum using $\alpha _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } = \alpha _ { n _ { m } + j }$ , by

$$
\mathcal { M } _ { m , k } ^ { x } : = \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } \chi ( n _ { m } + j + 1 , n _ { m } + k - 1 ) \Xi _ { n _ { m } + j + 1 } ,\tag{6.6}
$$

with $\mathcal { M } _ { m , 0 } ^ { x } : = \mathbf { 0 }$ . For $\delta > 0$ , define the blockwise fast-martingale event

$$
\mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta ) : = \left. \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } } \kappa _ { d _ { x } } \left\| \mathcal { M } _ { m , k } ^ { x } \right\| _ { \infty } < \delta \right. .\tag{6.7}
$$

Lemma 6.2 (Blockwise fast-martingale tail bound). For every $m \geq 0$ and $\delta > 0$

$$
\mathbb { P } \left( \left[ \mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta ) \right] ^ { c } \right) \leq 2 d _ { x } K _ { m } \exp \left\{ - \frac { c _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { x } \delta ^ { 2 } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \right\} .\tag{6.8}
$$

Equivalently,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { P } \left( \left[ \mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta ) \right] ^ { c } \right) \leq 2 d _ { x } K _ { m } \exp \left\{ - c _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { x } \delta ^ { 2 } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { \mathfrak { a } } \right\} . } \end{array}
$$

## 6.3 Comparison with the block-restarted averaged recursion

The successive-state term in the Poisson decomposition produces a stepsize-weighted telescoping residual. Summation by parts and the slow variation of $( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } )$ bound this residual by $O ( \alpha _ { n _ { m } } )$ Contraction then converts the martingale and residual terms into the following comparison. The residual calculation remains in Appendix S.5; see Sections A.2 and A.3 for the comparison proofs.

Lemma 6.3 (Local comparison with the averaged recursion). There exists $C _ { \mathrm { a u x } } < \infty$ , depending only on the problem parameters, such that, on ${ \mathcal G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta )$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| x _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \le C _ { \mathrm { a u x } } \bigl ( \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } \bigr ) , \qquad 0 \le k \le K _ { m } . } \end{array}\tag{6.9}
$$

## 6.4 Tracking the moving fixed point

We next compare $\widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) }$ with the moving fixed point $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } )$ . Set $q : = 1 - \gamma$ and, for $0 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ 2 define

$$
\mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) : = \prod _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \left( 1 - q \alpha _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \right) ,
$$

with the empty product equal to one. Thus ${ \mathcal { W } } _ { m } ( k )$ is the contraction accumulated from the block entrance to local index k

Lemma 6.4 (Moving-equilibrium comparison on a block). There exists $C _ { \mathrm { m o v } } < \infty$ , depending only on the problem parameters, such that, almost surely, for every $m \geq 0$ and $0 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ 2

$$
\begin{array} { c } { \displaystyle \left\| \tilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\| \leq \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) \left\| x _ { n _ { m } } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n _ { m } } ) \right\| } \\ { \displaystyle + \frac { C _ { \mathrm { m o v } } } { q } \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } . } \end{array}\tag{6.10}
$$

The two terms in (6.10) are, respectively, the contracted block-entry error and the deterministic tracking lag of order $\beta _ { n _ { m } } / \alpha _ { n _ { m } }$

## 6.5 Blockwise fast tracking

Finally, we combine the comparisons in Lemmas 6.3 and 6.4 using the triangle inequality. This gives pointwise, stepsize-weighted, and block-endpoint bounds for the fast tracking error. The block-endpoint estimate can then be iterated over successive blocks.

Proposition 6.1 (Blockwise fast tracking). Let

$$
\varepsilon _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } : = \| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| .
$$

There exists $C _ { x } < \infty$ , depending only on the problem parameters, such that, on ${ \mathcal G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta )$ 2

$$
\varepsilon _ { n _ { m } + k } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) \varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { x } \left( \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \right) , \qquad 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } .\tag{6.11}
$$

Moreover, there exists $C _ { x , T } < \infty$ , depending only on T and the problem parameters, such that, on the same event,

$$
\sum _ { r = n _ { m } } ^ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \beta _ { r } \lVert x _ { r } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { r } ) \rVert \leq C _ { x , T } \left( \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \right) .\tag{6.12}
$$

At the block endpoint

$$
\varepsilon _ { n _ { m + 1 } } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( K _ { m } ) \varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { x } \left( \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \right) .\tag{6.13}
$$

Proof. See Appendix A.1.

For $m \geq 0$ and $\delta > 0$ , set

$$
g _ { m } ^ { ( x ) } ( \delta ) : = \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } .\tag{6.14}
$$

Corollary 6.1 (Fast tracking across successive blocks). For every $m \geq 1$ and every choice of positive real numbers $\delta _ { 0 } , \ldots , \delta _ { m - 1 } ,$ on $\cap _ { j = 0 } ^ { m - 1 } { \mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( j ) } } ( \delta _ { j } )$ ，

$$
\varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \left( \prod _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { m - 1 } \mathcal { W } _ { \ell } ( K _ { \ell } ) \right) \varepsilon _ { n _ { 0 } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { x } \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { m - 1 } \left( \prod _ { \ell = j + 1 } ^ { m - 1 } \mathcal { W } _ { \ell } ( K _ { \ell } ) \right) g _ { j } ^ { ( x ) } ( \delta _ { j } ) .\tag{6.15}
$$

Furthermore,

$$
\mathbb { P } \left( \bigcup _ { j = 0 } ^ { m - 1 } \left[ \mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( j ) } ( \delta _ { j } ) \right] ^ { c } \right) \leq \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { m - 1 } 2 d _ { x } K _ { j } \exp \left\{ - \frac { c _ { \operatorname* { m g } } ^ { x } \delta _ { j } ^ { 2 } } { \alpha _ { n _ { j } } } \right\} .\tag{6.16}
$$

## 7 Concentration of the slow iterate

We now establish a blockwise high-probability comparison between the step embedding of the slow iterate and the projected ODE (5.1). On each block, we introduce an auxiliary projected recursion and decompose the comparison into two stages. First, we compare the original slow recursion with the auxiliary recursion, thereby isolating the fast-tracking error, the martingale noise in the slow recursion, and the Markov noise. Second, we compare the auxiliary recursion with the projected ODE. This second comparison controls the time-grid discretization error and the mismatch arising because the auxiliary recursion evaluates the first and second arguments of the averaged field at its own current state and the original slow state, respectively, whereas the ODE evaluates both arguments at its current state.

Throughout this section, "auxiliary recursion" refers to the slow projected recursion introduced below. It is distinct from the block-restarted averaged fast recursion of Section 6.

Since the projection operator is nonlinear, both comparisons are carried out through the corresponding unconstrained driving paths. Bounds on the driving paths are then transferred to the projected paths using the Lipschitz property of the Skorokhod map.

Fix $m \geq 0$ and use the block notation introduced in Section 4. In particular, for $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m }$ 2 write

$$
\theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } : = \theta _ { n _ { m } + n } , \qquad x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } : = x _ { n _ { m } + n } , \qquad Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } : = Y _ { n _ { m } + n } .
$$

For $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m } - 1$ , define

$$
\beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } : = \beta _ { n _ { m } + n } , \qquad \quad ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } : = M _ { n _ { m } + n + 1 } ^ { \prime } .
$$

For $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m }$ , also define the block-shifted filtration and the fast-tracking error by

$$
\mathscr { F } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } : = \mathscr { F } _ { n _ { m } + n } , \qquad e _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } : = x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) .
$$

Throughout this section, the parenthesized superscript (m) denotes reindexing relative to the mth block; it is neither an exponent nor an abbreviation for "martingale."

Our goal is to control

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq H _ { m } } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( t ) \right\| ,
$$

where $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) }$ solves (5.1) with

$$
z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( 0 ) = \theta ^ { \circ } ( T _ { m } ) = \theta _ { n _ { m } } .
$$

## 7.1 Auxiliary projected recursion

The shifted slow recursion can be written as

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \theta _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \Pi _ { \Theta , \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } } \left( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) + \mathcal { E } _ { f , n } ^ { ( m ) } + ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right) , } \end{array}
$$

where

$$
\mathcal { E } _ { f , n } ^ { ( m ) } : = f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) .
$$

Thus, $\mathcal { E } _ { f , n } ^ { ( m ) }$ is the drift error caused by the fast iterate not being at its moving fixed point.

Define the auxiliary projected recursion by setting

$$
\tilde { \theta } _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } = \theta _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) }
$$

and, for $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m } - 1$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \widetilde { \theta } _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \widetilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \Pi _ { \Theta , \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } } \left( \widetilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \widetilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right) . } \end{array}\tag{7.1}
$$

Equivalently,

$$
\tilde { \theta } _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \left( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right) .
$$

In (7.1), the two-parameter averaged field is evaluated off diagonal:

$$
f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( y ) f \big ( y , x ^ { \star } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \big ) .
$$

The auxiliary iterate ${ \widetilde \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) }$ determines where the drift is evaluated. The original slow iterate $\theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) }$ determines the averaging distribution $\mu ^ { ( \boldsymbol { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) }$ , since the Markov chain uses the transition kernel $p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) }$ at this iteration. Replacing the second argument by ${ \widetilde { \theta } } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) }$ gives the diagonal field used by the projected ODE. The error introduced by this replacement is bounded by a constant times $\lVert \widetilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } - \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \rVert$ , which we control by comparing the original and auxiliary recursions.

Before comparing the projected paths, we compare the unprojected update directions that drive their Skorokhod representations. The difference between the original and auxiliary update directions has the exact decomposition

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) + ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } \\ & { \quad = \big [ f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \big ] } \\ & { \quad \quad + \big [ f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \big ] } \\ & { \quad \quad + \big [ f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \big ] + ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } . } \end{array}
$$

The four terms are, respectively, the fast-tracking error, the centered Markovian sampling error, the auxiliary-state mismatch, and the explicit slow martingale noise.

To compare the projected recursions, introduce their unconstrained driving sequences. Set

$$
v _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } = \theta _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } , \qquad \tilde { v } _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } = \tilde { \theta } _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } ,
$$

and, for $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m } - 1$ , define

$$
\begin{array} { r } { v _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = v _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \left( f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) + ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right) , } \end{array}\tag{7.2}
$$

and

$$
\tilde { v } _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \tilde { v } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) .\tag{7.3}
$$

The step-path representation and Lipschitz comparison are proved in Lemma S.13.1 of the Online Companion's Skorokhod section. In particular, for every $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m }$ 2

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \leq L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| v _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { v } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| .
$$

Subtracting (7.3) from (7.2) gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { v _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { v } _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = v _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { v } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \left\{ f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\} } \\ & { \qquad + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \left\{ f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\} } \\ & { \qquad + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \left\{ f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\} } \\ & { \qquad + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } . } \end{array}\tag{7.4}
$$

The second term on the right-hand side of (7.4), namely,

$$
f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) .\tag{7.5}
$$

is the Markov noise obtained by subtracting the stationary average under $\mu ^ { ( \boldsymbol { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) }$ . To control it, we use a Poisson-equation decomposition for the controlled Markov chain.

Definition 7.1 (Poisson equation for the slow drift). For $\theta \in \Theta$ , consider the Poisson equation

$$
u ( y ) - \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y , j ) u ( j ) = f ( y , x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) , \theta ) - f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \theta ; \theta ) , \qquad y \in \mathcal { Y } .\tag{7.6}
$$

Let $\left\{ Y _ { n } \right\}$ be the Markov chain with transition matrix $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ . For $y \in \mathcal { V }$ , define

$$
u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) : = \mathbb { E } ^ { ( \theta ) } \left[ \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \tau - 1 } \left( f ( Y _ { n } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) , \theta ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta ; \theta ) \right) \biggm | Y _ { 0 } = y \right] ,\tag{7.7}
$$

where $\tau$ is the hitting time of the reference state $y ^ { \star }$ in Assumption 3.6. The expectation $\mathbb { E } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ is taken under a Markov chain that uses the transition kernel $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ at every step. The parameter $\theta$ in the summand is held fixed as the chain evolves.

The same argument as in Definition 6.1 shows that (7.7) solves (7.6), with $u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ^ { \star } ) = \mathbf { 0 }$

Next we define a martingale-difference sequence.

Lemma 7.1 (Poisson decomposition). Define, for $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m } - 1$ 2

$$
( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } : = u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } ) - \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , y ) u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( y ) .\tag{7.8}
$$

Then $\{ ( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \} _ { 0 \leq n \leq K _ { m - 1 } }$ is a martingale-difference sequence with respect to $\{ \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \} _ { 0 \leq n \leq K _ { m } } ;$ more precisely,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \big ( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } \big ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \ i s \ \mathcal { F } _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } { \cdot } m e a s u r a b l e , \qquad \mathbb { E } \Big [ \big ( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } \big ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \ \big | \ \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \Big ] = \mathbf { 0 } , } \end{array}
$$

for every $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m } - 1$ . Moreover, for every $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m } - 1$ , almost surely,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } \\ & { \qquad = u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } ) + ( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } . } \end{array}\tag{7.9}
$$

Let $C _ { P }$ and $C _ { L }$ denote the constants in Lemma S.11.3, and set

$$
R _ { m } : = 3 C _ { P } \beta _ { n _ { m } } + C _ { L } \{ b ( n _ { m } ) - b ( n _ { m + 1 } ) \} .
$$

To handle the Markov noise (7.5), we use the Poisson decomposition. Its martingale-difference term is controlled later on the slow-martingale good event defined below, and its Poisson boundary term is controlled bv the deterministic residual bound $R _ { m }$

Substituting (7.9) into the Markov noise term in (7.4) gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { v _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { v } _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = v _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { v } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \left\{ f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\} } \\ & { \qquad + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \left\{ u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\} } \\ & { \qquad + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \left\{ f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\} } \\ & { \qquad + \beta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \Big ( ( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } + ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \Big ) . } \end{array}\tag{7.10}
$$

The final term in (7.10) is the combined slow martingale: $( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) }$ is the explicit martingale noise in the slow recursion, while $( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } ) _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) }$ arises from the Poisson decomposition of the slow Markov noise. For each block m and each slow-martingale deviation threshold $\delta _ { s } > 0$ , define

$$
\mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } ) : = \left\{ \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 1 \leq k \leq K _ { m } } \left\| \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \left[ ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { \ell + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } + ( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } ) _ { \ell + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right] \right\| < \delta _ { s } \right\} .\tag{7.11}
$$

Thus, on $\mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } )$ , the cumulative slow-martingale perturbation remains below $\delta _ { s }$ throughout block m. The subscript θ identifies this as the slow-coordinate martingale event, while m is the block index. Define

$$
C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta ) } : = { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 1 } + { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 2 } { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 6 } + 4 { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 5 } { \mathrm { B } } _ { 8 } .
$$

Lemma 7.2. For every $m \geq 0$ and $\delta _ { s } > 0$

$$
\mathbb { P } \left( \left( \mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } ) \right) ^ { c } \right) \le 2 d _ { \theta } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K _ { m } } \exp \left( - \frac { \delta _ { s } ^ { 2 } } { 2 \kappa _ { d _ { \theta } } ^ { 2 } ( C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ) ^ { 2 } \{ b ( n _ { m } ) - b ( n _ { m } + k ) \} } \right) .
$$

Proposition 7.1 (Comparison between $\theta ^ { ( m ) }$ and $\tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } )$ . Fix $m \geq 0$ and $\delta _ { s } > 0$ . On the event $\mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } )$ , for every $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \leq L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \left[ L _ { f } \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { n - 1 } \beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \left\| e _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| + \delta _ { s } + R _ { m } \right] } \\ { \times \exp \left( L _ { f } ( L _ { f p } + 1 ) L _ { \mathrm { S k } } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m } + n ) \right) , } \end{array}\tag{7.12}
$$

Here $R _ { m }$ is the deterministic Poisson-residual bound proved in Lemma S.11.3.

Proof. See Appendix A.4.

Proposition 7.1 leaves the weighted fast-tracking term

$$
F _ { m } ^ { x } : = \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { K _ { m } - 1 } \beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \left\| e _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| = \sum _ { r = n _ { m } } ^ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \beta _ { r } \left\| x _ { r } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { r } ) \right\|
$$

explicit. For a fast-martingale deviation level $\delta > 0$ , define

$$
S _ { m } ( \delta ) : = C _ { x , T } \left( \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \right) ,\tag{7.13}
$$

where $C _ { x , T }$ is the constant in Proposition 6.1. By (6.12), on ${ \mathcal G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta )$

$$
F _ { m } ^ { x } \leq S _ { m } ( \delta ) .\tag{7.14}
$$

For $\delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } > 0$ , set

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \widetilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) : = L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \left[ L _ { f } S _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } ) + \delta _ { s } + R _ { m } \right] } \\ & { \qquad \times \exp \left\{ L _ { f } ( L _ { f p } + 1 ) L _ { \mathrm { S k } } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) \right\} . } \end{array}\tag{7.15}
$$

Corollary 7.1 (Original-auxiliary slow comparison). Fix $m \geq 0$ and $\delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } > 0$ . On

$$
\mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { f } ) \cap \mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } ) ,
$$

we have

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } } \left\| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \leq E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) .
$$

Equivalently,

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq H _ { m } } \Big \| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) \Big \| \leq E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) .
$$

## 7.2 Comparison of the auxiliary recursion with the projected ODE

We next compare the auxiliary recursion with the projected ODE

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \dot { z } ( t ) = \Pi _ { \Theta } \left( z ( t ) , f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( z ( t ) ; z ( t ) ) \right) . } \end{array}
$$

Recall that $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( 0 ) = \theta _ { n _ { m } }$ . The auxiliary recursion uses the off-diagonal evaluation $f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( { \tilde { \theta } } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } )$ whereas the ODE uses the diagonal evaluation $f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( z ; z )$ . For any $s \in [ 0 , H _ { m } ]$ within block $m$ add and subtract $f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . ) } } ( \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ; \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) )$ to write

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) ; z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ; \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ) } \\ & { \quad = \left[ f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) ; z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ; \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ) \right] } \\ & { \quad + \left[ f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ; \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ; \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) ) \right] . } \end{array}
$$

The first bracket is controlled by the difference between the ODE state $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s )$ and the auxiliary state $\tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s )$ The second is the off-diagonal mismatch between the stationary laws indexed by $\tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s )$ and $\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s )$ . To compare the auxiliary path $\tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ }$ with the projected-ODE trajectory $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) }$ , we first bound the difference between their unconstrained driving paths. The Lipschitz property of the Skorokhod map then bounds the difference between these two projected paths. Lemma S.13.2 of the Online Companion's Skorokhod section provides an absolutely continuous driver $w ^ { ( \tau ) }$ such that

$$
w ^ { ( \tau ) } ( 0 ) = z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( 0 ) = \theta ^ { \circ } ( \tau ) , \qquad \dot { w } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( t ) = f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( t ) ; z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( t ) ) ~ \mathrm { a . e . } , \qquad z ^ { ( \tau ) } = \Gamma ( w ^ { ( \tau ) } ) .
$$

We compare $w ^ { ( T _ { m } ) }$ with the step driver $\tilde { v } ^ { ( m ) , \circ }$ , and Lemma S.13.1 bounds the difference between the projected paths $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) }$ and $\tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ }$ in terms of the difference between their unconstrained driving paths.

Let $\boldsymbol { L } _ { f , \mathrm { a v g } }$ .denote the diagonal-field Lipschitz constant defined in Lemma S.11.2(ii).

Proposition 7.2 (Auxiliary-recursion-ODE comparison). Fix $m \geq 0$ and $\delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } > 0$ , and recall from Section 4 that $H _ { m } = t ^ { ( m ) } ( K _ { m } ) = a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } )$ . On

$$
\mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { f } ) \cap \mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } ) ,
$$

deine, for $0 \leq t \leq H _ { m }$ ，

$$
\mathcal { A } _ { m } ( t ) : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq s \leq t } \left\| w ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) - \tilde { v } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) \right\| .
$$

Then

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { A } _ { m } ( t ) \leq \exp \{ L _ { f , \mathrm { a v g . } } L _ { \mathrm { S k } } t \} \left[ \mathsf { B } _ { 8 } L _ { \mu } t E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) + \mathsf { B } _ { 8 } \beta _ { n _ { m } } \right] . } \end{array}\tag{7.16}
$$

Consequently,

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq s \leq t } \| z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) - \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( s ) \| } } \\ & { } & { \leq L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \exp \{ L _ { f , \mathrm { a v g . } } L _ { \mathrm { S k } } t \} [ \mathsf { B } _ { 8 } L _ { \mu } t E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) + \mathsf { B } _ { 8 } \beta _ { n _ { m } } ] . } \end{array}
$$

Proof. See Appendix A.5.

□

## 7.3 Putting the estimates together

For later use, define

$$
p _ { \mathrm { d e t } } : = \mathsf { b } - \mathsf { a } ,
$$

which is the decay exponent of the step-size ratio $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }$ . For $m \geq 0$ , a fast-martingale deviation threshold $\delta _ { f } > 0$ , and a slow-martingale deviation threshold $\delta _ { s } > 0$ , define

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { B _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) : = E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) } \\ & { \phantom { A _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) : = } + L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \exp \{ L _ { f , \mathrm { a v g . } } L _ { \mathrm { S k } } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) \} \left[ { \mathrm { B } _ { 8 } } L _ { \mu } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) \right. } \\ & { \phantom { A _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) : = } + \left. { \mathrm { B } _ { 8 } } \beta _ { n _ { m } } \right] . } \end{array}\tag{7.17}
$$

Theorem 7.1 (Localized blockwise tracking of the projected ODE). The deviation thresholds $\delta _ { f }$ and $\delta _ { s }$ may be chosen arbitrarily for each block m.
