Section 8 later substitutes the rate-specific choices $\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m )$ and $\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( m )$ . Here dev denotes a deviation threshold; x and θ denote the fast and slow martingales, respectively. Fix $m \geq 0$ and $\delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } > 0$ . On

$$
\mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { f } ) \cap \mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } ) ,
$$

we have

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq H _ { m } } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( t ) \right\| \leq B _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) .\tag{7.18}
$$

Moreover, there exists $C _ { B , T } < \infty$ , depending only on $T$ and the problem parameters, such that

$$
B _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) \leq C _ { B , T } \left\{ \delta _ { f } + \delta _ { s } + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \right\} .\tag{7.19}
$$

Consequently,

$$
B _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) \leq C _ { B , T } \left\{ \delta _ { f } + \delta _ { s } + ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - p _ { \mathrm { d e t } } } \right\} .\tag{7.20}
$$

Proof. See Appendix A.6.

□

## 8 Almost-sure convergence and last-iterate rates

We now convert the localized blockwise projected-ODE tracking estimate of Theorem 7.1 into iteration-wise almost-sure rates. The argument has three steps. First, we choose block-dependent fast- and slow-martingale deviation thresholds whose failure probabilities are summable over the blocks. The Borel-Cantelli lemma then implies that the blockwise tracking estimate holds on every sufficiently large block almost surely. Second, a uniform one-block Lyapunov decrease condition yields a perturbed nonlinear recursion at the block endpoints. Third, we analyze this recursion and use the within-block tracking estimate to transfer the endpoint bound to every discrete iterate. Uniform one-block contraction is the special case in which the Lyapunov value decreases by at least a fixed proportion over each block. Throughout this section, the standing assumptions and Condition 3.1 of Section 3, together with (3.3), remain in force.

For $s \geq 0$ , let $\mathsf { S } _ { s }$ denote the time-s flow map of the projected ODE (5.1). The well-posedness of (5.1) is established in Lemma S.15.1. Let $\mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta }$ denote the set of equilibrium points of the ODE (5.1):

$$
\mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } : = \left\{ z \in \Theta : \Pi _ { \Theta } ( z , f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( z ; z ) ) = \mathbf { 0 } \right\} .
$$

Definition 8.1 (Lyapunov function). Consider the projected ODE (5.1), repeated here for convenience:

$$
\dot { z } ( t ) = \Pi _ { \Theta } \left( z ( t ) , f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( z ( t ) ; z ( t ) ) \right) , \qquad z ( 0 ) = z _ { 0 } \in \Theta .
$$

A continuous function $J : \Theta \to \mathbb { R } _ { + }$ is called a Lyapunov function for (5.1) if $J ( z ) = 0$ if and only if $z \in \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta }$ , and if, for every solution z(·) of (5.1),

$$
J ( z ( t ) ) = J ( \mathsf { S } _ { t - s } ( z ( s ) ) ) \leq J ( z ( s ) ) , \qquad 0 \leq s \leq t < \infty .
$$

Thus J is nonincreasing along trajectories of the projected ODE.

For any Lyapunov function J as in Definition 8.1, write

$$
J _ { \mathrm { m a x } } : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { \theta \in \Theta } J ( \theta ) < \infty ,
$$

where finiteness follows from the continuity of J and the compactness of Θ. We use a Lyapunov function to measure convergence to the equilibrium set.

Assumption 8.1. The projected ODE (5.1) admits a Lyapunov function in the sense of Definition 8.1.

Theorem 7.1 bounds, on each slow-time block, the gap between the path of the slow iterates $\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ }$ and the projected-ODE trajectory $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) }$ started at $\theta _ { n _ { m } }$ . We now choose block-dependent thresholds $\delta _ { f }$ and $\delta _ { s }$ that vanish as $m \to \infty$ , while their block-failure probabilities remain summable. Smaller deviation levels sharpen this bound but increase the block-failure probabilities; the logarithmic choices below make both levels vanish while keeping those probabilities summable.

For $m \geq 0$ , set

$$
D _ { m } : = b ( n _ { m } ) - b ( n _ { m + 1 } ) , \qquad L _ { m } ^ { x } : = \log \Bigl ( 4 d _ { x } K _ { m } ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } \Bigr ) , \qquad L _ { m } ^ { \theta } : = \log \Bigl ( 4 d _ { \theta } K _ { m } ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } \Bigr ) .
$$

Define

$$
\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) : = \left( \frac { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } L _ { m } ^ { x } } { c _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { x } } \right) ^ { 1 / 2 } ,
$$

and

$$
\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( m ) : = \kappa _ { d _ { \theta } } C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta ) } \sqrt { 2 D _ { m } L _ { m } ^ { \theta } } .
$$

Define the blockwise rate event

$$
\mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } : = \mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } \big ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \big ) \cap \mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } \big ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( m ) \big ) .
$$

On $\mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ , both martingale perturbations stay below their rate-calibrated levels, allowing the blockwise tracking estimate below to be applied. By Lemmas 6.2 and 7.2, the chosen thresholds satisfy

$$
\mathbb { P } \Big ( [ \mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { \mathsf { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) ) ] ^ { c } \Big ) \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } } , \qquad \mathbb { P } \Big ( [ \mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { \mathsf { d e v } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( m ) ) ] ^ { c } \Big ) \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } } .
$$

By the union bound, $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { m > 1 } \mathbb { P } \big ( ( \mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } ) ^ { c } \big ) < \infty } \end{array}$ , and the Borel-Cantelli lemma implies that $\mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ holds eventually almost surely.

Define

$$
d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } : = \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) + \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( m ) + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } .
$$

By Theorem 7.1, applied with

$$
\delta _ { f } = \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) , \qquad \delta _ { s } = \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( m ) ,
$$

on $\mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq r \leq H _ { m } } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) - z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( r ) \right\| \leq C _ { B , T } d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } .\tag{8.1}
$$

The estimate (8.1) controls the stochastic perturbation accumulated within one block. To propagate this estimate over successive blocks, we impose a Lyapunov decrease condition over time $T$ along the projected-ODE flow, uniformly over initial states $\theta \in \Theta$

Assumption 8.2 (Uniform one-block Lyapunov decrease). Let J be the Lyapunov function in Assumption 8.1. Let $T > 0$ be the block horizon used in the definition of $\{ n _ { m } \}$ . There exists a continuous function

$$
\mathcal { D } _ { J } : [ 0 , J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } ] \to \mathbb { R } _ { + }
$$

such that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal D \boldsymbol J ( 0 ) = 0 , \qquad 0 < \mathcal D \boldsymbol J ( u ) \le u , \quad 0 < u \le J _ { \mathrm { m a x } } , } \end{array}
$$

and

$$
J ( \mathsf { S } _ { T } ( \theta ) ) \le J ( \theta ) - \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( J ( \theta ) ) , \qquad \theta \in \Theta .
$$

Since J is nonincreasing along the projected-ODE flow, for every $s \geq T$

$$
J ( \mathsf { S } _ { s } ( \theta ) ) \le J ( \mathsf { S } _ { T } ( \theta ) ) \le J ( \theta ) - \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( J ( \theta ) ) , \qquad \theta \in \Theta .
$$

A decrease proportional to the current Lyapunov value gives the following contraction condition.

Assumption 8.3 (Uniform one-block Lyapunov contraction). Let J be the Lyapunov function in Assumption 8.1, and let $T > 0$ be the block horizon used in the definition of $\{ n _ { m } \}$ . There exists $\rho _ { T } \in ( 0 , 1 )$ such that

$$
J ( \mathsf { S } _ { T } ( \theta ) ) \leq \rho _ { T } J ( \theta ) , \qquad \theta \in \Theta .
$$

Assumption 8.3 is the special case of Assumption 8.2 obtained by taking

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( u ) : = ( 1 - \rho _ { T } ) u . } \end{array}
$$

Define

$$
m ( n ) : = \operatorname* { m a x } \{ m \geq 0 : n _ { m } \leq n \} ,
$$

so that $n _ { m ( n ) } \leq n < n _ { m ( n ) + 1 }$

Theorem 8.1 (Fast tracking rate). Under Assumptions 3.1, 3.2, 3.3, 3.4, 3.5, 3.6, and 3.7, the step-size exponent requirement in Condition 3.1, and Condition (3.3),

$$
\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathsf { a } , \mathsf { s } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathbf { a } , \mathbf { b } ) } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \Big ) , \qquad r _ { x } ( \mathbf { a } , \mathbf { b } ) : = \operatorname* { m i n } \Big \{ \frac { \mathfrak { a } } { 2 } , \mathfrak { b } - \mathbf { a } \Big \} .
$$

The exponent $r _ { x } ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } )$ furnished by this bound is maximized over $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { a } } < { \mathfrak { b } } \leq 1 \ a t \ { \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ and ${ \mathfrak { a } } = 2 / 3$ , where $r _ { x } ( 2 / 3 , 1 ) = 1 / 3$

Theorem 8.2 (Almost-sure convergence under one-block Lyapunov decrease). Fix a Lyapunov function J as in Assumption 8.1. Suppose that J is $L _ { J } – L i p s c h i t z$ on Θ, and that Assumption 8.2 holds for the block horizon T used to define $\{ n _ { m } \}$ . Define

$$
U _ { m } : = J ( \theta _ { n _ { m } } ) , \qquad e _ { m } ^ { J } : = L _ { J } C _ { B , T } d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } .
$$

Then, almost surely, there exists a finite random index $m _ { 0 } = m _ { 0 } ( \omega )$ such that

$$
U _ { m + 1 } \leq U _ { m } - \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( U _ { m } ) + e _ { m } ^ { J } , \qquad m \geq m _ { 0 } .\tag{8.2}
$$

Whenever $m ( n ) \geq m _ { 0 }$

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq U _ { m ( n ) } + e _ { m ( n ) } ^ { J } .\tag{8.3}
$$

Consequently,

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) \longrightarrow 0 , \qquad \mathrm { d i s t } ( \theta _ { n } , { \mathcal { E } } { \mathcal { Q } } _ { \Theta } ) \longrightarrow 0 \quad a l m o s t s u r e l y .
$$

Proof. See Appendix A.7.

When ${ \mathfrak { b } } \ = \ 1 , \ \beta _ { n } \ = \ ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 }$ , and both the accumulated slow time and the number of completed blocks $m ( n )$ grow as $\log ( N _ { 0 } + n )$ ; for ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ , they grow as $( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { 1 - 6 }$ . Theorem 8.2 gives almost-sure convergence in both cases. Explicit rates require a quantitative lower bound on $\mathcal { D } _ { J }$ , as imposed in the next result

Theorem 8.3 (Rates under a power-law decrease condition). Assume the hypotheses of Theorem 8.2. Suppose, in addition, that there exist constants $p _ { J } > 1 , \kappa _ { J } > 0$ , and $u _ { J } > 0$ such that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( u ) \ge \kappa _ { J } u ^ { p _ { J } } , \qquad 0 \le u \le u _ { J } . } \end{array}\tag{8.4}
$$

If, for some $r > 0$ and $C _ { r } < \infty$ 2

$$
d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \leq C _ { r } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) }
$$

for all sufficiently large m, the following conclusions hold. $I f { \textsf { b } } < 1$ , then

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( \left( N _ { 0 } + n \right) ^ { - \frac { 1 - 6 } { p _ { J } - 1 } } + \left( N _ { 0 } + n \right) ^ { - \frac { r } { p _ { J } } } \left( \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \bigg ) .\tag{8.5}
$$

$I f { \boldsymbol { \mathfrak { b } } } = 1$ , then

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { - \frac { 1 } { p _ { J } - 1 } } \bigg ) .\tag{8.6}
$$

The rate (8.6) for ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ is sharp under the power-decrease hypothesis; see Remark S.7.1 in the Online Companion.

Theorem 8.2 allows a general lower bound on the Lyapunov decrease over one block. In the contraction case, the block recursion can be unrolled explicitly, giving the sharper estimate below.

Theorem 8.4 (Slow-iterate Lyapunov error rates under one-block contraction). Fix a Lyapunov function J as in Assumption 8.1. Suppose that J is Lj-Lipschitz on Θ, and that Assumption 8.3 holds for the block horizon T used to define $\{ n _ { m } \}$ . Then, almost surely, there exists a finite random index $m _ { 0 } = m _ { 0 } ( \omega )$ such that, with $d _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ as defined above, the following inequality holds for every n satisfying $m ( n ) \geq m _ { 0 }$ •.

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq \rho _ { T } ^ { m ( n ) - m _ { 0 } } J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } + L _ { J } C _ { B , T } \sum _ { j = m _ { 0 } } ^ { m ( n ) - 1 } \rho _ { T } ^ { m ( n ) - 1 - j } d _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } + L _ { J } C _ { B , T } d _ { m ( n ) } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } .
$$

Consequently, suppose that, for some $r > 0$ and $C _ { r } < \infty$ 2

$$
d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \leq C _ { r } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } , \qquad m \geq m _ { 0 } .
$$

$I f { \textsf { b } } < 1$ , then

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \bigg ) .
$$

$I f \ { \mathfrak { b } } = 1 ,$ deine

$$
\lambda _ { T } : = - \frac { \log \rho _ { T } } { T } > 0 .\tag{8.7}
$$

Then

$$
\begin{array} { r } { J ( \theta _ { n } ) = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \Big ) , } & { \lambda _ { T } > r , } \\ { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - r } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { 3 / 2 } \Big ) , } & { \lambda _ { T } = r , } \\ { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \lambda _ { T } } \Big ) , } & { 0 < \lambda _ { T } < r . } \end{array} \right. } \end{array}\tag{8.8}
$$

The block estimates in Lemmas S.15.4 and S.15.5 of the Online Companion imply that, for the polynomial step sizes above under Condition 3.1,

$$
d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) }
$$

for all sufficiently large m. Thus the rate-envelope conditions in Theorem 8.3 and in the rate part of Theorem 8.4 hold with exponent $r ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } )$ ; see the proof of Corollary 8.1 in Appendix S.7 of the Online Companion

Corollary 8.1 (Optimized two-time-scale rate under contraction). Assume the hypotheses of $T h e -$ orem $\ 8 . \mathit { 4 } .$ For ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) + \| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \Big ) .
$$

Moreover,

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } < \mathfrak { a } < \mathfrak { b } < 1 } r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) = \frac { 1 } { 3 } .
$$

More precisely, for every

$$
0 < \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } < \frac { 1 } { 1 2 } ,
$$

choose

$$
{ \mathfrak { b } } = 1 - 3 \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } , \qquad { \mathfrak { a } } = { \frac { 2 { \mathfrak { b } } } { 3 } } = { \frac { 2 } { 3 } } - 2 \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } .
$$

Then the step-size conditions hold and

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) + \| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 3 + \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \Big ) .
$$

At the harmonic endpoint, take ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ and ${ \mathfrak { a } } = 2 / 3$ . The slow rate is (8.8) with $r = 1 / 3$ , and Theorem 8.1 gives fast-tracking rate $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 3 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } )$ . If $J ( \theta ) = \| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \|$ for a xed equilibrium $\theta ^ { \star }$ of the projected ODE (5.1), each rate stated for $J ( \theta _ { n } )$ is the corresponding slow-iterate distance rate.

Corollary 8.2 (Optimized polynomial exponent under power Lyapunov decrease). Assume the hypotheses of Theorem 8.2, together with (8.4) for some $p _ { J } > 1$ . For the case ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ , choose

$$
{ \mathfrak { a } } = { \frac { 2 p _ { J } } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } , \qquad { \mathfrak { b } } = { \frac { 3 p _ { J } } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } .
$$

Then $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { a } } < { \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ , and

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \frac { 1 } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } \big ( \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \big ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \bigg ) ,
$$

and

$$
\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \frac { p _ { J } } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \bigg ) .
$$

If $J ( \theta ) = \lVert \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \rVert$ for a xed equilibrium $\theta ^ { \star }$ of the projected ODE (5.1), the first display is also the corresponding slow-iterate distance bound.

At the harmonic endpoint, take ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ and ${ \mathfrak { a } } = 2 / 3$ . The slow rate is (8.6), and Theorem 8.1 gives fast-tracking rate $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 3 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } )$ 1

## 8.1 Faster rates at an attracting equilibrium face

The general fast-tracking bound accounts for movement of $x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } )$ as the slow iterate evolves. This contribution can be reduced when the slow equilibrium set is a strictly attracting face of a box and $x ^ { \star }$ has the same value at every point of that face. The resulting rates are given next.

Proposition 8.1 (Rates near an equilibrium face). For (1.1)–(1.2), retain the standing assumptions of Section 3 on the chain, update maps, noise, and fast-iterate stability, and the assumptions on the Lipschitz Lyapunov function J in Theorem 8.2. Write $t _ { n } = N _ { 0 } + n$ . In place of Condition 3.1 and (3.3), allow either

$$
\begin{array} { l l l l } { \alpha _ { n } = t _ { n } ^ { - \mathfrak { a } } , } & { \beta _ { n } = t _ { n } ^ { - \mathfrak { b } } , } & { \frac { 1 } { 2 } < \mathfrak { a } < \mathfrak { b } \le 1 , } & { t _ { 1 } ^ { 1 - \mathfrak { a } } \ge 4 ; } \\ { \alpha _ { n } = A \log ( t _ { n } ) / t _ { n } , } & { \beta _ { n } = B / t _ { n } , } & { A , B > 0 , } & { N _ { 0 } \ge 3 , } & { \alpha _ { 1 } \le 1 , } & { A \log t _ { 1 } \ge \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 4 , B \} . } \end{array}\tag{8.9}
$$

Suppose that $\begin{array} { r } { \Theta = \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { d _ { \theta } } [ \ell _ { i } , u _ { i } ] } \end{array}$ , with $\ell _ { i } < u _ { i } ,$ and that, for some $I \subseteq \{ 1 , \ldots , d _ { \theta } \}$ , its projected-ODE equilibrium set is

$$
\mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } = \{ \theta \in \Theta : \theta _ { i } = v _ { i } \ ( i \in I ) \} , \qquad v _ { i } \in \{ \ell _ { i } , u _ { i } \} .
$$

For $i \in I$ ,set $s _ { i } = - 1$ at a lower endpoint and $s _ { i } = 1$ at an upper endpoint. Assume

$$
\operatorname* { i n f } _ { \theta \in \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } } s _ { i } f _ { i } ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta ; \theta ) > 0 , \qquad i \in I .\tag{8.10}
$$

Then

$$
\mathrm { d i s t } ( \theta _ { n } , { \mathcal { E } } { \mathcal { Q } } _ { \Theta } ) + J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \beta _ { n } \log t _ { n } ) .\tag{8.11}
$$

$H ,$ additionally, $x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) = x ^ { \dagger }$ for every $\theta \in \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta }$ , for some fxed $x ^ { \dagger } .$ , then

$$
\left\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \dagger } \right\| + \left\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Bigl ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log t _ { n } } \Bigr ) .\tag{8.12}
$$

Thus ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1 , { \mathfrak { a } } = 1 - 2 \varepsilon$ , with $0 < \varepsilon < 1 / 4$ , gives a joint rate $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 / 2 + \varepsilon } \sqrt { \log n } )$ . The second schedule in (8.9) gives $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 / 2 } \log n )$

Proof. See Appendix S.7.6.

## 8.2 Improved rates under contraction of the reduced slow map

A different mechanism yields the $n ^ { - 1 / 2 }$ polynomial exponent: assume that the reduced slow update map is a Euclidean contraction. Together with logarithmic separation of the two step sizes, this controls the slow error directly, even when its equilibrium lies on the boundary. Unlike Proposition 8.1, the next result permits an arbitrary compact convex polyhedron and does not require an attracting box face or a common fast target along a face. Its contraction assumption is stronger than the one-block Lyapunov condition.

Proposition 8.2 (Contraction rate with boundary equilibria). For (1.1)−(1.2), retain the standing assumptions of Section 3 on the chain, update maps, noise, fast contraction, and fast-iterate stability. Suppose that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \overline { G } ( \theta ) : = \theta + f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta ; \theta ) , \qquad \left\| \overline { G } ( \theta ) - \overline { G } ( \vartheta ) \right\| \leq \rho _ { s } \| \theta - \vartheta \| , \quad \theta , \vartheta \in \Theta , } \end{array}\tag{8.13}
$$

for some $\rho _ { s } \in [ 0 , 1 )$ , in the Euclidean slow-coordinate norm. Let $\theta ^ { \star }$ be the unique ixed point of proje G. In place of Condition 3.1 and (3.3), take

$$
t _ { n } = N _ { 0 } + n , \qquad \alpha _ { n } = \frac { A \log t _ { n } } { t _ { n } } , \qquad \beta _ { n } = \frac { B } { t _ { n } } , \qquad A > 0 , \quad B \geq \frac { 2 } { 1 - \rho _ { s } } ,\tag{8.14}
$$

where $N _ { 0 } \geq 3 , \alpha _ { 1 } < 1$ , and Alog $t _ { 1 } \geq \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 4 , B \}$ . Assumption 3.7 is required for this selected schedule. Then

$$
\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| + \| \theta _ { n } - \theta ^ { \star } \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \frac { \log t _ { n } } { \sqrt { t _ { n } } } \right) .\tag{8.15}
$$

The same bound holds for $\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) \|$ and for $J ( \theta _ { n } )$ whenever J is Lipschitz and $J ( \theta ^ { \star } ) = 0$

Proof. See Appendix S.17.

The equilibrium drift may be nonzero and outward; neither interiority nor strict complementarity is assumed. Condition (8.13) implies contraction of distance along the projected ODE and is stronger than the one-block Lyapunov contraction used in Theorem 8.4.

In the terminology of Chandak [6], “true" two-time-scale stochastic approximation requires $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }  0$ , not specifically polynomial separation. The schedule (8.14) satisfies this condition since $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n } = B / ( A \log t _ { n } )$ Under the additional contraction condition (8.13), the bound (8.15) has polynomial exponent $1 / 2$ for both iterate errors, with only logarithmic separation of the step sizes. This improvement uses the stronger contraction assumption together with the revised schedule and proof; it does not follow from changing the schedule alone. The proof adapts the auxiliary noise-averaging idea of that work to the projected recursion and controlled Markov noise, using a feasible auxiliary sequence to handle boundary equilibria

One setting in which the reduced-map condition can be checked directly is projected gradient descent for a smooth strongly convex objective. If $\overline { { G } } ( \theta ) = \theta - \eta \nabla F ( \theta )$ and m. $I \preceq \nabla ^ { 2 } F ( \theta ) \preceq L I$ on Θ, then, for $0 < \eta < 2 / L$ , the mean-value theorem gives

$$
\left\| \overline { G } ( \theta ) - \overline { G } ( \vartheta ) \right\| \leq \operatorname* { m a x } \{ | 1 - \eta m | , \ | 1 - \eta L | \} \left\| \theta - \vartheta \right\| .
$$

The displayed factor is less than one. Projection onto a compact convex polyhedron preserves this contraction bound, including when the constrained minimizer lies on a boundary face.

## 9 Actor-Critic Application

Actor-critic provides an example in which the slow iterate controls both the sampling law and the projected update. We first specify the single-trajectory algorithm and its constrained policy class, then state the rate obtained from the attracting-face result. The verification of the assumptions is in the Online Companion; standalone projected TD(0) and projected-SGD applications appear in Appendices S.1 and S.2 there.

Consider a finite-state, finite-action Markov decision process (MDP); see, for example, Puterman [21]. Let S and A be its state and action spaces, $P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid s , a )$ its transition probabilities, and $c ( s , a )$ its expected one-step cost. At time $n ,$ the current actor parameter determines a policy from which $A _ { n }$ is drawn at state $S _ { n }$ The environment then produces $S _ { n + 1 }$ and a realized one-step cost $C _ { n + 1 }$ Let ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } }$ be the history immediately before $A _ { n }$ is drawn. For every $s ^ { \prime } \in \mathcal { S }$

$$
\mathbb { P } ( S _ { n + 1 } = s ^ { \prime } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } , A _ { n } ) = P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid S _ { n } , A _ { n } ) , \qquad \mathbb { E } [ C _ { n + 1 } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } , A _ { n } ] = c ( S _ { n } , A _ { n } ) .
$$

The Online Companion, Appendix S.8, expresses the critic and actor updates in the drift-plusmartingale form of $( 1 . 1 ) \ – ( 1 . 2 )$ and checks the assumptions used in the rate results.

Fix $0 < \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } < 1$ . For a stationary policy $\pi ,$ its discounted total-cost value is

$$
V _ { \pi } ( s ) : = \mathbb { E } _ { s } ^ { \pi } \left[ \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } ^ { k } C _ { k + 1 } \right] , \qquad s \in \mathcal { S } .
$$

We analyze an online actor-critic recursion using one observed state-action transition per iteration, motivated by Konda and Borkar [18, Algorithm 3]. In the original asynchronous form, an update associated with a state-action pair uses a step size indexed by that pair's visit count. Here the critic coordinate for the observed state and the actor coordinate for the observed state-action pair are updated using the global step sizes $\alpha _ { n }$ and $\beta _ { n } .$ , indexed by the number n of environment transitions. The rate below applies to this global-clock recursion.

Assume $| C _ { n + 1 } | \leq C _ { \mathrm { m a x } }$ . The actor parameter $\vartheta = ( \vartheta ( s , a ) ) _ { s , a }$ consists of state-action logits, also called preference parameters. Fix $B _ { \mathrm { a c } } > 0$ and set

$$
\Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } } : = [ - B _ { \mathrm { a c } } , B _ { \mathrm { a c } } ] ^ { | { \cal S } | | { \cal A } | } , \qquad \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) : = \frac { e ^ { \vartheta ( s , a ) } } { \sum _ { b \in \mathcal { A } } e ^ { \vartheta ( s , b ) } } ,
$$

and draw $A _ { n } \sim \pi _ { \vartheta _ { n } } ( \cdot \mid S _ { n } )$ before the time-n update. For $s \in { \mathcal { S } }$ and $a \in { \mathcal { A } }$ let $\boldsymbol { e } _ { s } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { | \boldsymbol { S } | }$ and $e _ { s , a } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { | S | | \mathcal { A } | }$ denote the corresponding standard coordinate vectors. With

$$
\delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } : = C _ { n + 1 } + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } V _ { n } ( S _ { n + 1 } ) - V _ { n } ( S _ { n } ) ,
$$

the recursion is

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { V _ { n + 1 } = V _ { n } + \alpha _ { n } e _ { S _ { n } } \delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } , } \\ & { \vartheta _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } } } \left( \vartheta _ { n } - \beta _ { n } e _ { S _ { n } , A _ { n } } \delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } \right) . } \end{array}\tag{9.1}
$$

For $\vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ , let $d _ { \vartheta }$ and $V _ { \vartheta }$ be the stationary state distribution and discounted cost value under πθ. Assume that the controlled state process induced by the policy family $\{ \pi _ { \vartheta } : \vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } } \}$ satisfies Assumptions 3.1, 3.2, and 3.6 uniformly over $\vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ . Assume also that the directed graph on $\boldsymbol { s }$ with an edge $s  s ^ { \prime }$ whenever $P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid s , a ) > 0$ for some $a \in A ,$ is strongly connected. As shown in Appendix S.8 of the Online Companion, the full support of the bounded-logit softmax policies and compactness of $\Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ then imply

$$
d _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ^ { \operatorname { a c } } : = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { \vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } } } \operatorname* { m i n } _ { s \in \mathcal { S } } d _ { \vartheta } ( s ) > 0 .\tag{9.2}
$$

We refer to (9.2) as uniform stationary state coverage. Set

$$
\pi _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } : = \frac { e ^ { - 2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } } { | \boldsymbol { A } | } , \qquad q _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } : = d _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } \pi _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } .
$$

Initialize $\vartheta _ { 1 } \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ and choose $\left\| V _ { 1 } \right\| _ { \infty } \le C _ { \mathrm { m a x } } / ( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } )$ . The critic recursion preserves this cube, which also contains every discounted value vector $V _ { \vartheta }$ . On that bounded set, the finite MDP and the softmax policy on the compact logit box give the localized Lipschitz bounds required for the sampled updates. In the fast-coordinate norm $\| \cdot \| _ { \infty } ,$ the averaged critic map is contractive on $\mathbb { R } ^ { | \boldsymbol { S } | }$ cube invariance supplies fast-iterate stability. The Online Companion verifies these claims.

Let $V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star }$ be the optimal value among policies representable, state by state, by logits in $[ - B _ { \mathrm { a c } } , B _ { \mathrm { a c } } ] ^ { | \mathcal { A } | }$ We measure the actor's aggregate value gap by

$$
J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta ) : = \sum _ { s \in \mathcal { S } } \{ V _ { \vartheta } ( s ) - V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star } ( s ) \} .
$$

For a fixed actor parameter $\vartheta ,$ the stationary frequency of the state-action pair $( s , a )$ is $q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a ) : =$ $d _ { \vartheta } ( s ) \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s )$ . These frequencies weight the coordinates of the averaged actor drift. Appendix S.8 of the Online Companion uses their uniform positive lower bound to establish Lyapunov decrease, identify the restricted-optimal equilibrium face, and show that the projected actor ODE reaches that face in uniformly bounded time. On the face, the critic equilibrium is $V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star }$ , and each coordinate fixed at an endpoint has strictly outward drift. Hence Proposition 8.1 applies.

Corollary 9.1 (Actor-critic rate). Suppose that the preceding MDP, policy, and initialization conditions hold, and choose either step-size schedule in Proposition 8.1. Then

$$
J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \beta _ { n } \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ) ,\tag{9.3}
$$

and

$$
\| V _ { n } - V _ { \vartheta _ { n } } \| _ { \infty } + \left\| V _ { n } - V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star } \right\| _ { \infty } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \biggr ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \biggr ) .\tag{9.4}
$$

For $\alpha _ { n } = A \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) / ( N _ { 0 } + n )$ and $\beta _ { n } = B / ( N _ { 0 } + n )$ , with the shift specified in that proposition, the actor value gap is $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 } \log n )$ , and

$$
J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta _ { n } ) + \left\| V _ { n } - V _ { \vartheta _ { n } } \right\| _ { \infty } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \mathopen { } \mathclose \bgroup \left( \frac { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } { \sqrt { N _ { 0 } + n } } \aftergroup \egroup \right) .\tag{9.5}
$$

This controls the aggregate value Lyapunov gap and the critic tracking error. If $V ^ { \star }$ is the unrestricted optimal value, then

$$
0 \leq \sum _ { s \in \mathcal { S } } \{ V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star } ( s ) - V ^ { \star } ( s ) \} \leq \frac { 2 | \mathcal { S } | ( | \mathcal { A } | - 1 ) C _ { \mathrm { m a x } } e ^ { - 2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } } { ( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } ) ^ { 2 } } .
$$

Consequently, for any prescribed target tolerance $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { p o l } } > 0$ , a fixed $B _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ can be chosen sufficiently large that the displayed restriction bias is at most $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { p o l } }$ . The unrestricted aggregate value gap is then at most $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { p o l } }$ plus the vanishing term in (9.3).

## 10 Conclusion

We obtained finite-time concentration bounds for projected two-time-scale stochastic approximation driven by a controlled Markov chain. On each slow-time block, the bounds compare the fast iterate with its moving equilibrium and the projected slow path with its ODE trajectory. The Skorokhod-map comparison handles boundary discontinuities, while the estimates identify separately the martingale, Markovian, and time-scale contributions.

A one-block Lyapunov decrease gives almost-sure convergence to the slow equilibrium set; power decrease and contraction give explicit last-iterate rates. For polynomial step sizes, the optimized joint exponent under one-block contraction approaches $1 / 3$ . With a strictly attracting equilibrium face and a common fast target, logarithmically separated step sizes give the joint $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 / 2 } \log n )$ rate. Euclidean contraction of the reduced slow map gives the same joint order on a general compact convex polyhedron, including boundary equilibria. In actor-critic, the attracting-face result further gives an $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( n ^ { - 1 } \log n )$ constrained-policy value gap. The Online Companion develops the actorcritic proof, projected TD(0), projected SGD, and fast-coordinate projection.

## A Selected Proofs of the Main Results

We give the path comparisons underlying the main concentration bounds and the argument that carries Lyapunov control from block endpoints to every iteration. These comprise the two comparisons for the fast recursion, their fast-tracking consequence, the original-auxiliary and auxiliary-ODE comparisons for the slow recursion, and the localized projected-ODE comparison. The step-path Skorokhod representation, supporting Poisson-equation and martingale estimates, and the remaining proofs are collected in the Online Companion.

## A.1 Proof of Proposition 6.1

Proof. The triangle inequality and Lemmas 6.3 and 6.4 give, on ${ \mathcal G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta )$ and for $0 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ 2

$$
\varepsilon _ { n _ { m } + k } ^ { ( x ) } \le \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) \varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { \mathrm { a u x } } \big ( \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } \big ) + \frac { C _ { \mathrm { m o v } } } { q } \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } .
$$

This proves (6.11), for instance with

$$
C _ { x } : = \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ C _ { \mathrm { a u x } } , { \frac { C _ { \mathrm { m o v } } } { q } } \right\} .
$$

Assumption 3.7 and the definition of $\mathfrak { c } _ { 8 }$ give $\varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \mathfrak { c } _ { 6 } + \mathfrak { c } _ { 8 }$ . Moreover, monotonicity of $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }$ and $\mathscr { W } _ { m } ( k + 1 ) = \mathscr { W } _ { m } ( k ) ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + k } )$ yield

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { K _ { m } - 1 } \beta _ { n _ { m } + k } \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) \leq \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { q \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \big [ 1 - \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( K _ { m } ) \big ] \leq \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { q \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } .
$$

Since $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { r = n _ { m } } ^ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \beta _ { r } \leq T + 1 } \end{array}$ , summing the pointwise bound proves (6.12) with $C _ { x , T } = ( { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 6 } + { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 8 } ) / q +$ $( T + 1 ) C _ { x }$ .Equation (6.13) is (6.11) at $k = K _ { m }$ □

## A.2 Proof of Lemma 6.3

We first control the discrepancy between the original block-reindexed fast iterate $x _ { k } ^ { ( m ) }$ and the block-restarted averaged recursion $\widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) }$ . Both recursions use the same slow-parameter path and are unprojected, so their comparison is controlled directly by contraction in the fast state; no parameter-path or Skorokhod-map comparison is required. Subtracting their updates and using the Poisson decomposition separates this discrepancy into a martingale transform, a Poisson residual, and a contractive propagation term. The first term is controlled on ${ \mathcal G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta )$ , while the second is bounded deterministically.

Proof. Fix $m \geq 0$ , write $s : = n _ { m }$ , and recall $q = 1 - \gamma$ . Let $\mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { x }$ and $C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x }$ be as in (S.5.2) and Proposition S.5.1, respectively, and set

$$
c : = \delta + C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } \alpha _ { s } .
$$

Subtracting the averaged recursion (6.1) from the original update (1.1), applying the Poisson decomposition (6.5), and iterating from $D _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } = 0$ gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { D _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } = \mathcal { M } _ { m , k } ^ { x } + \mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { x } } } \\ & { } & { + \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { s + j } \chi ( s + j + 1 , s + k - 1 ) \big [ h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( x _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ) - h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \widetilde { x } _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ) \big ] . } \end{array}
$$

Define

$$
H _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { s + j } \chi ( s + j + 1 , s + k - 1 ) \left\| D _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| , \qquad H _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } : = 0 .
$$

The polynomial step sizes satisfy $0 \textless \alpha _ { n } \textless 1$ , so the weights $\alpha _ { s + j } \chi ( s + j + 1 , s + k - 1 )$ are nonnegative. Hence the triangle inequality and Assumption 3.4, which makes the averaged fast map γ-contractive in its state argument, give

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \| \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { s + j } \chi ( s + j + 1 , s + k - 1 ) \big [ h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( x _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ) - h ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { x } _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } ) \big ] \| } } \\ & { } & { \leq \gamma \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { s + j } \chi ( s + j + 1 , s + k - 1 ) \| D _ { j } ^ { ( m ) } \| = \gamma H _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } . } \end{array}
$$

On ${ \mathcal G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta )$ , the definition of $\kappa _ { d _ { x } }$ and (6.7) give $\left\| \mathcal { M } _ { m , k } ^ { x } \right\| \le \kappa _ { d _ { x } } \left\| \mathcal { M } _ { m , k } ^ { x } \right\| _ { \infty } < \delta$ , while Proposition S.5.1 gives $\left\| \mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { x } \right\| \leq C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } \alpha _ { n _ { m } } = C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } \alpha _ { s }$ . Combining these estimates and weakening the strict martingale bound to a non-strict one gives

$$
\left\| D _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \le c + \gamma H _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } .\tag{A.1}
$$

Moreover,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { H _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = ( 1 - \alpha _ { s + k } ) H _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } + \alpha _ { s + k } \left. D _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right. } \\ & { \qquad \leq ( 1 - q \alpha _ { s + k } ) H _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } + \alpha _ { s + k } c . } \end{array}
$$

Iteration and telescoping give

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle { \cal H } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \leq c \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { s + j } \displaystyle \prod _ { \ell = j + 1 } ^ { k - 1 } ( 1 - q \alpha _ { s + \ell } ) } } \\ { { \displaystyle ~ = \frac { c } { q } \left[ 1 - \prod _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } ( 1 - q \alpha _ { s + \ell } ) \right] \leq \frac { c } { q } } . } \end{array}
$$

Substitution into (A.1), together with $1 + \gamma / q = 1 / q$ , yields

$$
\left\| D _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \leq \frac { c } { q } = \frac { \delta + C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } { q } \leq C _ { \mathrm { a u x } } \big ( \delta + \alpha _ { n _ { m } } \big ) ,
$$

where

$$
C _ { \mathrm { a u x } } : = \frac { 1 } { q } \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 1 , C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } \} .
$$

This proves (6.9).

## A.3 Proof of Lemma 6.4

Proof. Set $C _ { \mathrm { m o v } } : = L _ { f p } ( \mathfrak { c } _ { 1 } + \mathfrak { c } _ { 2 } \mathfrak { c } _ { 6 } + \mathsf { B } _ { 6 } )$ , and define

$$
d _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = \left. \widetilde { x } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right. .
$$

The fixed-point identity $h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) ; \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) = x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } )$ , Assumption 3.4, and (6.1) give

$$
d _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \leq ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + k } ) d _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } + \left\| x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } ) - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\| .
$$

By Lemma 3.1, nonexpansiveness of the projection in (1.2), and Assumptions 3.3 and 3.7,

$$
\left\| x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } ) - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\| \leq C _ { \mathrm { m o v } } \beta _ { n _ { m } + k } .
$$

Iteration of the scalar recursion yields

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle d _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \leq \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) d _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } } } \\ { { \displaystyle \quad + C _ { \operatorname* { m o v } } \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \beta _ { n _ { m } + j } \prod _ { \ell = j + 1 } ^ { k - 1 } ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) . } } \end{array}
$$

Since $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }$ is nonincreasing,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \beta _ { n _ { m } + j } \prod _ { \ell = j + 1 } ^ { k - 1 } ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) } } \\ & { \le \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } k - 1 } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { n _ { m } + j } \prod _ { \ell = j + 1 } ^ { k - 1 } ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) } \\ & { = \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { q \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } [ 1 - \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) ] } \\ & { \le \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { q \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } . } \end{array}
$$

Indeed,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle q \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { n _ { m } + j } \prod _ { \ell = j + 1 } ^ { k - 1 } \left( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + \ell } \right) } \\ & { \quad = \displaystyle \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \left[ \prod _ { \ell = j + 1 } ^ { k - 1 } \left( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + \ell } \right) - \prod _ { \ell = j } ^ { k - 1 } ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) \right] } \\ & { \quad = \displaystyle 1 - \prod _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) = 1 - \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( k ) . } \end{array}
$$

In the first step, we have used

$$
\beta _ { n _ { m } + j } \leq \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \alpha _ { n _ { m } + j } .
$$

Finally,

$$
d _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } = \left. x _ { n _ { m } } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n _ { m } } ) \right. ,
$$

which proves (6.10).

## A.4 Proof of Proposition 7.1

Proof. For $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m }$ let

$$
A _ { n } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| v _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { v } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| , \quad \quad L _ { 0 } : = L _ { f } ( L _ { f p } + 1 ) L _ { \mathrm { S k } } .
$$

Lemma S.11.2 and the Skorokhod comparison in Lemma S.13.1 give

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\| \le L _ { 0 } A _ { \ell } . } \end{array}
$$

Moreover, since $e _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } = x _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } )$ , Assumption 3.5 gives

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| f ( Y _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } , x _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } , \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ( Y _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ) \right\| \leq L _ { f } \left\| e _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| . } \end{array}
$$

Iterating (7.10), maximizing over terminal indices, and using $\mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } )$ and Lemma S.11.3 therefore gives

$$
A _ { n } \leq L _ { 0 } \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { n - 1 } \beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } A _ { \ell } + B _ { n } , \qquad B _ { n } : = L _ { f } \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { n - 1 } \beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \left\| e _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| + \delta _ { s } + R _ { m } .
$$

Because $B _ { n }$ is nondecreasing, discrete Gronwall yields

$$
A _ { n } \leq B _ { n } \exp \{ L _ { 0 } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m } + n ) \} .
$$

A final application of Lemma S.13.1 gives $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \leq L _ { \mathrm { S k } } A _ { n } } \end{array}$ , which is (7.12). □

## A.5 Proof of Proposition 7.2

Proof. Write $F ( \vartheta ) : = f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \vartheta ; \vartheta )$ and $C _ { \star } : = \mathsf { B } _ { 8 }$ . On $[ t ^ { ( m ) } ( \ell ) , t ^ { ( m ) } ( \ell { + } 1 ) )$ , let $G _ { m } ( s ) : = f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \tilde { \theta } _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } )$ ， and set $G _ { m } ( H _ { m } ) : = \mathbf { 0 }$ . The ODE driver satisfies

$$
w ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( t ) = \theta _ { n _ { m } } + \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } F ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) ) d s .
$$

The auxiliary step-path driver accumulates only completed grid increments. Consequently, its difference from $\begin{array} { r }  \theta _ { n _ { m } } + \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } G _ { m } ( s ) \ d s \qquad \end{array}$ ds has norm at most $C _ { \star } \beta _ { n _ { m } }$ : the difference is the contribution of the single grid interval containing t. Therefore

$$
\left\| w ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( t ) - \tilde { v } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) \right\| \leq \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \left\| F ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) ) - G _ { m } ( s ) \right\| d s + C _ { \star } \beta _ { n _ { m } } .
$$

For almost every $s \in [ 0 , H _ { m } ]$ , Lemma S.11.2, the identities $z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } = \Gamma ( w ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } )$ and $\tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } = \Gamma ( \tilde { v } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } )$ from Lemmas S.13.2 and S.13.1, and Corollary 7.1 give

$$
\Big \| F ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( s ) ) - G _ { m } ( s ) \Big \| \leq L _ { f , \mathrm { a v g . } } L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \mathcal { A } _ { m } ( s ) + C _ { \star } L _ { \mu }  { E _ { m } } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) .
$$

Taking the supremum up to t therefore yields

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { A } _ { m } ( t ) \le L _ { f , \mathrm { a v g . } } L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \displaystyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \mathcal { A } _ { m } ( s ) d s + C _ { \star } L _ { \mu } t E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) + C _ { \star } \beta _ { n _ { m } } . } \end{array}
$$

Gronwall proves (7.16). Applying the same finite-horizon Skorokhod estimate to these drivers proves the final assertion. □

## A.6 Proof of Theorem 7.1

Proof. By the triangle inequality,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq H _ { m } } \Big \| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( t ) \Big \| } } \\ & { \leq \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq H _ { m } } \| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) \| + \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq H _ { m } } \| \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( t ) \| . } \end{array}
$$

Corollary 7.1 bounds the first term by $E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , { \tilde { \theta } } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } )$ . Proposition 7.2, applied with $t = H _ { m } =$ $a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } )$ , bounds the second term by

$$
L _ { \mathrm { S k } } e ^ { L _ { f , \mathrm { a v g . } } L _ { \mathrm { S k } } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) } \left[ { \mathsf { B } } _ { 8 } L _ { \mu } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) + { \mathsf { B } } _ { 8 } \beta _ { n _ { m } } \right] .
$$

This proves (7.18) with $B _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } )$ defined in (7.17).

For the rate bounds, let $C _ { B , T }$ increase from line to line. Since $a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) \leq T + 1$ , the definitions of $B _ { m }$ and $E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , { \tilde { \theta } } ) }$ imply

$$
B _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } ) \leq C _ { B , T } \{ S _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } ) + \delta _ { s } + R _ { m } + \beta _ { n _ { m } } \} .
$$

Using $R _ { m } \leq C _ { B , T } \beta _ { n _ { m } }$ , (7.13), and $\beta _ { n _ { m } } \leq \alpha _ { n _ { m } }$ proves (7.19). Finally,

$$
\alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \leq 2 ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - p _ { \mathrm { d e t } } } ,
$$

which gives (7.20).

## A.7 Proof of Theorem 8.2

Proof. By the Borel-Cantelli argument in Section 8, almost surely there exists $m _ { 0 } ~ < ~ \infty$ such that $\mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ holds for every $m \geq m _ { 0 }$ . Fix such a sample path and write $U _ { m } : = J ( \theta _ { n _ { m } } )$ and $e _ { m } ^ { J } : =$ $L _ { J } C _ { B , T } d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ . At the block endpoint, (8.1) and the Lipschitz continuity of $J ,$ followed by nonincrease of J along the ODE flow, $H _ { m } \geq T$ , and Assumption 8.2, give

$$
U _ { m + 1 } \leq J \big ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( H _ { m } ) \big ) + e _ { m } ^ { J } \leq U _ { m } - { \mathcal { D } } _ { J } ( U _ { m } ) + e _ { m } ^ { J } .
$$

This proves (8.2).

For $n _ { m } \leq n < n _ { m + 1 }$ , let $r _ { n } : = t ( n ) - T _ { m } \in [ 0 , H _ { m } ]$ . Then $\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r _ { n } ) = \theta _ { n }$ , and the within-block tracking estimate gives

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq J \bigl ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( r _ { n } ) \bigr ) + e _ { m } ^ { J } \leq U _ { m } + e _ { m } ^ { J } .
$$

This proves (8.3).

It remains to prove convergence. If $J _ { \mathrm { m a x } } = 0$ , the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise, fix $0 < \eta \leq 2 J _ { \mathrm { m a x } }$ and set $c _ { \eta } : = \mathrm { m i n } _ { u \in [ \eta / 2 , J _ { \mathrm { m a x } } ] } \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( u ) > 0$ . The definition of $d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ and Lemmas S.15.4 and $\mathrm { { S . 1 5 . 5 } }$ , in their respective cases, give $d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \to 0$ , and hence $e _ { m } ^ { J } \to 0$ . Thus, for all sufficiently large $m .$

$$
e _ { m } ^ { J } \leq \operatorname* { m i n } \{ c _ { \eta } / 2 , \eta / 2 \} , \qquad U _ { m } \geq \eta / 2 \implies U _ { m + 1 } \leq U _ { m } - c _ { \eta } / 2 .
$$

The sequence must therefore eventually enter $[ 0 , \eta / 2 )$ . Once it does, the two preceding inequalities show that it remains in $[ 0 , \eta ]$ below $\eta / 2 .$ its next increase is at most $e _ { m } ^ { J } ,$ while on $[ \eta / 2 , \eta ]$ it decreases by at least $c _ { \eta } / 2$ Since $\eta > 0$ is arbitrary, $U _ { m } \to 0$ . The within-block estimate and $e _ { m } ^ { J } \to 0$ give $J ( \theta _ { n } ) \to 0$ . Finally, compactness of Θ, continuity of $^ { J , }$ and $J ^ { - 1 } ( \{ 0 \} ) = { \mathcal { E } } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta }$ imply dist $( \theta _ { n } , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } )  0$ □

Guide to the Supplementary Appendix. Appendix S.1 gives a standalone projected onpolicy linear TD(0) application and its last-iterate rate. Appendix S.2 gives the projected-SGD application, including distance and objective-gap rates, and Appendix S.3 states the general onetime-scale specializations under contraction and power Lyapunov decrease.

Appendix S.4 establishes regularity of the stationary law and fast equilibrium. Appendices S.5- S.7 contain, respectively, the remaining fast-scale proofs and supporting results; the remaining slow-scale martingale, Poisson, and projected-path arguments; and the remaining convergence and rate proofs, including those for the one-time-scale specializations. Appendix S.7 also proves Proposition 8.1 and records the harmonic-endpoint sharpness example. The seven selected proofs of the main results appear in Sections A.1- A.7 of the main-paper appendix. Appendix S.5 also records, in Remark S.5.1, the extension of the unprojected fast-scale analysis to an arbitrary fixed fast-coordinate norm. Appendix S.8 verifies the actor-critic application, including controlled-chain coverage, critic contraction, Lyapunov geometry, restriction bias, and improved actor and critic rates. Appendix S.9 gives the optional extension with every-step compact-polyhedral Euclidean projection of the fast iterate, including high-probability tracking and almost-sure rates. Appendices S.10–S.12 collect, in order, the auxiliary fast-scale Poisson estimates, the auxiliary slow-scale Poisson estimates, and the strong-monotonicity consequences for projected ODEs. Appendix S.13 contains the Skorokhod-map tools used for projection. Appendix S.14 derives auxiliary results related to step-sizes. Appendix S.15 contains several miscellaneous results, while Appendix S.16 discusses the shifted-step-size and finite-burn-in alternatives.

For $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { b } } \leq 1$ and $\lambda > 0$ , define the rate envelope used in the one-time-scale contraction results below (including the projected TD(0) and projected-SGD applications) by

$$
\mathfrak { R } _ { n } ( \mathfrak { b } , \lambda ) : = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \mathfrak { b } / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } , } & { \frac { 1 } { 2 } < \mathfrak { b } < 1 , } \\ { ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } , } & { \mathfrak { b } = 1 , \ \lambda > \frac { 1 } { 2 } , } \\ { ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 2 } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { 3 / 2 } , } & { \mathfrak { b } = 1 , \ \lambda = \frac { 1 } { 2 } , } \\ { ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \lambda } , } & { \mathfrak { b } = 1 , \ 0 < \lambda < \frac { 1 } { 2 } . } \end{array} \right.
$$

## S.1 Projected On-Policy Linear TD(0)

Projected linear TD(0) approximates a fixed policy's discounted value from one Markov trajectory. Fix a policy π in a finite discounted Markov reward process. Let P be the resulting irreducible transition matrix on $s ,$ let d be its stationary distribution, and set $D : = \mathrm { d i a g } ( d )$ . Given the natural pre-transition history ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { t d } }$ , suppose that $( R _ { n + 1 } , S _ { n + 1 } )$ is drawn from a fixed joint reward-transition kernel depending only on $S _ { n }$ , with transition marginal $P ,$ and that

$$
r ( s ) : = \mathbb { E } [ R _ { n + 1 } \mid S _ { n } = s ] , \qquad | R _ { n + 1 } | \leq R _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } \quad { \mathrm { a . s . } }
$$

Fix $0 < \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } < 1$ , and let $V ^ { \pi }$ denote the fixed policy's value function, so that $V ^ { \pi } = r + \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } P V ^ { \pi }$ Let $\Psi = ( \psi ( s ) ^ { \top } ) _ { s \in { \cal S } } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { | { \cal S } | \times d _ { u } }$ be a full-column-rank feature matrix; its columns span the linear class $V _ { w } : = \Psi w .$ SO $V _ { w } ( s ) = \psi ( s ) ^ { \top } w$ . Let $W$ be a nonempty compact convex polyhedron, assume $w _ { 1 } \in W$ , and consider

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { w _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { W } \left[ w _ { n } + \eta _ { n } \psi ( S _ { n } ) \left\{ R _ { n + 1 } + \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } \psi ( S _ { n + 1 } ) ^ { \top } w _ { n } - \psi ( S _ { n } ) ^ { \top } w _ { n } \right\} \right] , } \\ & { \qquad \eta _ { n } = c _ { \mathrm { t d } } ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \mathfrak { b } } , \qquad c _ { \mathrm { t d } } > 0 , \qquad \frac { 1 } { 2 } < \mathfrak { b } \le 1 . } \end{array}\tag{S.1.1}
$$

Here $c _ { \mathrm { t d } } > 0$ is the multiplicative gain in the TD step size. Define

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } & { A _ { \mathrm { t d } } : = \Psi ^ { \top } D ( I - \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } P ) \Psi , } & { q _ { \mathrm { t d } } : = \Psi ^ { \top } D r , } \\ & { \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } : = ( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } ) \lambda _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( \Psi ^ { \top } D \Psi ) , } & { w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } : = A _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { - 1 } q _ { \mathrm { t d } } . } \end{array}\tag{S.1.2}
$$

The proof below verifies explicitly that $A _ { \mathrm { t d } }$ is nonsingular and $\mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } > 0$ . Assume that $w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \in W$

Corollary S.1.1 (Projected linear TD(0)). Under the preceding conditions, with $\lambda _ { \mathrm { t d } } : = c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } }$

$$
\lVert w _ { n } - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \rVert = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \mathfrak { R } _ { n } ( \mathfrak { b } , \lambda _ { \mathrm { t d } } ) ) .
$$

Thus, if $\dot { 1 } / 2 < 6 < 1$

$$
\left\| w _ { n } - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - { \mathfrak b } / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \bigg ) .
$$

At ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ , equivalently,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| w _ { n } - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \| = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \Big ) , } & { \lambda _ { \mathrm { t d } } > 1 / 2 , } \\ { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 2 } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { 3 / 2 } \Big ) , } & { \lambda _ { \mathrm { t d } } = 1 / 2 , } \\ { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \lambda _ { \mathrm { t d } } } \Big ) , } & { 0 < \lambda _ { \mathrm { t d } } < 1 / 2 . } \end{array} \right. } \end{array}
$$

When b = 1 and $\lambda _ { \mathrm { t d } } > 1 / 2$

$$
\| \Psi ( w _ { n } - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } ) \| _ { D } ^ { 2 } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \frac { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } { N _ { 0 } + n } \right) , \qquad \| v \| _ { D } ^ { 2 } : = v ^ { \top } D v .
$$

Proof. Write $\beta _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 6 }$ and define

$$
g _ { \mathrm { t d } } ( s , w ) : = c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \psi ( s ) \left\{ r ( s ) + \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } \sum _ { s ^ { \prime } } P ( s , s ^ { \prime } ) \psi ( s ^ { \prime } ) ^ { \top } w - \psi ( s ) ^ { \top } w \right\} .
$$

Set

$$
N _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { t d } } : = c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \psi ( S _ { n } ) \left\{ R _ { n + 1 } + \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } \psi ( S _ { n + 1 } ) ^ { \top } w _ { n } - \psi ( S _ { n } ) ^ { \top } w _ { n } \right\} - g _ { \mathrm { t d } } ( S _ { n } , w _ { n } ) .
$$

Then (S.1.1) has the form

$$
w _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { W } \Bigl [ w _ { n } + \beta _ { n } \{ g _ { \mathrm { t d } } ( S _ { n } , w _ { n } ) + N _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { t d } } \} \Bigr ] ,
$$

and

$$
\mathbb { E } [ N _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { t d } } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { t d } } ] = { \bf 0 } .
$$

Because W is compact, S is finite, and the rewards and features are bounded, $\{ N _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { t d } } \}$ is uniformly bounded. Because the transition kernel P does not depend on w, the required Lipschitz condition on the kernel holds with constant zero. Finite-state irreducibility gives the unique stationary law and the required hitting-time bound, and $g _ { \mathrm { t d } } ( s , \cdot )$ is uniformly Lipschitz on W. Moreover,

$$
\sum _ { s } d ( s ) g _ { \mathrm { t d } } ( s , w ) = c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \{ q _ { \mathrm { t d } } - A _ { \mathrm { t d } } w \} .
$$

It remains to verify contraction of the projected mean ODE. For $u \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { w } }$ , put $ { \boldsymbol { v } } \ = \ \Psi  { \boldsymbol { u } }$ Stationarity of d and Jensen's inequality give

$$
\| P v \| _ { D } ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { s } d ( s ) \left( \sum _ { s ^ { \prime } } P ( s , s ^ { \prime } ) v ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right) ^ { 2 } \leq \sum _ { s , s ^ { \prime } } d ( s ) P ( s , s ^ { \prime } ) v ( s ^ { \prime } ) ^ { 2 } = \| v \| _ { D } ^ { 2 } .
$$

Consequently,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { u ^ { \top } A _ { \mathrm { t d } } u = \left\| v \right\| _ { D } ^ { 2 } - \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } \langle v , P v \rangle _ { D } } \\ & { \qquad \geq \left( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } \right) \left\| v \right\| _ { D } ^ { 2 } \geq \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } \left\| u \right\| ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}\tag{S.1.3}
$$

Here the first inequality also uses Cauchy-Schwarz and $\| P v \| _ { D } \leq \| v \| _ { D }$ .Finite-state irreducibility gives $d ( s ) > 0$ for every s, and full column rank of Ψ therefore makes $\Psi ^ { \top } D \Psi$ positive definite. Hence $\mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } > 0$ . If $A _ { \mathrm { t d } } u = 0$ , then (S.1.3) gives

$$
0 = \boldsymbol { u } ^ { \top } \boldsymbol { A } _ { \mathrm { t d } } \boldsymbol { u } \geq \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } \left. \boldsymbol { u } \right. ^ { 2 } ,
$$

SO $u = 0$ . Thus $A _ { \mathrm { t d } }$ is nonsingular.

Consider

$$
\dot { z } = \Pi _ { W } \big ( z , c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \big \{ q _ { \mathrm { t d } } - A _ { \mathrm { t d } } z \big \} \big ) .
$$

For every $z , \bar { w } \in W$ and $\boldsymbol { h } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { w } }$ , the tangent-cone projection satisfies

$$
\left. z - \bar { w } , \Pi _ { W } ( z , h ) - h \right. \le 0 .
$$

Apply this inequality with $\bar { w } = w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star }$ and $h = c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \{ q _ { \mathrm { t d } } - A _ { \mathrm { t d } } z \}$ . Since $q _ { \mathrm { t d } } = A _ { \mathrm { t d } } w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star }$ , equation (S.1.3) gives, for almost every t,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \| z ( t ) - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \| ^ { 2 } = \langle z ( t ) - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } , \Pi _ { W } ( z ( t ) , c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \{ q _ { \mathrm { t d } } - A _ { \mathrm { t d } } z ( t ) \} ) \rangle } \\ & { \qquad \leq - c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \big ( z ( t ) - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \big ) ^ { \top } A _ { \mathrm { t d } } \big ( z ( t ) - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \big ) } \\ & { \qquad \leq - c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } \| z ( t ) - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \| ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

Thus its flow satisfies

$$
\left\| \mathsf { S } _ { t } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( w ) - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \right\| \leq e ^ { - c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } t } \left\| w - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \right\| .
$$

Proposition S.3.1, with $J ( w ) = \lVert w - w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \rVert , \rho _ { T } = e ^ { - c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } } T }$ , and $\lambda _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } = c _ { \mathrm { t d } } \mu _ { \mathrm { t d } }$ , gives the stated rates. The squared value-error conclusion follows from norm equivalence in finite dimensions. □

Let $T _ { \mathrm { t d } } v : = r + \gamma _ { \mathrm { t d } } P v$ , and let

$$
\Pi _ { D } : = \Psi ( \Psi ^ { \top } D \Psi ) ^ { - 1 } \Psi ^ { \top } D
$$

be the D-orthogonal projector onto range(Ψ). Then $\Psi { w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } } = \Pi _ { D } T _ { \mathrm { t d } } ( \Psi { w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } } )$ , SO $\Psi w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star }$ is the usual onpolicy projected-Bellman solution; see Tsitsiklis and Van Roy [22]. This Bellman projection differs from projw, which safeguards the iterate. If W excludes $w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star }$ , the target becomes the constrained variational-inequality solution for $A _ { \mathrm { t d } } w - q _ { \mathrm { t d } } ;$ assuming $w _ { \mathrm { t d } } ^ { \star } \in W$ preserves the usual TD target.

Finite-time TD results differ in sampling model, projection, output, and guarantee. Bhandari et al. [1] study expectation bounds for projected TD driven by a Markov trajectory, whereas Chandak and Borkar [7] obtain uniform-in-time concentration for raw unprojected online TD iterates. The result above instead gives an eventual almost-sure envelope for the raw iterate of explicitly projected TD as a direct specialization of Proposition S.3.1.

## S.2 Optimization: Projected Stochastic Gradient Descent

We consider the constrained convex optimization problem $\Phi ^ { \star } : = \mathrm { m i n } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \Phi ( \theta )$ , where Θ is a compact convex polyhedron and Φ is continuously differentiable on an open neighborhood of Θ, convex on Θ, and has an Lø-Lipschitz gradient there. Projected SGD for this problem is

$$
\theta _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \left[ \theta _ { n } - \beta _ { n } G _ { n + 1 } \right] , \qquad \beta _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 6 } , \qquad \frac { 1 } { 2 } < { \sf b } \leq 1 ,\tag{S.2.1}
$$

Let $\mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { o p t } }$ denote the natural optimization history through time $n ,$ including the current iterate and all oracle samples revealed before $G _ { n + 1 }$ is sampled. Assume that, for some deterministic $B _ { \mathrm { s g d } } < \infty$

$$
\mathbb { E } [ G _ { n + 1 } \mid { \mathcal { F } } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { o p t } } ] = \nabla \Phi ( \theta _ { n } ) , \qquad \| G _ { n + 1 } - \nabla \Phi ( \theta _ { n } ) \| \leq B _ { \mathrm { s g d } } \quad \mathrm { a . s . }
$$

This is a one-time-scale singleton-state application, so Markov noise is absent. Its limiting projected gradient flow is

$$
\dot { z } = \Pi _ { \Theta } \left( z , - \nabla \Phi ( z ) \right) .\tag{S.2.2}
$$

Corollary S.2.1 (Almost-sure rates for projected SGD). Suppose that $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { b } } \leq 1$ and that Φ is mφ-strongly convex on an open neighborhood of Θ, and let $\theta ^ { \star }$ be its unique minimizer over Θ. Then,

$$
\left\| \theta _ { n } - \theta ^ { \star } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \mathfrak { R } _ { n } ( \mathfrak { b } , m _ { \Phi } ) \right) ,\tag{S.2.3}
$$

$$
\Phi ( \theta _ { n } ) - \Phi ^ { \star } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \Re _ { n } ( { \mathsf { b } } , m _ { \Phi } ) \right) .\tag{S.2.4}
$$

$H ,$ in addition, $\theta ^ { \star } \in \operatorname { i n t } ( \Theta )$ , then

$$
\Phi ( \theta _ { n } ) - \Phi ^ { \star } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \mathfrak { R } _ { n } ( \mathfrak { b } , m _ { \Phi } ) ^ { 2 } \right) .\tag{S.2.5}
$$

Proof. Write

$$
- G _ { n + 1 } = - \nabla \Phi ( \theta _ { n } ) + \{ \nabla \Phi ( \theta _ { n } ) - G _ { n + 1 } \} .
$$

By the oracle assumptions following (S.2.1), the term in braces is a uniformly bounded martingale difference with respect to $\{ \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { o p t } } \}$ . Hence (S.2.1) has the one-time-scale form in Proposition S.3.1 with drift -∇Φ. Taking the Markov state to be a singleton makes Assumptions 3.1, 3.2, and 3.6 automatic. The oracle condition gives the bounded martingale difference required by Assumption 3.3, while $g = - \nabla \Phi$ is Lφ-Lipschitz on $\Theta ,$ as required by Assumption 3.5; $3 . 5 ;$ compactness of Θ also makes this drift bounded. The required flow contraction is verified next.

Under strong convexity,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \langle - \nabla \Phi ( { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ) + \nabla \Phi ( { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ^ { \star } ) , { \boldsymbol { \theta } } - { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ^ { \star } \rangle \leq - m _ { \Phi } \left\| { \boldsymbol { \theta } } - { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ^ { \star } \right\| ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

The first-order optimality condition $\left. \nabla \Phi ( \theta ^ { \star } ) , \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \right. \geq 0$ for every $\theta \in \Theta$ is equivalent to $\begin{array} { r l } { \small } & { { } \Pi _ { \Theta } ( \theta ^ { \star } , - \nabla \Phi ( \theta ^ { \star } ) ) = } \end{array}$ $0 ;$ see Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Lemma 1]. Thus $\theta ^ { \star }$ is an equilibrium of (S.2.2).

Hence Corollary S.12.1 gives, with $\mathsf { S } _ { t } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ denoting the flow in (S.2.2),

$$
\left\| \mathsf { S } _ { t } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( \theta ) - \theta ^ { \star } \right\| \leq e ^ { - m _ { \Phi } t } \| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \| , \qquad t \geq 0 .
$$

Thus, for $J ( \theta ) = \| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \|$ , one may take $\rho _ { T } = e ^ { - m _ { \Phi } T } ;$ SO $\lambda _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } = m _ { \Phi }$ . Proposition S.3.1 then gives $J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \Re _ { n } ( \mathfrak { b } , m _ { \Phi } ) )$ , proving (S.2.3). Since ∇Φ is bounded on the compact set Θ, the fundamental theorem of calculus along the segment from $\theta ^ { \star }$ to $\theta ,$ followed by Cauchy-Schwarz, gives

$$
0 \leq \Phi ( \theta ) - \Phi ^ { \star } \leq \left( \operatorname* { s u p } _ { u \in \Theta } \left\| \nabla \Phi ( u ) \right\| \right) \left\| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \right\| .
$$

Thus the objective-function gap is bounded by a finite deterministic multiple of the distance to $\theta ^ { \star }$ Combining this inequality with the distance rate (S.2.3) proves the objective-gap rate (S.2.4). If $\theta ^ { \star } \in \operatorname { i n t } ( \Theta )$ , then $\nabla \Phi ( \theta ^ { \star } ) = 0$ , and the descent lemma gives

$$
0 \leq \Phi ( \theta _ { n } ) - \Phi ^ { \star } \leq \frac { L _ { \Phi } } { 2 } \left\| \theta _ { n } - \theta ^ { \star } \right\| ^ { 2 } .
$$

Thus, in the interior case, the objective-function gap is controlled by a finite deterministic multiple of the squared distance. Squaring the distance rate in (S.2.3) therefore proves (S.2.5).

## S.3 One-time-scale specialization

Recursions without a fast variable can be embedded in the general scheme by taking the fast coordinate, its update, and its noise identically zero. We state the resulting one-time-scale guarantee as follows.

Proposition S.3.1 (One-time-scale contraction specialization). Consider

$$
\theta _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \left( \theta _ { n } + \beta _ { n } \{ g ( Y _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) + N _ { n + 1 } \} \right) , \qquad \beta _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 6 } ,\tag{S.3.1}
$$

where $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { b } } \leq 1$ . Suppose that $\left\{ Y _ { n } \right\}$ is a controlled Markov chain on the finite state space Y whose conditional transition kernel, given ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { n }$ , is $p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } , \cdot )$ . Assume that each $p ^ { ( \theta ) }$ has a unique stationary distribution $\mu ^ { ( \theta ) }$ , that the kernels depend Lipschitz-continuously on $\theta ,$ and that they satisfy the uniform hitting-time bound. Suppose also that $g ( y , \cdot )$ is Lipschitz on $\Theta ,$ uniformly in $y \in \mathcal { V }$ , and that $\{ N _ { n + 1 } \} _ { n \geq 1 }$ is a uniformly bounded martingale-difference sequence with respect to $\{ \mathcal { F } _ { n } \}$ . Define

$$
\bar { g } ( \theta ) : = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) g ( y , \theta )
$$

and consider the projected ODE

$$
\dot { z } = \Pi _ { \Theta } ( z , \bar { g } ( z ) ) .
$$

Let $\mathsf { S } _ { s } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ denote the time-s flow of this ODE. Assume that a Lipschitz function $J : \Theta \to \mathbb { R } _ { + }$ is nonincreasing along this ODE and that there exist $T > 0$ and $\rho _ { T } \in ( 0 , 1 )$ such that

$$
J ( \mathsf { S } _ { s } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( \theta ) ) \leq \rho _ { T } J ( \theta ) , \qquad \theta \in \Theta , \quad s \geq T .
$$

$I f { \textsf { b } } < 1$ , then, almost surely, there are finite random variables C and N such that

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq C ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - { \mathfrak { b } } / { 2 } } { \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } } , \qquad n \geq N .
$$

$I f \ { \mathfrak { b } } = 1 ,$ set

$$
\lambda _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } : = - \frac { \log \rho _ { T } } { T } .
$$

Then, almost surely, there are fnite random variables $C _ { 1 }$ and $N _ { 1 }$ such that, for $n \geq N _ { 1 }$

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq C _ { 1 } \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } , } & { \lambda _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } > 1 / 2 , } \\ { ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 2 } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { 3 / 2 } , } & { \lambda _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } = 1 / 2 , } \\ { ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \lambda _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } } , } & { 0 < \lambda _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } < 1 / 2 . } \end{array} \right.
$$

Proposition S.3.2 (One-time-scale specialization under power Lyapunov decrease). Consider (S.3.1) under the stochastic regularity and boundedness hypotheses of Proposition S.3.1, but replace its oneblock contraction hypothesis as follows. Let $\mathsf { S } _ { s } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ denote the flow of the limiting projected ODE, and suppose that $J : \Theta \to \mathbb { R } _ { + }$ is Lipschitz and nonincreasing along this flow. Let

$$
J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } J ( \theta ) .
$$

Assume that there exist $T > 0$ and a continuous function $\mathcal { D } _ { J } : [ 0 , J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } ] \to \mathbb { R } _ { + }$ such that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { D } \ j ( 0 ) = 0 , \qquad 0 < \mathcal { D } \ j ( u ) \le u \quad ( 0 < u \le J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } ) , } \end{array}
$$

and

$$
J ( \mathsf { S } _ { T } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( \theta ) ) \leq J ( \theta ) - \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( J ( \theta ) ) , \qquad \theta \in \Theta .
$$

Then $J ( \theta _ { n } ) \to 0$ almost surely.

If, in addition, for some $p _ { J } > 1 , \kappa _ { J } > 0$ , and $u _ { J } > 0$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( u ) \ge \kappa _ { J } u ^ { p _ { J } } , \qquad 0 \le u \le u _ { J } , } \end{array}
$$

then the following quantitative conclusions hold. $I f { \textsf { b } } < 1$

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( \left( N _ { 0 } + n \right) ^ { - \frac { 1 - 0 } { p _ { J } - 1 } } + \left( N _ { 0 } + n \right) ^ { - \frac { 6 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \left( \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \right) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \bigg ) .
$$

The optimized choice within the branch $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ is

$$
{ \mathfrak { b } } = { \frac { 2 p _ { J } } { 3 p _ { J } - 1 } } ,
$$

for which

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \frac { 1 } { 3 p _ { J } - 1 } } \big ( \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \big ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \bigg ) .
$$

At the harmonic endpoint ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ , the same power-decrease hypothesis gives instead

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { - \frac { 1 } { p _ { J } - 1 } } \bigg ) .
$$

We next prove Lipschitz continuity of $\boldsymbol { \theta } \mapsto \boldsymbol { \mu } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ . Otherwise, let $Q ^ { ( \theta ) }$ be the restriction of $p ^ { \dot { ( \theta ) } }$ to ${ \mathcal { V } } _ { 0 } \times { \mathcal { V } } _ { 0 }$ . We regard distributions on ${ \mathcal { V } } _ { 0 }$ as row vectors. Under the global matrix-norm convention,

$$

In particular, $\left\| \cdot \right\| _ { \infty }$ is submultiplicative. For $i , j \in \mathcal { V } _ { 0 }$ 2

Consequently,

and therefore

Thus $( 1 , v ^ { ( \theta ) } ) / d _ { \mathrm { c y c } } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ is a stationary probability vector. Uniqueness in Assumption 3.1 gives

## S.5 Supporting Results and Proofs for Main-Paper Section 6

## S.5.1 Proof of Lemma 6.1

Proof. The controlled Markov property gives

$$
\mathbb { E } \left[ u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } \right] = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } , y ) u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( y ) .
$$

Hence $\mathbb { E } [ M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ] = \mathbf { 0 }$ almost surely. Since $u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) }$ solves (6.2),

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { h ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } ) } \quad } & { } \\ & { = u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } ) - \displaystyle \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } , y ) u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( y ) . } \end{array}
$$

Adding and subtracting $u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } )$ , and using (6.4), proves (6.5).

## S.5.2 Proof of Lemma 6.2

Proof. Fix $m \ge 0 , 1 \le k \le K _ { m }$ , and $i \in \{ 1 , \ldots , d _ { x } \}$ . For these fixed indices, the partial sums in $j$ defining $\mathcal { M } _ { m , k } ^ { x } ( i )$ form a scalar martingale. By Assumptions 3.3 and $3 . 7 ,$ and by part (iii) of Lemma S.10.1, its jth increment is bounded in absolute value by $\alpha _ { n _ { m } + j } \chi ( n _ { m } + j + 1 , n _ { m } + k - 1 ) C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( x ) }$ Lemma S.14.1 bounds the corresponding squared-weight sum by $2 \alpha _ { n _ { m } } .$ Applying the Azuma Hoeffding inequality in Theorem S.15.1, using the coordinate threshold $\delta / \kappa _ { d _ { x } }$ , and taking a union bound over $i = 1 , \ldots , d _ { x }$ and $k = 1 , \ldots , K _ { m }$ proves (6.8) with the stated constant $c _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { x }$
