Under the arbitrary-norm formulation of Remark S.5.1, let $\left\| \cdot \right\| _ { x }$ be the fixed fast-coordinate norm and set $\begin{array} { r } { \kappa _ { x } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| u \| _ { \infty } \leq 1 } \| u \| _ { x } } \end{array}$ and $\lambda _ { x } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| u \| _ { x } \leq 1 } \left\| u \right\| _ { \infty }$ . For every $r \geq 1 , \| \Xi _ { r + 1 } \| _ { x } \leq C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( x ) }$ and hence $\| \Xi _ { r + 1 } \| _ { \infty } \leq \lambda _ { x } C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( x ) }$ . The good event uses the coordinate threshold $\delta / \kappa _ { x }$ . The same calculation therefore replaces $c _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { x }$ by

$$
\frac { 1 } { 4 \kappa _ { x } ^ { 2 } \lambda _ { x } ^ { 2 } ( C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( x ) } ) ^ { 2 } } ,
$$

which is the asserted norm-robust constant.

## S.5.3 Fast Poisson residual

For the residual calculation, set

$$
A _ { x } : = { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 1 } + { \mathsf { B } } _ { 6 } + { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 6 } ( 1 + { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 2 } ) , \qquad A _ { \theta } : = { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 1 } + { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 2 } { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 6 } + { \mathsf { B } } _ { 6 } ,
$$

$$
U _ { h } : = 2 \mathfrak { c } _ { 5 } \mathsf { B } _ { 6 } , \qquad C _ { \theta } : = C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { \theta } , \qquad C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } : = 8 C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { x } + 3 U _ { h } .
$$

Here $A _ { x }$ and $A _ { \theta }$ control the one-step changes of the fast and slow iterates, respectively, while $U _ { h }$ bounds the fast Poisson solution.

The definitions above and Condition 3.1 imply

$$
C _ { \theta } \beta _ { 1 } \leq C _ { h , { \mathrm { P o i s . } } } A _ { x } \alpha _ { 1 } .\tag{S.5.1}
$$

Indeed, $A _ { x } = A _ { \theta } + { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 6 } \geq A _ { \theta } , C _ { \theta } = C _ { h , \operatorname { P o i s } . } A _ { \theta }$ and $\beta _ { 1 } \leq \alpha _ { 1 }$ . Together with the monotonicity of $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n } .$ this is the domination used below to control changes in the Poisson solution.

Recall that

$$
\Delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { h } = u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } ) - u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) .
$$

For $m \geq 0$ and $0 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ , define the associated local Poisson residual by

$$
\mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { x } : = \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { n _ { m } + j } \chi \bigl ( n _ { m } + j + 1 , n _ { m } + k - 1 \bigr ) \Delta _ { n _ { m } + j + 1 } ^ { h } ,\tag{S.5.2}
$$

with $\mathcal { R } _ { m , 0 } ^ { x } : = \mathbf { 0 }$

Proposition S.5.1 (Local fast Poisson residual). Under (3.3), with (S.5.1) as established above,

$$
\left\| \mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { x } \right\| \leq C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } \alpha _ { n _ { m } } , \qquad m \geq 0 , \quad 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } .\tag{S.5.3}
$$

Proof. The case $k = 0$ is immediate. Fix $k \geq 1$ . For the remainder of this proof, write

$$
\begin{array} { r } { s : = n _ { m } , \qquad t : = n _ { m } + k , \qquad u _ { r } : = u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { r } , \theta _ { r } ) } , \qquad A _ { r } : = \alpha _ { r } \chi ( r + 1 , t - 1 ) . } \end{array}
$$

Summation by parts in (S.5.2) gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l } {  { \mathcal R _ { m , k } ^ { x } = A _ { s } u _ { s } ( Y _ { s } ) - A _ { t - 1 } u _ { t - 1 } ( Y _ { t } ) } } \\ & { \quad \ + \sum _ { r = s + 1 } ^ { t - 1 } A _ { r } \big [ u _ { r } ( Y _ { r } ) - u _ { r - 1 } ( Y _ { r } ) \big ] } \\ & { \quad \ + \sum _ { r = s + 1 } ^ { t - 1 } ( A _ { r } - A _ { r - 1 } ) u _ { r - 1 } ( Y _ { r } ) , } \end{array}
$$

with empty sums interpreted as zero. By part $( \mathrm { i v } )$ of Lemma S.10.1, (S.5.1), and the monotonicity of $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| u _ { r } ( Y _ { r } ) - u _ { r - 1 } ( Y _ { r } ) \| \leq C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { x } \alpha _ { r - 1 } + C _ { \theta } \beta _ { r - 1 } \leq 2 C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { x } \alpha _ { r - 1 } . } \end{array}
$$

Since $\alpha _ { r - 1 } \leq 2 \alpha _ { r }$ , Lemma S.14.1 yields

$$
\left\| \sum _ { r = s + 1 } ^ { t - 1 } A _ { r } \big [ u _ { r } ( Y _ { r } ) - u _ { r - 1 } ( Y _ { r } ) \big ] \right\| \leq 8 C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { x } \alpha _ { t - 1 } .
$$

The subadditivity argument used in Lemma S.14.2 shows that $A _ { r }$ is nondecreasing. Hence

$$
\sum _ { r = s + 1 } ^ { t - 1 } | A _ { r } - A _ { r - 1 } | = A _ { t - 1 } - A _ { s } \leq \alpha _ { t - 1 } .
$$

Part (ii) of Lemma S.10.1 bounds the second interior sum by $U _ { h } \alpha _ { t - 1 }$ , and the two boundary terms by $2 U _ { h } \alpha _ { s }$ . Since $\alpha _ { t - 1 } \leq \alpha _ { s }$ 2

$$
\left\| \mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { x } \right\| \leq \bigl ( 8 C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { x } + 3 U _ { h } \bigr ) \alpha _ { s } = C _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { x } \alpha _ { n _ { m } } ,
$$

which proves (S.5.3).

## S.5.4 Proof of Corollary 6.1

Proof. On $\mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( j ) } ( \delta _ { j } )$ , (6.13) gives

$$
\varepsilon _ { n _ { j + 1 } } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \mathcal { W } _ { j } ( K _ { j } ) \varepsilon _ { n _ { j } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { x } g _ { j } ^ { ( x ) } ( \delta _ { j } ) .
$$

Iterating this recursion for $j = 0 , \ldots , m - 1$ proves (6.15). The probability estimate (6.16) follows from the union bound and Lemma 6.2. □

## S.5.5 Arbitrary fast-coordinate norms

Remark S.5.1 (Choice of norm for the fast coordinate). The fast-scale arguments in Sections $6 - 8$ remain valid if the Euclidean norm on $\mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { x } }$ is replaced by any fixed norm $\| \cdot \| _ { x } ,$ provided that the contraction, local Lipschitz, bounded-set, stability, and noise bounds involving the fast coordinate are formulated in that norm. In $\mathsf { B } ( R )$ , the fast-state radius and the norm of $h$ are then interpreted using $\| \cdot \| _ { x } ,$ while the norm of $f$ remains Euclidean. Recall the two norm-equivalence constants introduced in the proof of Lemma 6.2:

$$
\kappa _ { x } = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| u \| _ { \infty } \leq 1 } \| u \| _ { x } , \qquad \lambda _ { x } = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \| u \| _ { x } \leq 1 } \| u \| _ { \infty } .
$$

Thus $\left\| z \right\| _ { x } \leq \kappa _ { x } \left\| z \right\| _ { \infty }$ and $\left\| z \right\| _ { \infty } \leq \lambda _ { x } \left\| z \right\| _ { x } .$ The first inequality converts the coordinatewise martingale estimate to $\| \cdot \| _ { x } ,$ while the second converts an x-norm increment bound to a coordinatewise one. The corresponding fast-martingale event and tail constant are recorded in the proof of Lemma 6.2 in Section S.5.2 of the Online Companion. The slow projection and Skorokhod-map arguments remain Euclidean.

## S.6 Proofs from Main-Paper Section 7

## S.6.1 Proof of Lemma 7.1

Proof. The martingale-difference property follows from

$$
\mathbb { E } \left[ u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } ) \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } \right] = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } , y ) u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( y ) .
$$

Since $u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) }$ solves (7.6) with the parameter θ equal to $\theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) }$ , we have

$$
f ( Y _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } , x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) , \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) - f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ; \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) = u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) - \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } , y ) u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( y ) .
$$

Adding and subtracting $u _ { f } ^ { ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } ) } ( Y _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } )$ gives (7.9).

## S.6.2 Proof of Lemma 7.2

Proof. By Assumptions 3.3 and 3.7, and part (iii) of Lemma S.11.1, the coordinate increments of the weighted martingale differences $\beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } [ ( M ^ { \prime } ) _ { \ell + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } + ( M ^ { \prime \prime \prime } ) _ { \ell + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } ]$ are bounded by $\beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ . Apply the Azuma-Hoeffding inequality in Theorem S.15.1 coordinatewise, use $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } ( \boldsymbol { \beta } _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } ) ^ { 2 } = b ( n _ { m } ) - b ( n _ { m } + } \end{array}$ k), and take a union bound over $i = 1 , \ldots , d _ { \theta }$ and $k = 1 , \ldots , K _ { m }$ to obtain the stated bound.

## S.6.3 Proof of Corollary 7.1

Proof. On $\mathcal { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { f } )$ , Proposition 6.1, specifically (6.12), gives

$$
\sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { K _ { m } - 1 } \beta _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \left\| e _ { \ell } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| = \sum _ { r = n _ { m } } ^ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \beta _ { r } \left\| x _ { r } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { r } ) \right\| \leq S _ { m } ( \delta _ { f } ) .
$$

On $\mathcal { G } _ { \theta } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { s } )$ , Proposition 7.1 with $n = K _ { m }$ therefore yields

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } } \Big \| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \Big \| \leq E _ { m } ^ { ( \theta , \tilde { \theta } ) } ( \delta _ { f } , \delta _ { s } )
$$

by (7.15). Since both paths are right-continuous step embeddings on the same slow-time grid,

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq H _ { m } } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) \right\| = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq K _ { m } } \left\| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| .
$$

The asserted step-path bound follows.

## S.7 Proofs from Main-Paper Section 8

## S.7.1 Proof of Theorem 8.3

Proof. Theorem 8.2 first gives $U _ { m } \to 0$ . Consequently, the local power condition (8.4) and (8.2) imply, eventually almost surely,

$$
U _ { m + 1 } \leq U _ { m } - \kappa _ { J } U _ { m } ^ { p _ { J } } + e _ { m } ^ { J } .
$$

Suppose first that ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ . By (S.15.5),

$$
N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } \asymp ( m + 1 ) ^ { 1 / ( 1 - \mathfrak { b } ) } .
$$

The assumed rate envelope for $d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ therefore gives

$$
e _ { m } ^ { J } \leq C ( m + 2 ) ^ { - \frac { r } { 1 - { \mathfrak { b } } } } \{ \log ( m + 2 ) \} ^ { 1 / 2 } .
$$

Applying Lemma S.15.2 with $\zeta = r / ( 1 - \mathfrak { b } )$ and $\ell = 1 / 2$ yields

$$
U _ { m } \leq C ( \omega ) \left\{ ( m + 2 ) ^ { - 1 / ( p , - 1 ) } + ( m + 2 ) ^ { - \frac { r } { p _ { J } ( 1 - \mathfrak { b } ) } } \{ \log ( m + 2 ) \} ^ { 1 / ( 2 p , J ) } \right\} .
$$

Using (S.15.5) once more gives

$$
U _ { m } \leq C ( \omega ) \left\{ \left( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } \right) ^ { - \frac { 1 - 6 } { p _ { J } - 1 } } + \left( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } \right) ^ { - \frac { r } { p _ { J } } } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) \} ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \right\} .
$$

For $n _ { m } \leq n < n _ { m + 1 }$ , the within-block contribution in (8.3) is of order $( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) }$ 2 which is eventually bounded by its $p _ { J } \mathrm { t h }$ root. The comparison between $N _ { 0 } + n$ and $N _ { 0 } + n _ { m }$ used in the proof of Theorem 8.4 then proves (8.5).

Suppose now that ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ . By Lemma S.15.5, $N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } \asymp e ^ { m T }$ . Hence the assumed envelope gives

$$
e _ { m } ^ { J } \leq C e ^ { - r T m } ( m + 1 ) ^ { 1 / 2 } .
$$

For every $Q > 0$ , the right-hand side is eventually at most $C _ { Q } ( m + 2 ) ^ { - Q }$ . Choose $Q > p _ { J } / ( p _ { J } - 1 )$ and apply Lemma S.15.2 with $\zeta = Q$ and $\ell = 0$ . Then

$$
U _ { m } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( m + 2 ) ^ { - 1 / ( p _ { J } - 1 ) } \Big ) .
$$

Here and below, the implicit constants in $O _ { T } ( \cdot )$ may depend on the fixed block horizon T. Since $m = T ^ { - 1 } \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) + O _ { T } ( 1 )$ , and since Lemma S.15.5 makes $N _ { 0 } + n$ comparable with $N _ { 0 } + n _ { m }$ throughout block m, while $e _ { m } ^ { J }$ is exponentially smaller, the within-block estimate yields

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { - 1 / ( p _ { J } - 1 ) } \Big ) ,
$$

which is (8.6).

Remark S.7.1 (Sharpness of the harmonic-endpoint power rate). The logarithmic rate in (8.6) is intrinsic to the assumed power decrease. Indeed, for $u _ { 1 } > 0$ and $N _ { 0 }$ sufficiently large, the deterministic scalar recursion

$$
u _ { n + 1 } = u _ { n } - \frac { \kappa _ { J } } { N _ { 0 } + n } u _ { n } ^ { p _ { J } } , \qquad p _ { J } > 1 ,
$$

remains positive and satisfies

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } u _ { n } \big [ ( p _ { J } - 1 ) \kappa _ { J } \log n \big ] ^ { 1 / ( p _ { J } - 1 ) } = 1 .
$$

To see this, set $v _ { n } : = u _ { n } ^ { - ( p _ { J } - 1 ) }$ . A Taylor expansion gives

$$
v _ { n + 1 } - v _ { n } = \frac { ( p _ { J } - 1 ) \kappa _ { J } } { N _ { 0 } + n } + O \Big ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 2 } \Big ) ,
$$

and summation yields $v _ { n } = ( p _ { J } - 1 ) \kappa _ { J } \log n + O ( 1 )$ . Thus a polynomial iteration-wise rate cannot be guaranteed at ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ from the power-decrease condition alone. In particular, the rate is $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( 1 / \log n )$ when $p _ { J } = 2$

## S.7.2 Proof of Theorem 8.4

Proof. Since

$$
\sum _ { m \geq 1 } \mathbb { P } \Big ( ( \mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } ) ^ { c } \Big ) < \infty ,
$$

the Borel-Cantelli lemma implies that, almost surely, there exists $m _ { 0 } < \infty$ such that $\mathcal { G } _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ holds for every $m \geq m _ { 0 }$ . Fix such a sample path and write

$$
U _ { m } : = J \bigl ( \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( 0 ) \bigr ) .
$$

From (8.1), the $L _ { J ^ { - } } \mathrm { I }$ ipschitz continuity of J, $H _ { m } \geq T$ , the ODE contraction assumption, and $\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( \dot { H } _ { m } ) = \theta ^ { ( m + 1 ) , \circ } ( 0 )$ , we obtain

$$
U _ { m + 1 } \leq \rho _ { T } U _ { m } + L _ { J } C _ { B , T } d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } , \qquad m \geq m _ { 0 } .
$$

Iteration gives

$$
U _ { m ( n ) } \leq \rho _ { T } ^ { m ( n ) - m _ { 0 } } J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } + L _ { J } C _ { B , T } \sum _ { j = m _ { 0 } } ^ { m ( n ) - 1 } \rho _ { T } ^ { m ( n ) - 1 - j } d _ { j } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } .
$$

If n lies in block $m = m ( n )$ , then

$$
t ( n ) - T _ { m } \in [ 0 , H _ { m } ] , \qquad \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ( n ) - T _ { m } ) = \theta _ { n } .
$$

Hence (8.1), the Lipschitz continuity of $^ { J , }$ and the nonincrease of J along the ODE trajectory imply

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq J \Big ( z ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( t ( n ) - T _ { m } ) \Big ) + L _ { J } C _ { B , T } d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } } } \\ { { \qquad \leq U _ { m } + L _ { J } C _ { B , T } d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } . } } \end{array}
$$

Substituting the preceding bound for $U _ { m }$ proves the first claim. For the rate assertion, suppose first that ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ . Applying Lemma S.15.3, with the present value of $r ,$ to the assumed bound on $d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ shows that both the geometric convolution and the initial transient are bounded by a constant multiple of

$$
( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m ( n ) } ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m ( n ) } ) } .
$$

Moreover, Lemma S.15.4 gives

$$
N _ { 0 } + n _ { m ( n ) } \leq N _ { 0 } + n \leq N _ { 0 } + n _ { m ( n ) } + C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m ( n ) } ) ^ { \flat } \leq 2 ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m ( n ) } )
$$

for all sufficiently large $m ( n )$ . Thus $N _ { 0 } + n _ { m ( n ) }$ and $N _ { 0 } + n ,$ as well as their logarithms, are comparable. This proves the ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ assertion.

Suppose ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ , and recall $\lambda _ { T }$ from (8.7). Lemma S.15.6, with $\ell = 1 / 2$ and $\rho = \rho _ { T }$ , bounds the endpoint term $U _ { m }$ and its initial transient. For $n _ { m } \leq n < n _ { m + 1 }$ , the within-block estimate established above gives

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq U _ { m } + L _ { J } C _ { B , T } d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } .
$$

Writing $X _ { m } : = N _ { 0 } + n _ { m }$ , Lemma S.15.5 gives

$$
X _ { m } \leq N _ { 0 } + n < ( e ^ { T } + 1 ) X _ { m } .
$$

Thus $N _ { 0 } + n \stackrel { \smile } { \sim } _ { T } X _ { m } .$ and their logarithms are comparable. Moreover, the current-block term $d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } = O ( X _ { m } ^ { - r } { \sqrt { \log X _ { m } } } )$ is bounded by the corresponding endpoint order in each of the three cases below. Consequently,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { J ( \theta _ { n } ) = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } ) , } & { \lambda _ { T } > r , } \\ { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - r } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { 3 / 2 } ) , } & { \lambda _ { T } = r , } \\ { O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \lambda _ { T } } ) , } & { 0 < \lambda _ { T } < r , } \end{array} \right. } \end{array}
$$

which proves (8.8).

## S.7.3 Proof of Theorem 8.1

Proof. Let

$$
\varepsilon _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } : = \| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| ,
$$

and recall $q = 1 - \gamma$ . Lemma 6.2 and the definition of $\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m )$ give

$$
\mathbb { P } \Big ( \Big [ \mathscr { G } _ { x } ^ { ( m ) } \big ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \big ) \Big ] ^ { c } \Big ) \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } } .
$$

Thus, by Borel-Cantelli, these events hold for all sufficiently large m, almost surely. On those blocks, (6.13) gives

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \varepsilon _ { n _ { m + 1 } } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \mathcal { W } _ { m } ( K _ { m } ) \varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { x } g _ { m } ^ { ( x ) } \mathopen { } \mathclose \bgroup \left( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \aftergroup \egroup \right) . } \end{array}
$$

Moreover,

$$
\sum _ { r = n _ { m } } ^ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \alpha _ { r } \ge \frac { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) \ge T ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { \mathfrak { n } - \mathfrak { a } } ,
$$

SO

$$
{ \mathcal W } _ { m } ( K _ { m } ) \le \exp \{ - q T ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { \mathfrak { h } - { \mathfrak a } } \} .
$$

Suppose first that ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ . The preceding factor is at most $1 / 2$ for all sufficiently large $m$ . Hence, eventually almost surely,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \varepsilon _ { n _ { m + 1 } } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 } \varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { x } g _ { m } ^ { ( x ) } \bigl ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \bigr ) . } \end{array}
$$

Lemma S.15.4 gives

$$
g _ { m } ^ { ( x ) } \Bigl ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \Bigr ) \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

Applying Lemma S.15.3 to the endpoint recursion yields

$$
\varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } \leq C ( \omega ) ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) }
$$

for all sufficiently large m. If $n _ { m } \leq n < n _ { m + 1 }$ , then (6.11) and ${ \mathcal { W } } _ { m } ( n - n _ { m } ) \leq 1$ give

$$
\varepsilon _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } \leq \varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } + C _ { x } g _ { m } ^ { ( x ) } \big ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \big ) .
$$

Finally, Lemma S.15.4 gives $K _ { m } \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { \mathfrak { b } }$ , and ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ implies $N _ { 0 } + n \le 2 ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } )$ for all sufficiently large m. The endpoint rate therefore holds at every iterate n.

Suppose now that ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ , and put

$$
\varphi _ { m } ^ { x } : = X _ { m } ^ { - r _ { x } ( 0 , 1 ) } \sqrt { \log X _ { m } } , \qquad X _ { m } : = N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } .
$$

Lemma S.15.5 gives $g _ { m } ^ { ( x ) } \bigl ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \bigr ) \leq C _ { T } \varphi _ { m } ^ { x }$ . Choose any $\eta \in ( 0 , e ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , 1 ) T } )$ . Since

$$
\mathcal { W } _ { m } ( K _ { m } ) \leq \exp \{ - q T X _ { m } ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak { a } } } \} ,
$$

eventually $\mathcal { W } _ { m } ( K _ { m } ) \leq \eta$ . The endpoint recursion and Lemma S.15.6, with $\rho = \eta$ and $\ell = 1 / 2$ therefore give

$$
\varepsilon _ { n _ { m } } ^ { ( x ) } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigl ( X _ { m } ^ { - r _ { x } ( { \mathsf { a } } , 1 ) } \sqrt { \log X _ { m } } \bigr ) .
$$

The pointwise block estimate and Lemma S.15.5 transfer this bound to every n, proving the harmonic-endpoint assertion. □

## S.7.4 Proof of Corollary 8.1

Proof. Suppose first that ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ . We identify the rate of $d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } }$ . By Lemma S.15.4, for all sufficiently large $m .$

$$
D _ { m } \leq ( T + 1 ) \beta _ { n _ { m } } , \qquad K _ { m } \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { \mathfrak { b } } ,
$$

and

$$
L _ { m } ^ { x } + L _ { m } ^ { \theta } \leq C _ { T } \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) .
$$

Consequently,

$$
\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( x ) } ( m ) \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - { \mathfrak { a } } / 2 } { \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } } ,
$$

and

$$
\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( m ) \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - { \mathfrak { b } } / 2 } { \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } } .
$$

Moreover,

$$
\alpha _ { n _ { m } } + \frac { \beta _ { n _ { m } } } { \alpha _ { n _ { m } } } \leq 2 ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - p _ { \mathrm { d e t } } } .
$$

Since

$$
r _ { x } ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } ) \leq p _ { \mathrm { d e t } } ,
$$

all deterministic terms are covered by the same envelope. Hence

$$
d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

Theorem 8.4 then gives the same iteration-wise exponent for $J ( \theta _ { n } )$ , and Theorem 8.1 gives the fast tracking bound.

It remains to optimize $r _ { x } ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } )$ . For fixed ${ \mathfrak { b } } > 3 / 4$ , its maximum is obtained by balancing ${ \mathfrak { a } } / 2 = { \mathfrak { b } } - { \mathfrak { a } }$ , that is, ${ \mathfrak { a } } = 2 { \mathfrak { b } } / 3$ and equals ${ \mathfrak { b } } / 3 .$ For $1 / 2 < { \mathsf { b } } \leq 3 / 4$ , its supremum is ${ \mathfrak { b } } - 1 / 2$

approached as ${ \mathfrak { a } } \downarrow 1 / 2$ (and not attained under the strict constraint). In either case, the supremum over $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { a } } < { \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ is at most $1 / 3$ . For the displayed choice

$$
{ \mathfrak { b } } = 1 - 3 \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } , \qquad { \mathfrak { a } } = { \frac { 2 { \mathfrak { b } } } { 3 } } = { \frac { 2 } { 3 } } - 2 \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } ,
$$

the restriction $0 < \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } < 1 / 1 2$ gives ${ \mathfrak { b } } > 3 / 4$ , so the balancing branch above applies, and

$$
\frac { 1 } { 2 } < a < { \mathfrak { b } } < 1 ,
$$

and

$$
r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) = \mathrm { m i n } \left\{ \frac { 1 } { 3 } - \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } , \frac { 1 } { 3 } - \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \right\} = \frac { 1 } { 3 } - \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } .
$$

This proves the stated ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ slow and fast optimized rates.

For the harmonic endpoint ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ , set $X _ { m } : = N _ { 0 } + n _ { m }$ . Lemma S.15.5 gives

$$
D _ { m } \leq C _ { T } X _ { m } ^ { - 1 } , \qquad K _ { m } \leq C _ { T } X _ { m } , \qquad L _ { m } ^ { x } + L _ { m } ^ { \theta } \leq C _ { T } \log X _ { m } .
$$

Consequently,

$$
d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \leq C _ { T } X _ { m } ^ { - r _ { x } ( { \mathsf { a } } , 1 ) } { \sqrt { \log X _ { m } } } .
$$

The slow assertions follow from Theorem 8.4, and the fast assertion follows from Theorem 8.1. Finally, $r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , 1 )$ is maximized by ${ \mathfrak { a } } / 2 = 1 - { \mathfrak { a } }$ , namely ${ \mathfrak { a } } = 2 / 3$ , and its maximum is $1 / 3$ This proves the harmonic-endpoint claims. □

Remark S.7.2 (Comparison with recent mean-square bounds). For every fixed $0 < \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } < 1 / 1 2$ Corollary 8.1 gives an almost-sure distance envelope of order $n ^ { - 1 / 3 + \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } } \sqrt { \log n } .$ whose square has trajectory-wise scale $n ^ { - 2 / 3 + 2 \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { r a t e } } } \log n$ . This observation does not imply a mean-square bound: passing from an almost-sure envelope with a random prefactor to an expectation requires an additional integrability argument. At the level of exponents, the trajectory-wise squared scale agrees, up to logarithmic and arbitrarily small polynomial losses, with the $O ( n ^ { - 2 / 3 } )$ mean-square rate for contractive two-time-scale $\mathrm { S A }$ with arbitrary-norm contractions and Markov noise in Chandak et al. [9]. Other $O ( k ^ { - a } )$ or $O ( k ^ { - 1 } )$ mean-square results have been obtained by Chandak [6], Doan [14] under different assumptions or for modified recursions. Because their algorithms, assumptions, and modes of convergence differ, those results are not directly comparable with the almost-sure envelope above. Our result instead provides blockwise high-probability tracking for projected-ODE slow dynamics without assuming a contractive discrete slow update map. The comparison in this remark concerns the linear-decrease branch, which uses uniform one-block contraction of a Lipschitz Lyapunov function. The nonlinear decrease result gives different exponents and is not being compared here with the cited contractive mean-square bounds.

## S.7.5 Proof of Corollary 8.2

Proof. For the case ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ , the proof of Corollary 8.1 shows that

$$
d _ { m } ^ { \mathrm { r a t e } } \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r _ { x } ( \mathfrak { a } , \mathfrak { b } ) } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

Theorem 8.3 therefore gives the slow polynomial exponent

$$
s ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } ) : = \operatorname* { m i n } \left\{ { \frac { 1 - { \mathfrak { b } } } { p _ { J } - 1 } } , { \frac { r _ { x } ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } ) } { p _ { J } } } \right\} .
$$

Fix $\sigma > 0$ If $s ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } ) \geq \sigma$ , then necessarily

$$
{ \mathfrak { a } } \geq 2 p _ { J } \sigma , \qquad { \mathfrak { b } } - { \mathfrak { a } } \geq p _ { J } \sigma , \qquad { \mathfrak { b } } \leq 1 - ( p _ { J } - 1 ) \sigma .
$$

Hence

$$
3 p _ { J } \sigma \leq { \mathsf { b } } \leq { \mathsf { 1 } } - ( p _ { J } - 1 ) \sigma ,
$$

which implies

$$
\sigma \leq { \frac { 1 } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } .
$$

Equality is attained at

$$
{ \mathfrak { a } } = { \frac { 2 p _ { J } } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } , \qquad { \mathfrak { b } } = { \frac { 3 p _ { J } } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } .
$$

For this choice,

$$
r _ { x } ( { \mathfrak { a } } , { \mathfrak { b } } ) = { \frac { p _ { J } } { 4 p _ { J } - 1 } } .
$$

The slow bound follows from Theorem 8.3, while the fast bound follows from Theorem 8.1.

At the harmonic endpoint b = 1, take ${ \mathfrak { a } } = 2 / 3$ . The harmonic branch of Theorem 8.3 gives

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { - 1 / ( p _ { J } - 1 ) } \Big ) ,
$$

while Theorem 8.1 gives

$$
\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 3 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \bigg ) .
$$

## S.7.6 Proof of Proposition 8.1

Proof. All bounds below hold for the iterates generated by the selected schedule; in particular, the deterministic stability assumption is retained for those iterates. We first establish convergence for both schedules. This step is needed because the second schedule is outside Condition 3.1.

A comparison on fxed fast-time intervals. For either schedule in (8.9), the sequences $\alpha _ { n } , \beta _ { n }$ and $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }$ are nonincreasing, with

$$
0 < \beta _ { n } \leq \alpha _ { n } \leq 1 , \qquad { \frac { \beta _ { n } } { \alpha _ { n } } } \longrightarrow 0 , \qquad t _ { n } \alpha _ { n } \longrightarrow \infty .
$$

Both step-size sums diverge. For the second schedule these facts follow directly from

$$
{ \frac { \beta _ { n } } { \alpha _ { n } } } = { \frac { B } { A \log t _ { n } } } , \qquad \sum _ { n < k } \alpha _ { n } = { \frac { A } { 2 } } ( \log t _ { k } ) ^ { 2 } + O ( 1 ) , \qquad \sum _ { n < k } \beta _ { n } = B \log t _ { k } + O ( 1 ) .
$$

The shift conditions ensure that $\alpha _ { n } , \beta _ { n }$ and $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }$ are nonincreasing and that $0 < \beta _ { n } \leq \alpha _ { n } \leq 1$ for every $n \geq 1$

Fix a fast-time block horizon $T _ { x } > 0$ , and define deterministic block endpoints

$$
k _ { 0 } = 1 , \qquad k _ { j + 1 } = \operatorname* { m i n } \left\{ k > k _ { j } : \sum _ { n = k _ { j } } ^ { k - 1 } \alpha _ { n } \geq T _ { x } \right\} .
$$

For either schedule,

$$
k _ { j + 1 } - k _ { j } = O ( \alpha _ { k _ { j } } ^ { - 1 } ) , \qquad \frac { t _ { k _ { j + 1 } } } { t _ { k _ { j } } } \longrightarrow 1 , \qquad \sum _ { n = k _ { j } } ^ { k _ { j + 1 } - 1 } \alpha _ { n } \in [ T _ { x } , T _ { x } + \alpha _ { k _ { j } } ] .\tag{S.7.1}
$$

Indeed, $t \alpha ( t )  \infty$ , and each displayed step-size function has logarithmic derivative of magnitude at most $1 / t$ for $t \geq t _ { 1 }$ . On intervals of length $O ( 1 / \alpha _ { k _ { j } } )$ , its ratio to its initial value therefore tends to one. This proves (S.7.1), as well as uniform comparability of the steps within each such interval.

Put

$$
\xi _ { n + 1 } = h ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } ) + M _ { n + 1 } .
$$

The Poisson equation (6.3) decomposes this term as a bounded martingale difference plus $u _ { n } ( Y _ { n } ) -$ $u _ { n } ( Y _ { n + 1 } )$ , where $u _ { n } = u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) }$ . The increment and Poisson-sensitivity bounds of Lemma S.10.1 use only the standing assumptions and $\beta _ { n } \leq \alpha _ { n } ;$ they therefore hold for both schedules. Summation by parts gives, uniformly for $k _ { j } < r \leq k _ { j + 1 }$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \left\| \displaystyle \sum _ { n = k _ { j } } ^ { r - 1 } \alpha _ { n } \{ u _ { n } ( Y _ { n } ) - u _ { n } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) \} \right\| } \\ & { \quad \le C \left\{ \alpha _ { k _ { j } } + \displaystyle \sum _ { n = k _ { j } + 1 } ^ { r - 1 } | \alpha _ { n } - \alpha _ { n - 1 } | + \displaystyle \sum _ { n = k _ { j } + 1 } ^ { r - 1 } \alpha _ { n - 1 } ( \| x _ { n } - x _ { n - 1 } \| + \| \theta _ { n } - \theta _ { n - 1 } \| ) \right\} \le C _ { T _ { x } } \alpha _ { k _ { j } } . } \end{array}
$$

Here and below deterministic constants may depend on $T _ { x }$ . The martingale part has bounded increments of order $\alpha _ { n }$ , and

$$
\sum _ { n = k _ { j } } ^ { k _ { j + 1 } - 1 } \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } \leq ( T _ { x } + 1 ) \alpha _ { k _ { j } } .
$$

The maximal martingale inequality, applied coordinatewise, consequently bounds its maximum partial sum above u with probability at most $C \exp \{ - c u ^ { 2 } / \alpha _ { k _ { j } } \}$ . Choosing a sufficiently large multiple of $\sqrt { \alpha _ { k _ { j } } \log t _ { k _ { j } } }$ , the failure probabilities are at most $C t _ { k _ { j } } ^ { - 3 }$ , and are summable since $k _ { j } \geq$ $j + 1$ . Thus, almost surely,

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } _ { k _ { j } \le r \le k _ { j + 1 } } \left\| \sum _ { n = k _ { j } } ^ { r - 1 } \alpha _ { n } \xi _ { n + 1 } \right\| = O \Big ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { k _ { j } } \log t _ { k _ { j } } } \Big ) .\tag{S.7.2}
$$

Restart an averaged recursion at each $k _ { j }$

$$
\widehat { x } _ { k _ { j } } ^ { ( j ) } = x _ { k _ { j } } , \qquad \widehat { x } _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( j ) } = ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) \widehat { x } _ { n } ^ { ( j ) } + \alpha _ { n } h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \widehat { x } _ { n } ^ { ( j ) } ; \theta _ { n } ) .
$$

The parameter sequence in this recursion is the actual $\theta _ { n }$ . The averaged drift $h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ; \theta ) - x$ is $( 1 + \gamma ) \dashv$ Lipschitz in x, uniformly in θ. Subtracting the two recursions, taking partial sums, and applying discrete Gronwall over a fast-time interval of length at most $T _ { x } + 1$ to (S.7.2) yields

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } _ { k _ { j } \le n \le k _ { j + 1 } } \left\| x _ { n } - \widehat { x } _ { n } ^ { ( j ) } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \sqrt { \alpha _ { k _ { j } } \log t _ { k _ { j } } } \right) .\tag{S.7.3}
$$

This argument estimates martingale partial sums before making a pathwise drift comparison; it introduces no martingale weights containing future slow iterates.

Initial convergence. Let $e _ { j } : = \left\| x _ { k _ { j } } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { k _ { j } } ) \right\|$ , and recall $q = 1 - \gamma > 0$ . One step of the averaged recursion contracts the distance to the current fast target by $1 - q \alpha _ { n }$ , while the target moves by at most $L _ { f p } A _ { \theta } \beta _ { n }$ . Using (S.7.3) and monotonicity of $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }$ , we obtain

$$
e _ { j + 1 } \leq e ^ { - q T _ { x } } e _ { j } + O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \sqrt { \alpha _ { k _ { j } } \log t _ { k _ { j } } } + \frac { \beta _ { k _ { j } } } { \alpha _ { k _ { j } } } \right) .\tag{S.7.4}
$$

The deterministic envelope on the right tends to zero and has consecutive ratio tending to one by (S.7.1). Geometric comparison gives the same bound for $e _ { j }$ , and the within-interval comparison gives

$$
\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \biggl ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log t _ { n } } + \frac { \beta _ { n } } { \alpha _ { n } } \biggr ) \longrightarrow 0 .\tag{S.7.5}
$$

For clarity, the geometric comparison follows by dividing (S.7.4) by its envelope at $k _ { j + 1 }$ . The coefficient of the normalized previous error tends to $e ^ { - q T _ { x } } < 1$ , so the normalized errors are eventually bounded.

Now use the deterministic slow-time blocks $n _ { m }$ with horizon T from Assumption 8.2. Their lengths in slow time lie in $[ T , T + \beta _ { n m } ]$ . For either schedule, $t _ { n _ { m + 1 } } / t _ { n _ { m } }$ is bounded. In the second schedule it tends to $e ^ { T / B }$ . Apply the slow Poisson decomposition (Lemma 7.1) to $f ( y , x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) , \theta ) \ : -$ $f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \theta ; \theta )$ The same partial-sum argument, now with weights $\beta _ { n } .$ gives almost-sure martingale errors of order $\sqrt { \beta _ { n _ { m } } \log t _ { n _ { m } } }$ and Poisson residuals of order $\beta _ { n _ { m } }$ . Their failure probabilities can again be chosen at most $C t _ { n _ { m } } ^ { - 3 }$ The forcing due to the fast error is bounded by $( T +$ $\begin{array} { r } { 1 ) L _ { f } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { n _ { m } \leq n < n _ { m + 1 } } \| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| } \end{array}$ . Consequently, the deterministic Skorokhod-map and Gronwall comparison used in the proof of Theorem 7.1 gives a uniform slow-path error on block m tending to zero. This uses only the driver estimates just obtained and the vanishing largest step in the block. In particular, it applies to the second schedule without invoking any pure-power rate formula.

Lipschitz continuity of J and its one-block decrease therefore give

$$
J ( \theta _ { n _ { m + 1 } } ) \leq J ( \theta _ { n _ { m } } ) - \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( J ( \theta _ { n _ { m } } ) ) + o ( 1 ) \quad \mathrm { a l m o s t ~ s u r e l y } ,
$$

with a within-block increase bounded by $o ( 1 )$ . Since $\mathcal { D } _ { J }$ is continuous and strictly positive away from zero, the scalar convergence argument in Theorem 8.2 gives $J ( \theta _ { n } ) \to 0$ . Compactness of Θ and $J ^ { - 1 } ( \{ 0 \} ) = { \mathcal { E } } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta }$ then imply dist $( \theta _ { n } , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } )  0$ almost surely. These consistency statements are the only inputs from this step needed for the sharper rates.

Distance from a required endpoint. Fix $i \in I .$ choose $\begin{array} { r } { 0 < \eta _ { i } \le \operatorname* { i n f } _ { \theta \in \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } } s _ { i } f _ { i } ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \theta ; \theta ) } \end{array}$ , and write

$$
y _ { n } : = s _ { i } ( v _ { i } - \theta _ { n , i } ) , \qquad X _ { n } : = s _ { i } \{ f _ { i } ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) + M _ { n + 1 , i } ^ { \prime } \} .
$$

Thus $0 \leq y _ { n } \leq u _ { i } - \ell _ { i }$ , and coordinatewise box projection gives the exact recursion

$$
y _ { n + 1 } = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ u _ { i } - \ell _ { i } , [ y _ { n } - \beta _ { n } X _ { n } ] _ { + } \} .\tag{S.7.6}
$$

The standing boundedness assumptions give a deterministic constant $D < \infty$ with $| X _ { n } | \leq D$ for every n.

We next obtain a positive conditional mean over a fixed number of steps from the averaged condition (8.10). Let $\Pi _ { \mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta } }$ denote Euclidean projection onto $\mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta }$ , and define

$$
A _ { i } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { y \in \mathcal { V } , \zeta \in \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } } | f _ { i } ( y , x ^ { \star } ( \zeta ) , \zeta ) | , \qquad C _ { i } : = L _ { \mu } A _ { i } ,
$$

$$
g _ { i } ( y , \theta ) : = s _ { i } f _ { i } ( y , x ^ { \star } ( \Pi _ { \mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta } } \theta ) , \Pi _ { \mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta } } \theta ) + C _ { i } \operatorname { d i s t } ( \theta , \mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta } ) .
$$

These functions are bounded and Lipschitz: the maps $x ^ { \star } , \Pi \varepsilon _ { \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } }$ , and the distance function dist $( \cdot , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } )$ are Lipschit $^ { \mathrm { ~ \tiny ~ Z ~ , ~ } }$ and the arguments of $f$ remain in a fixed bounded set. The stationary-distribution bound in Lemma 3.1 gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } { \displaystyle \bar { g } _ { i } ( \theta ) : = \sum _ { y } \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) g _ { i } ( y , \theta ) } & { } & \\ & { } & { \qquad \quad \geqslant s _ { i } f _ { i } ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \Pi _ { \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } \mathrm { e } } \theta ; \Pi _ { \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } \mathrm { e } } \theta ) - L _ { \mu } A _ { i } \mathrm { d i s t } ( \theta , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } ) + C _ { i } \mathrm { d i s t } ( \theta , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } ) \geq \eta _ { i } , \qquad \theta \in \Theta . } \end{array}
$$

The added term makes this lower bound valid throughout the box. It vanishes as the iterates approach the face.

Put

$$
X _ { n } ^ { 0 } : = g _ { i } ( Y _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) + s _ { i } M _ { n + 1 , i } ^ { \prime } .
$$

For the bounded-set Lipschitz constant $L _ { f }$ from the standing assumptions,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { | X _ { n } - X _ { n } ^ { 0 } | \leq L _ { f } \left. x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \right. + \{ L _ { f } ( 1 + L _ { f p } ) + C _ { i } \} \operatorname { d i s t } ( \theta _ { n } , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } ) \longrightarrow 0 \quad \mathrm { a l m o s t ~ s u r e l y . ~ ( S . 7 . 7 ) } } \end{array}
$$

The variables $X _ { n } ^ { 0 }$ are uniformly bounded

A conditional exponential estimate. The same finite-state Poisson argument as in Lemma S.10.1, applied to the bounded Lipschitz scalar observable $g _ { i } .$ gives solutions

$$
u _ { i } ^ { ( \theta ) } - P ^ { ( \theta ) } u _ { i } ^ { ( \theta ) } = g _ { i } ( \cdot , \theta ) - \bar { g } _ { i } ( \theta )
$$

with a uniform bound $U _ { i }$ and a uniform Lipschitz constant $L _ { u , i }$ in $\theta .$ The standing bounds and nonexpansiveness of projection give $\lVert \theta _ { n + 1 } - \theta _ { n } \rVert \leq A _ { \theta } \beta _ { n }$ . Telescoping the Poisson equation over $k , \ldots , k + L - 1$ , and taking conditional expectations, therefore gives

$$
\mathbb { E } \left[ \sum _ { j = k } ^ { k + L - 1 } X _ { j } ^ { 0 } \Bigg | \mathcal { F } _ { k } \right] \ge L \eta _ { i } - 2 U _ { i } - L _ { u , i } A _ { \theta } L \beta _ { k } .\tag{S.7.8}
$$

Indeed, the Poisson martingale and the original martingale increments have zero conditional mean. The two endpoint terms contribute at most $2 U _ { i } .$ and changing the Poisson parameter between consecutive indices contributes at most $L _ { u , i } \sum _ { i = k } ^ { k + L - 2 } \| \theta _ { j + 1 } - \theta _ { j } \|$

Choose a positive integer $L$ such that $2 U _ { i } \ ' \le \eta _ { i } L / 4$ . For all sufficiently large deterministic $k ,$ the last term in (S.7.8) is at most $\eta _ { i } L / 4$ , and hence

$$
\mathbb { E } \left[ \sum _ { j = k } ^ { k + L - 1 } X _ { j } ^ { 0 } \Bigg | \mathcal { F } _ { k } \right] \geq \eta _ { i } L / 2 .
$$

Set $W _ { n } : = X _ { n } ^ { 0 } - \eta _ { i } / 4$ , and let $H _ { i }$ be a deterministic bound on $| W _ { n } |$ . For sufficiently small $\lambda > 0$ Taylor's inequality gives, uniformly over all such $k ,$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { E } \left[ \exp \left\{ - \lambda \displaystyle \sum _ { j = k } ^ { k + L - 1 } W _ { j } \right\} \bigg | \mathcal { F } _ { k } \right] } \\ & { \qquad \leq 1 - \lambda \eta _ { i } L / 4 + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \lambda ^ { 2 } L ^ { 2 } H _ { i } ^ { 2 } e ^ { \lambda L H _ { i } } \leq \rho < 1 . } \end{array}
$$

Iterating over consecutive full blocks and bounding the last incomplete block by $( L - 1 ) H _ { i }$ yields constants $C , c > 0$ such that

$$
\mathbb { E } \left[ \exp \left\{ - \lambda \sum _ { j = k } ^ { n - 1 } W _ { j } \right\} \bigg | \mathcal { F } _ { k } \right] \leq C e ^ { - c ( n - k ) } , \qquad n > k ,\tag{S.7.9}
$$

whenever k exceeds a fixed deterministic index.

Rescaling the endpoint distance. Let $t _ { n } = N _ { 0 } + n , \ z _ { n } = y _ { n } / \beta _ { n }$ , and $r _ { n } = \beta _ { n } / \beta _ { n + 1 }$ . Both schedules satisfy $r _ { n } - 1 \leq 1 / t _ { n }$ . Equation (S.7.6) implies

$$
z _ { n + 1 } \leq [ r _ { n } z _ { n } - r _ { n } X _ { n } ] _ { + } \leq [ z _ { n } - X _ { n } + e _ { n } ] _ { + } ,
$$

where

$$
0 \leq e _ { n } : = ( r _ { n } - 1 ) ( z _ { n } + D ) \leq { \frac { y _ { n } } { t _ { n } \beta _ { n } } } + D / t _ { n } \longrightarrow 0 \quad { \mathrm { a l m o s t ~ s u r e l y } } .
$$

Here $y _ { n } \to 0$ and $1 / ( t _ { n } \beta _ { n } )$ is bounded: it equals $t _ { n } ^ { \mathsf { b - 1 } }$ for the power schedule and $1 / B$ for the second schedule. Together with (S.7.7), this proves that, almost surely, for every sufficiently large n,

$$
z _ { n + 1 } \leq [ z _ { n } - W _ { n } ] _ { + } .\tag{S.7.10}
$$

To handle this random starting index, fix a deterministic N beyond the starting index in (S.7.9), and define

$$
Q _ { N } = z _ { N } , \qquad Q _ { n + 1 } = [ Q _ { n } - W _ { n } ] _ { + } , \quad n \geq N .
$$

On the event that (S.7.10) holds for every $n \geq N$ , monotonicity gives $z _ { n } \leq Q _ { n }$ . The union of these events over deterministic N has probability one. Direct iteration of the last recursion gives

$$
Q _ { n } = \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ z _ { N } - \sum _ { j = N } ^ { n - 1 } W _ { j } , \operatorname* { m a x } _ { N < k \leq n } \left( - \sum _ { j = k } ^ { n - 1 } W _ { j } \right) \right\} .
$$

Since $z _ { N } \le ( u _ { i } - \ell _ { i } ) / \beta _ { N }$ deterministically, Markov's inequality, (S.7.9), and a union bound over the suffixes give, for every $u > 0$ 2

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \mathbb { P } ( Q _ { n } > u ) \leq C e ^ { - \lambda u + \lambda ( u _ { i } - \ell _ { i } ) / \beta _ { N } } e ^ { - c ( n - N ) } + C e ^ { - \lambda u } \sum _ { m \geq 0 } e ^ { - c m } } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad \leq C _ { N } e ^ { - \lambda u } . } \end{array}
$$

Take $u = K \log ( N _ { 0 } + n )$ , with $\lambda K > 2$ . Borel-Cantelli yields $Q _ { n } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \log n )$ for every fixed $N .$ Intersecting these probability-one events over the countable set of N, and using eventual domination, gives

$$
y _ { n } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \beta _ { n } \log n ) .
$$

There are finitely many constrained coordinates, and dist $\begin{array} { r } { ( \theta _ { n } , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } ) ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { i \in I } y _ { n , i } ^ { 2 } } \end{array}$ . This proves the distance bound. Lipschitz continuity of J and $J = 0$ on $\mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta }$ imply $J ( \theta _ { n } ) \leq L _ { J } \mathrm { d i s t } ( \theta _ { n } , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } )$ proving (8.11). If I is empty, this conclusion is immediate because $\mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta } = \Theta$

The fast rate under a common target. Suppose $x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) = x ^ { \dagger }$ on $\mathcal { E Q } _ { \Theta }$ . The fixed-point identity, contraction, and Lipschitz continuity of $x ^ { \star }$ give

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ^ { \dagger } ; \theta ) - x ^ { \dagger } \right\| \leq ( 1 + \gamma ) \left\| x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) - x ^ { \dagger } \right\| \leq ( 1 + \gamma ) L _ { f p } \mathrm { d i s t } ( \theta , \mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } ) . } \end{array}\tag{S.7.11}
$$

Return to the fixed fast-time intervals (S.7.1) and their averaged recursions. One averaged step contracts the distance to $x ^ { \dagger }$ by $1 - q \alpha _ { n }$ and adds at most $\alpha _ { n } ( 1 + \gamma ) L _ { f p } \mathrm { d i s t } ( \theta _ { n } , { \mathcal { E } } { \mathcal { Q } } _ { \Theta } )$ . The sum of the resulting contraction weights is at most $1 / q$ . Combining this with (S.7.3) and the face bound already proved yields

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \left\| x _ { k _ { j + 1 } } - x ^ { \dagger } \right\| \leq e ^ { - q T _ { x } } \left\| x _ { k _ { j } } - x ^ { \dagger } \right\| } \\ & { \qquad + O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Bigl ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { k _ { j } } \log t _ { k _ { j } } } + \beta _ { k _ { j } } \log t _ { k _ { j } } \Bigr ) . } \end{array}
$$

The deterministic envelope again has consecutive ratio tending to one. Geometric comparison and the within-interval bound therefore give

$$
\left\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \dagger } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( { \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log t _ { n } } } + \beta _ { n } \log t _ { n } \right) .
$$

For both schedules, $\beta _ { n }$ log $t _ { n } = o ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log t _ { n } } )$ . Moreover,

$$
\left\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \right\| \leq \left\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \dagger } \right\| + L _ { f p } \operatorname { d i s t } ( \theta _ { n } , { \mathcal { E } } { \mathcal { Q } } _ { \Theta } ) .
$$

This proves (8.12). The two stated polynomial exponents follow by substituting the corresponding schedules. No lower bound on A relative to $q$ is needed: each fixed fast-time interval has a fixed contraction factor, and its forcing envelope has consecutive ratio tending to one. □

## S.7.7 Proof of Proposition S.3.1

Proof. Repeat the proof of Theorem 7.1 with the fast component absent. The term corresponding to $F _ { m } ^ { x }$ is absent.

For $\vartheta , \theta ^ { \prime } \in \Theta$ , define the two-parameter one-time-scale averaged field

$$
g ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \vartheta ; \theta ^ { \prime } ) : = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ^ { \prime } ) } ( y ) g ( y , \vartheta ) .
$$

Thus

$$
g ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \theta ; \theta ) = \bar { g } ( \theta ) .
$$

For $\tau \geq 0$ , let $z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( \cdot )$ solve

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \dot { z } _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( s ) = \Pi _ { \Theta } \Big ( z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( s ) , g ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } \big ( z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( s ) ; z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( s ) \big ) \Big ) } \\ & { \qquad = \Pi _ { \Theta } \Big ( z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( s ) , \bar { g } \big ( z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( s ) \big ) \Big ) , \qquad z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( 0 ) = \theta ^ { \circ } ( \tau ) . } \end{array}
$$

Equivalently,

$$
z _ { g } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( s ) = { \sf S } _ { s } ^ { ( 1 ) } \left( \theta ^ { \circ } ( \tau ) \right) .
$$

Let $u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta ) }$ be the hitting-time-normalized Poisson solution of

$$
u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) - \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y , j ) u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( j ) = g ( y , \theta ) - \bar { g } ( \theta ) , \qquad y \in \mathcal { Y } ,
$$

and define

$$
M _ { n + 1 } ^ { g } : = u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) - \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { V } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } , j ) u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( j ) .
$$

Then $\{ M _ { n + 1 } ^ { g } \}$ is a martingale-difference sequence and

$$
\begin{array} { r } { g ( Y _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - \bar { g } ( \theta _ { n } ) = u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } ) - u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) + M _ { n + 1 } ^ { g } . } \end{array}
$$

The one-time-scale analogues of the standing bounded-noise and uniform hitting-time assumptions, together with compactness of Θ, give constants $C _ { N } , C _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) } , C _ { L } ^ { ( 1 ) } < \infty$ such that

$$
\begin{array} { r l r l } & { \| N _ { n + 1 } \| \le C _ { N } } & { \mathrm { a l m o s t ~ s u r e l y ~ f o r ~ e v e r y ~ } n \ge 1 , \quad } & { C _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) } : = \displaystyle \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta , y \in \mathcal { Y } } \left\| u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) \right\| < \infty , } \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \left\| u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) - u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta ^ { \prime } ) } ( y ) \right\| \le C _ { L } ^ { ( 1 ) } \left\| \theta - \theta ^ { \prime } \right\| , \qquad \theta , \theta ^ { \prime } \in \Theta .
$$

Let

$$
A _ { g } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { y \in \mathcal { V } , \theta \in \Theta } \| g ( y , \theta ) \| + C _ { N } < \infty .
$$

Because $\theta _ { r } \in \Theta$ and Euclidean projection is nonexpansive,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| \theta _ { r + 1 } - \theta _ { r } \| \leq A _ { g } \beta _ { r } \quad \mathrm { a l m o s t ~ s u r e l y } , \qquad r \geq 1 . } \end{array}
$$

For $m \geq 1$ and $1 \leq k \leq K _ { m }$ , define

$$
\mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { ( 1 ) } : = \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \beta _ { n _ { m } + \ell } \left[ u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) } ( Y _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) - u _ { g } ^ { ( \theta _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ) } ( Y _ { n _ { m } + \ell + 1 } ) \right] .
$$

The summation-by-parts argument used in the proof of Lemma S.11.3 gives

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } _ { 1 \leq k \leq K _ { m } } \left\| \mathcal { R } _ { m , k } ^ { ( 1 ) } \right\| \leq R _ { m } ^ { ( 1 ) } ,
$$

where

$$
R _ { m } ^ { ( 1 ) } : = 3 C _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) } \beta _ { n _ { m } } + C _ { L } ^ { ( 1 ) } A _ { g } D _ { m } = O _ { T } ( \beta _ { n _ { m } } ) .
$$

Hence, with

$$
C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta , 1 ) } : = C _ { N } + 2 C _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) } ,
$$

we have, for every $n \geq 1$ ，

$$
\lVert N _ { n + 1 } + M _ { n + 1 } ^ { g } \rVert \leq C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta , 1 ) } \quad \mathrm { a l m o s t ~ s u r e l y } .
$$

For $m \geq 1$ and $\delta > 0$ , define the one-time-scale good event

$$
\mathcal { G } _ { \mathrm { o n e } } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta ) : = \left\{ \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 1 \leq k \leq K _ { m } } \left\| \sum _ { \ell = 0 } ^ { k - 1 } \beta _ { n _ { m } + \ell } ( N _ { n _ { m } + \ell + 1 } + M _ { n _ { m } + \ell + 1 } ^ { g } ) \right\| < \delta \right\} .
$$

Coordinatewise Azuma-Hoeffding and a union bound give

$$
\mathbb { P } \bigg ( \big [ \mathcal { G } _ { \mathrm { o n e } } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta ) \big ] ^ { c } \bigg ) \le 2 d _ { \theta } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K _ { m } } \exp \left\{ - \frac { \delta ^ { 2 } } { 2 \kappa _ { d _ { \theta } } ^ { 2 } ( C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta , 1 ) } ) ^ { 2 } \{ b ( n _ { m } ) - b ( n _ { m } + k ) \} } \right\} .
$$

Set

$$
\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( m ) : = \kappa _ { d _ { \theta } } C _ { \mathrm { m g } } ^ { ( \theta , 1 ) } \sqrt { 2 D _ { m } L _ { m } ^ { \theta } } .
$$

Since $b ( n _ { m } ) - b ( n _ { m } + k ) \leq D _ { m }$ , the definition of $L _ { m } ^ { \theta }$ implies

$$
\mathbb { P } \Big ( \big [ \mathscr { G } _ { \mathrm { o n e } } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( m ) ) \big ] ^ { c } \Big ) \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } } .
$$

Thus the failure probabilities are summable. These are exactly the substitutions

$$
f ( y , x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) , \theta ) \mapsto g ( y , \theta ) , \qquad f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta ; \theta ^ { \prime } ) \mapsto g ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta ; \theta ^ { \prime } ) , \qquad M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime } \mapsto N _ { n + 1 } , \qquad M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime \prime \prime } \mapsto M _ { n + 1 } ^ { g }
$$

in $( 7 . 6 ) \mathrm { - } ( 7 . 1 1 )$ , with every fast term omitted. The resulting version of Lemma 7.2 and the comparison proof of Theorem 7.1 show that there exist $C _ { T } , C _ { T } ^ { \prime } < \infty$ such that, on $\mathcal { G } _ { \mathrm { o n e } } ^ { ( m ) } ( \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( m ) )$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq r \leq H _ { m } } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) - z _ { g } ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( r ) \right\| \leq C _ { T } \left\{ \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( m ) + R _ { m } ^ { ( 1 ) } + \beta _ { n _ { m } } \right\} } \\ & { \qquad \leq C _ { T } ^ { \prime } \left\{ \delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( m ) + \beta _ { n _ { m } } \right\} . } \end{array}
$$

As before,

$$
D _ { m } = b ( n _ { m } ) - b ( n _ { m + 1 } ) \leq ( T + 1 ) \beta _ { n _ { m } } ,
$$

and Lemmas S.15.4 and S.15.5, in their respective cases, show that the logarithmic union-bound factor is $O ( \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) )$ . Therefore,

$$
\delta _ { \mathrm { d e v } } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( m ) \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - 6 / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

Since $\beta _ { n _ { m } } = ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - 6 }$ is of smaller order, the Borel-Cantelli lemma yields, eventually almost surely,

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq r \leq H _ { m } } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) - z _ { g } ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( r ) \right\| \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - { \mathfrak b } / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

If ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ , the block-contraction argument in the proof of Theorem 8.4, together with Lemma S.15.3, gives the claimed iteration-wise rate. If $\mathfrak { b } \ : = \ : 1$ , applying Lemma S.15.6 with $r = \ell = 1 / 2$ and $\rho = \rho _ { T }$ , and then using the within-block comparison in Lemma S.15.5, gives the three cases stated in Proposition S.3.1. □

## S.7.8 Proof of Proposition S.3.2

Proof. The proof of Proposition S.3.1 gives, eventually almost surely,

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq r \leq H _ { m } } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) - z _ { g } ^ { ( T _ { m } ) } ( r ) \right\| \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - { \mathfrak b } / 2 } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

Set $U _ { m } : = J ( \theta _ { n _ { m } } )$ . Repeating the endpoint and within-block argument in the proof of Theorem 8.2 gives qualitative convergence and, under the power-decrease condition, the eventual recursion

$$
U _ { m + 1 } \leq U _ { m } - \kappa _ { J } U _ { m } ^ { p _ { J } } + C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - { \mathfrak { b } } / { 2 } } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

Suppose first that ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ . By (S.15.5), the perturbation on the right-hand side is bounded by

$$
C ( m + 2 ) ^ { - { \frac { 6 } { 2 ( 1 - 6 ) } } } \{ \log ( m + 2 ) \} ^ { 1 / 2 } .
$$

Applying Lemma S.15.2 with $\zeta = { \mathfrak { b } } / { \{ 2 ( 1 - { \mathfrak { b } } ) \} }$ and $\ell = 1 / 2$ , and then converting block number back to iteration number gives

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \frac { 1 - 6 } { p _ { J } - 1 } } + ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \frac { 6 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 p _ { J } } } \bigg ) .
$$

To optimize the polynomial exponent, consider

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } \left\{ \frac { 1 - 6 } { p _ { J } - 1 } , \frac { 6 } { 2 p _ { J } } \right\} .
$$

The two terms are equal when

$$
{ \mathfrak { b } } = { \frac { 2 p _ { J } } { 3 p _ { J } - 1 } } ,
$$

and their common value is $1 / ( 3 p _ { J } - 1 )$ , proving the final claim.

Suppose now that ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ . By Lemma S.15.5, the perturbation is

$$
O \left( e ^ { - T m / 2 } ( m + 1 ) ^ { 1 / 2 } \right) ,
$$

and hence is $O ( ( m + 2 ) ^ { - Q } )$ for every $Q > 0$

Taking $Q > p _ { J } / ( p _ { J } - 1 )$ and applying Lemma S.15.2 with $\zeta = Q$ and $\ell = 0$ gives

$$
U _ { m } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( ( m + 2 ) ^ { - 1 / ( p _ { J } - 1 ) } \Big ) .
$$

Since $m = T ^ { - 1 } \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) + O _ { T } ( 1 )$ , the within-block comparison in Lemma S.15.5 yields

$$
J ( \theta _ { n } ) = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \Big ( \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { - 1 / ( p _ { J } - 1 ) } \Big ) ,
$$

as claimed.

## S.8 Proofs from Main-Paper Section 9 (Actor-Critic)

We begin by discussing the filtration and sampling conventions used in the proof. For $n \geq 1$ , let

$$
\mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } : = \sigma ( V _ { 1 } , \vartheta _ { 1 } , S _ { 1 } , \{ ( A _ { k } , C _ { k + 1 } , S _ { k + 1 } ) \} _ { 1 \leq k < n } ) ,
$$

the history immediately before $A _ { n }$ is drawn. Then $V _ { n } , \vartheta _ { n } ,$ and $Y _ { n } = S _ { n }$ are ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } }$ -measurable. The filtration is taken before $A _ { n }$ is drawn from the current policy; the bounded one-step cost and finite-valued next state are then observed before the update. We now prove Corollary 9.1.

Proof of Corollary 9.1. For $\vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } } ,$ define

$$
P _ { \vartheta } ( s , s ^ { \prime } ) : = \sum _ { a \in \mathcal { A } } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid s , a ) .
$$

With $c ( s , a )$ as in the finite-MDP description in the main paper, define

$$
c _ { \vartheta } ( s ) : = \sum _ { a \in \mathcal { A } } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) c ( s , a ) .
$$

Take $Y _ { n } = S _ { n }$ , and take ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } }$ before $A _ { n }$ is drawn. Conditional on this history, $A _ { n } \sim \pi _ { \vartheta _ { n } } ( \cdot \mid S _ { n } )$ thereafter, for every $s ^ { \prime } \in \mathcal { S }$

$$
\mathbb { E } [ C _ { n + 1 } \mid { \mathcal F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } , A _ { n } ] = c ( S _ { n } , A _ { n } ) , \qquad \mathbb { P } ( S _ { n + 1 } = s ^ { \prime } \mid { \mathcal F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } , A _ { n } ) = P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid S _ { n } , A _ { n } ) .
$$

Thus the controlled kernel for $Y _ { n }$ is $P ^ { ( \vartheta ) } = P _ { \vartheta }$

For $s \in \mathcal { S } , V \in \mathbb { R } ^ { | S | }$ , and $\vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ set

$$
\bar { \delta } _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V , \vartheta ) : = c _ { \vartheta } ( s ) + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } \sum _ { s ^ { \prime } } P _ { \vartheta } ( s , s ^ { \prime } ) V ( s ^ { \prime } ) - V ( s ) ,
$$

$$
h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V , \vartheta ) : = V + e _ { s } \bar { \delta } _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V , \vartheta ) ,
$$

and

$$
f _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( \boldsymbol { s } , V , \vartheta ) : = - \sum _ { a \in \mathcal { A } } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid \boldsymbol { s } ) e _ { \boldsymbol { s } , a } \left\{ c ( \boldsymbol { s } , a ) + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } \sum _ { \boldsymbol { s } ^ { \prime } } P ( \boldsymbol { s } ^ { \prime } \mid \boldsymbol { s } , a ) V ( \boldsymbol { s } ^ { \prime } ) - V ( \boldsymbol { s } ) \right\} .
$$

Define

$$
M _ { n + 1 } ^ { V } : = e _ { S _ { n } } \left\{ \delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } - \bar { \delta } _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( S _ { n } , V _ { n } , \vartheta _ { n } ) \right\}
$$

and

$$
M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \vartheta } : = - e _ { S _ { n } , A _ { n } } \delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } - f _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( S _ { n } , V _ { n } , \vartheta _ { n } ) .
$$

The conditional cost and transition identities above imply

$$
\mathbb { E } [ \delta _ { n + 1 } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } , A _ { n } ] = c ( S _ { n } , A _ { n } ) + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } \sum _ { s ^ { \prime } \in S } P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid S _ { n } , A _ { n } ) V _ { n } ( s ^ { \prime } ) - V _ { n } ( S _ { n } ) .
$$

Averaging this identity over $A _ { n } \sim \pi _ { \vartheta _ { n } } ( \cdot \mid S _ { n } )$ , using the tower property, gives

$$
{ \mathbb E } [ M _ { n + 1 } ^ { V } \ | \ \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } ] = { \mathbb E } [ M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \vartheta } \ | \ \mathcal { F } _ { n } ^ { \mathrm { M D P } } ] = { \bf 0 } .
$$

Hence (9.1) is precisely (1.1)-(1.2) under the identifications

$$
x _ { n } = V _ { n } , \quad \quad \theta _ { n } = \vartheta _ { n } , \quad \quad Y _ { n } = S _ { n } , \quad \quad h = h _ { \mathrm { a c } } , \quad \quad f = f _ { \mathrm { a c } } .
$$

Let $D _ { \vartheta } : = \mathrm { d i a g } ( ( d _ { \vartheta } ( s ) ) _ { s \in { \cal S } } )$ and define

$$
T _ { \vartheta } V : = c _ { \vartheta } + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } P _ { \vartheta } V .
$$

The averaged fast map is

$$
h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( V ; \vartheta ) = V + D _ { \vartheta } ( T _ { \vartheta } V - V ) ,
$$

whose unique fixed point is $V _ { \vartheta }$ . For all $V , V ^ { \prime } $

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( V ; \vartheta ) - h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( V ^ { \prime } ; \vartheta ) \right\| _ { \infty } \leq \left\{ 1 - ( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } ) d _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } \right\} \left\| V - V ^ { \prime } \right\| _ { \infty } . } \end{array}\tag{S.8.1}
$$

Thus $h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \cdot ; \vartheta )$ is a contraction in $\left\| \cdot \right\| _ { \infty }$ with the common modulus $1 - ( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } ) d _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } < 1$ , uniformly over $\vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ . The critic is bounded: if $B _ { V } : = C _ { \mathrm { m a x } } / ( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } )$ and $\| V _ { n } \| _ { \infty } \leq B _ { V }$ , then its updated coordinate is a convex combination of $V _ { n } ( S _ { n } )$ and $C _ { n + 1 } + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } V _ { n } ( S _ { n + 1 } )$ , both in $[ - B _ { V } , B _ { V } ]$ , since $0 < \alpha _ { n } \leq 1$ . Induction therefore gives

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { n \geq 1 } \left\| V _ { n } \right\| _ { \infty } \leq B _ { V } .
$$

We apply Remark S.5.1 with $\| \cdot \| _ { x } = \| \cdot \| _ { \infty }$ . Thus $\kappa _ { x } = \lambda _ { x } = 1$ , and the fast contraction, local Lipschitz, bounded-set, stability, and martingale bounds are interpreted in this norm; the slow coordinate and its projection remain Euclidean.

We next verify the localized Lipschitz condition directly for the original update maps. Since the softmax map $\vartheta \mapsto \pi _ { \vartheta }$ is continuously differentiable and its Jacobian is continuous, its Jacobian is uniformly bounded on the compact logit box. Moreover, $c _ { \vartheta }$ and $P _ { \vartheta }$ are linear functions of $\pi _ { \vartheta }$ . Hence, by the line-segment form of the mean-value theorem, there exist finite constants $L _ { \pi } , L _ { c } , L _ { P } > 0$ such that, for all $\vartheta , \vartheta ^ { \prime } \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { m a x } _ { s \in S } \| \pi _ { \vartheta } ( \cdot  { \left| \begin{array} { l } { s } \end{array} \right| } s ) - \pi _ { \vartheta ^ { \prime } } ( \cdot  { \left| \begin{array} { l } { s } \end{array} \right. } \| _ { 1 } \leq L _ { \pi } \left\| \vartheta - \vartheta ^ { \prime } \right\| , } \\ { \displaystyle \| c _ { \vartheta } - c _ { \vartheta ^ { \prime } } \| _ { \infty } \leq L _ { c } \left\| \vartheta - \vartheta ^ { \prime } \right\| , } \\ { \displaystyle \| P _ { \vartheta } - P _ { \vartheta ^ { \prime } } \| _ { \infty } \leq L _ { P } \left\| \vartheta - \vartheta ^ { \prime } \right\| . } \end{array}
$$

Recall that $\left\| \cdot \right\| _ { \infty }$ denotes the induced matrix infinity norm, equivalently the maximum absolute row-sum norm. Consequently, when $\| V \| _ { \infty } , \| V ^ { \prime } \| _ { \infty } \leq R$

$$
\left\| P \vartheta V - P \vartheta \prime V ^ { \prime } \right\| _ { \infty } \leq \left\| V - V ^ { \prime } \right\| _ { \infty } + L _ { P } R \left\| \vartheta - \vartheta ^ { \prime } \right\| .
$$

Substitution in the definitions of $h _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ and $f _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ therefore gives finite constants $L _ { h } ( R ) , L _ { f } ( R )$ , independent of s, such that

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } & { } & { \| h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V , \vartheta ) - h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V ^ { \prime } , \vartheta ^ { \prime } ) \| _ { \infty } \leq L _ { h } ( R ) \{ \| V - V ^ { \prime } \| _ { \infty } + \| \vartheta - \vartheta ^ { \prime } \| \} , } \\ & { } & { \| f _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V , \vartheta ) - f _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V ^ { \prime } , \vartheta ^ { \prime } ) \| \leq L _ { f } ( R ) \{ \| V - V ^ { \prime } \| _ { \infty } + \| \vartheta - \vartheta ^ { \prime } \| \} . } \end{array}
$$

Thus Assumption 3.5 holds on every bounded critic set; in particular, the invariant cube contains every actual $V _ { n }$ , while the Bellman resolvent gives sup $_ { \vartheta } \parallel V _ { \vartheta } \parallel _ { \infty } \leq B _ { V }$

On the invariant cube $\| V \| _ { \infty } \leq B _ { V }$ , the definitions give

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| h _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V , \vartheta ) \| _ { \infty } \leq B _ { V } , \qquad \| f _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( s , V , \vartheta ) \| \leq C _ { \mathrm { m a x } } + ( 1 + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } ) B _ { V } = 2 B _ { V } , } \end{array}
$$

uniformly over $s \in S$ and $\vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ . These are the required deterministic bounded-set drift envelopes. The definitions of $M _ { n + 1 } ^ { V }$ and $M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \vartheta }$ , together with $| C _ { n + 1 } | \leq C _ { \mathrm { m a x } }$ , separately give the bounded martingale-noise estimates in Assumption 3.3. The remaining boundedness and martingale hypotheses follow from the invariant cube and the stated controlled-kernel assumptions. To prove (9.2), observe that full support of the softmax policy gives

$$
P _ { \vartheta } ( s , s ^ { \prime } ) > 0 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid s , a ) > 0 \quad { \mathrm { f o r ~ s o m e ~ } } a \in { \mathcal A } .
$$

Thus the strongly connected graph assumed in the main paper is the support graph of every $P _ { \vartheta }$ Hence $P _ { \vartheta }$ is irreducible and $d _ { \vartheta } ( s ) > 0$ for every $s \in { \mathcal { S } }$ and $\vartheta \in \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ . If $\vartheta _ { k }  \vartheta$ , every subsequential limit of $d _ { \vartheta _ { k } }$ is stationary for $P _ { \vartheta }$ , by continuity of $\vartheta \mapsto P _ { \vartheta }$ . Uniqueness of the stationary distribution therefore gives $d _ { \vartheta _ { k } }  d _ { \vartheta }$ . Thus $\vartheta \mapsto \mathrm { m i n } _ { s \in { \mathcal { S } } } d _ { \vartheta } ( s )$ is continuous and strictly positive. Compactness of $\Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ proves (9.2). The separately assumed uniform hitting-time bound provides the uniform Poisson-equation bounds required by the abstract theorem. For the actor calculation, define the reduced cost

$$
K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) : = c ( s , a ) + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } \sum _ { s ^ { \prime } } P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid s , a ) V _ { \vartheta } ( s ^ { \prime } ) - V _ { \vartheta } ( s ) .
$$

It satisfies $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { a } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) = 0 } \end{array}$ . At the fast fixed point, the averaged slow field is

$$
F _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( \vartheta ) = - \sum _ { s , a } q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a ) e _ { s , a } K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) , \qquad q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a ) : = d _ { \vartheta } ( s ) \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) .\tag{S.8.2}
$$

Here $q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a )$ is the stationary activation frequency of coordinate $( s , a )$ . Put

$$
\pi _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } : = \frac { e ^ { - 2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } } { | \boldsymbol { A } | } , \qquad q _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } : = d _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } \pi _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } .
$$

Then $\pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) \geq \pi _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } }$ and $q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a ) \geq q _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } }$

Lyapunov decrease. Let

$$
L _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( \vartheta ) : = \sum _ { s \in \cal S } V _ { \vartheta } ( s ) .
$$

Encode the coordinates blocked by the box projection by setting $\chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) = 0$ when either $\vartheta ( s , a ) = B _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ and $K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) < 0$ , or $\vartheta ( s , a ) = - B _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ and $K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) > 0$ , and let $\chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) = 1$ otherwise. Thus, almost everywhere along the projected actor ODE,

$$
\dot { \vartheta } ( s , a ) = - q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a ) \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) .
$$

Along any absolutely continuous solution of this ODE, differentiating the softmax map gives

$$
\dot { \pi } _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) = \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) \left\{ \dot { \vartheta } ( s , a ) - \sum _ { b } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( b \mid s ) \dot { \vartheta } ( s , b ) \right\} .
$$

Consequently, using $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { a } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) = 0 } \end{array}$ , we obtain

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \sum _ { a } K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) \dot { \pi } _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) = \displaystyle \sum _ { a } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) \dot { \vartheta } ( s , a ) } \\ { \displaystyle = - \sum _ { a } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a ) \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) ^ { 2 } \leq 0 . } \end{array}
$$

Applying Konda and Borkar [18, Lemma 5.4] to the induced policy-space vector field, as in their proof of Lemma 5.10, and summing over s therefore gives

$$
\begin{array} { l l l } { \displaystyle \frac { d } { d t } J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta ( t ) ) = \displaystyle \frac { d } { d t } L _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( \vartheta ( t ) ) } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad \leq - \sum _ { s , a } \pi _ { \vartheta } ( a \mid s ) q _ { \vartheta } ( s , a ) \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad \leq - \pi _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } q _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } \sum _ { s , a } \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}\tag{S.8.3}
$$

Control through the Bellman residual. To express the preceding Lyapunov-decrease estimate in terms of $J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } }$ , we first bound $J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } }$ by the Bellman residual $\| V _ { \vartheta } - T _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } V _ { \vartheta } \| _ { \infty }$ , and then bound that residual by the square root of the active squared-gap sum. The set of policies generated by the logit box is given by

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { P } _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } : = \left\{ \mu \in \Delta ( \mathcal { A } ) : \mu ( a ) \leq e ^ { 2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } \mu ( b ) , \ a , b \in \mathcal { A } \right\} . } \end{array}
$$

This is exactly the softmax image of the logit box. The forward inclusion follows from the logit ratios. Conversely, the ratio constraints force every $\mu ( a ) > 0$ and make the range of $\{ \log \mu ( a ) : a \in$ $\mathcal { A } \}$ at most $2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } }$ . Hence

$$
\eta ( a ) : = \log \mu ( a ) - \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left\{ \operatorname* { m a x } _ { b } \log \mu ( b ) + \operatorname* { m i n } _ { b } \log \mu ( b ) \right\} \in [ - B _ { \mathrm { a c } } , B _ { \mathrm { a c } } ]
$$

and $\begin{array} { r } { e ^ { \eta ( a ) } / \sum _ { b \in \mathcal { A } } e ^ { \eta ( b ) } = \mu ( a ) } \end{array}$ for every $a \in { \mathcal { A } }$ , which proves the reverse inclusion. The restricted Bellman operator is

$$
[ T _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } V ] ( s ) : = \operatorname * { m i n } _ { \mu \in \mathcal P _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } } \sum _ { a } \mu ( a ) \left\{ c ( s , a ) + \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } \sum _ { s ^ { \prime } } P ( s ^ { \prime } \mid s , a ) V ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right\} ,
$$

and $V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star }$ is its fixed point. Fix $s ,$ and for $\eta \in [ - B _ { \mathrm { a c } } , B _ { \mathrm { a c } } ] ^ { | A | }$ define

$$
N _ { s } ( \eta ) : = \sum _ { a } e ^ { \eta ( a ) } K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) , \qquad Z _ { s } ( \eta ) : = \sum _ { a } e ^ { \eta ( a ) } .
$$

Because $N _ { s } ( \vartheta ( s , \cdot ) ) = 0$ , and every coordinate blocked by the projection is already at its beneficial endpoint,

$$
N _ { s } ( \eta ) \ge - ( e ^ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } - e ^ { - B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ) \sum _ { a } \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) | K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) | .
$$

Indeed, in the difference $N _ { s } ( \eta ) - N _ { s } ( \vartheta ( s , \cdot ) )$ , every blocked coordinate contributes nonnegatively, while the sum of the remaining contributions is bounded below by the right-hand side above. Because the softmax image of the logit box is exactly the restricted policy class,

$$
0 \leq V _ { \vartheta } ( s ) - [ T _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } V _ { \vartheta } ] ( s ) = - \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \eta \in [ - B _ { \mathrm { a c } } , B _ { \mathrm { a c } } ] \vert A \vert } \frac { N _ { s } ( \eta ) } { Z _ { s } ( \eta ) } \leq \frac { e ^ { 2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } - 1 } { \vert A \vert } \sum _ { a } \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) \vert K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) \vert .
$$

Indeed, $N _ { s } ( \vartheta ( s , \cdot ) ) = 0$ , so the minimum is nonpositive, which gives the left inequality. The preceding lower bound on $N _ { s } ( \eta )$ , together with $Z _ { s } ( \eta ) \geq | { \mathcal { A } } | e ^ { - B _ { \mathrm { a c } } }$ , gives the right inequality. Since $T _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } }$ is a $\gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } .$ -contraction in the sup norm,

$$
\left\| { V _ { \vartheta } - V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star } } \right\| _ { \infty } \leq \frac { 1 } { 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } } \left\| { V _ { \vartheta } - T _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } V _ { \vartheta } } \right\| _ { \infty } .
$$

Using the componentwise inequality $V _ { \vartheta } \geq V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star }$ , we therefore have

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta ) = \displaystyle \sum _ { s \in { \cal S } } \{ V _ { \vartheta } ( s ) - V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star } ( s ) \} } \\ & { \qquad \leq | { \cal S } | \left\| V _ { \vartheta } - V _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \star } \right\| _ { \infty } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \displaystyle \frac { | { \cal S } | } { 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } } \left\| V _ { \vartheta } - T _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } V _ { \vartheta } \right\| _ { \infty } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \displaystyle \frac { | { \cal S } | \left( e ^ { 2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } - 1 \right) } { | { \cal A } | \left( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } \right) } \displaystyle \operatorname* { m a x } _ { s \in { \cal S } } \displaystyle \sum _ { a } \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) | { \cal K } _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) | . } \end{array}
$$

Finally, Cauchy-Schwarz and $\chi _ { s , a } \in \{ 0 , 1 \}$ bound the preceding maximum by $\sqrt { | { \cal A } | } \{ \sum _ { s , a } \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) ^ { 2 } \} ^ { 1 / 2 }$ Therefore,

$$
J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta ) \leq A _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } \left\{ \sum _ { s , a } \chi _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) K _ { s , a } ( \vartheta ) ^ { 2 } \right\} ^ { 1 / 2 } , \qquad A _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } : = \frac { | { \cal S } | ( e ^ { 2 B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } - 1 ) } { ( 1 - \gamma _ { \mathrm { d i s c } } ) \sqrt { | { \cal A } | } } .\tag{S.8.4}
$$

Combining (S.8.3) and (S.8.4) yields

$$
\frac { d } { d t } J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta ( t ) ) \leq - \kappa _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta ( t ) ) ^ { 2 } \quad \mathrm { f o r ~ a l m o s t ~ e v e r y ~ } t , \qquad \kappa _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } : = \frac { \pi _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } q _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { \mathrm { a c } } } { A _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ^ { 2 } } > 0 .\tag{S.8.5}
$$

The map $t \mapsto J _ { B _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta ( t ) )$ is absolutely continuous.
