Theorem S.12.1. Let K be a nonempty compact convex polyhedron and let $F : K  \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ be Lipschitz. Suppose that $x ^ { \star } \in K$ is an equilibrium of

$$
\dot { x } = \Pi _ { K } ( x , F ( x ) )
$$

and that $F$ is strongly monotone at $x ^ { \star }$ , in the sense of Deinition S.12.1, with constant $\mu _ { \mathrm { s m } } > 0$ Then the projected ODE is well posed and every solution satisfies

$$
\| x ( t ) - x ^ { \star } \| \leq e ^ { - \mu _ { \mathrm { s m } } t } \| x ( 0 ) - x ^ { \star } \| , \qquad t \geq 0 .
$$

In particular, $x ^ { \star }$ is the unique globally exponentially stable equilibrium.

Proof. Set

$$
F _ { \mathrm { e x t } } ( z ) : = F ( \mathrm { p r o j } _ { K } z ) , \qquad z \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } .
$$

Since Euclidean projection is nonexpansive, $F _ { \mathrm { e x t } }$ is globally Lipschitz; since K is compact, it is also bounded. Thus Assumption 1 of Dupuis and Nagurney [16] holds. Taking $x _ { 0 } ^ { n } = x _ { 0 } , b _ { i } ^ { n } = F _ { \mathrm { e x t } }$ and $a _ { i } ^ { n } = 1 / n$ in their Theorem 3 gives existence, while their Theorem 2 gives uniqueness

Since $x ^ { \star }$ is an equilibrium, it is a constant solution. Repeating the comparison calculation leading to equation (15) in the proof of Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem $2 ]$ , with their drift $b = F _ { \mathrm { e x t } }$ and with the two solutions $x ( \cdot )$ and $x ^ { \star }$ , gives, for almost every $t \geq 0$

$$
\frac { 1 } { 2 } \frac { \mathrm { d } } { \mathrm { d } t } \left\| x ( t ) - x ^ { \star } \right\| ^ { 2 } \leq \langle F ( x ( t ) ) - F ( x ^ { \star } ) , x ( t ) - x ^ { \star } \rangle \leq - \mu _ { \operatorname { s m } } \left\| x ( t ) - x ^ { \star } \right\| ^ { 2 } .
$$

Gronwall's inequality gives

$$
\| x ( t ) - x ^ { \star } \| \leq e ^ { - \mu _ { \mathrm { s m } } t } \| x ( 0 ) - x ^ { \star } \| , \qquad t \geq 0 .
$$

Applying this estimate to any constant equilibrium trajectory also proves that $x ^ { \star }$ is the unique equilibrium. □

Corollary S.12.1 (Lyapunov and one-block consequences). Under the hypotheses of Theorem S.12.1, let $\mathsf { S } _ { t } ^ { F }$ denote the projected-ODE flow and define

$$
J ( x ) : = \| x - x ^ { \star } \| .
$$

Then J is a 1-Lipschitz Lyapunov function and, for every $T > 0$

$$
J ( \mathsf { S } _ { T } ^ { F } ( x ) ) \le \rho _ { T } J ( x ) , \qquad \rho _ { T } : = e ^ { - \mu _ { \mathrm { s m } } T } \in ( 0 , 1 ) , \qquad x \in K .
$$

Thus the uniform one-block Lyapunov contraction condition holds for every $T > 0$

Lemma S.12.1. Let K be a nonempty compact convex polyhedron. Consider the projected ODE

$$
\dot { \boldsymbol { x } } = \Pi _ { K } \big ( \boldsymbol { x } , \mathcal { T } _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) - \boldsymbol { x } \big ) ,
$$

where $\mathcal { T } _ { \mathrm { c t r } } : K  K$ is a contraction with contraction factor $\gamma \in [ 0 , 1 )$ . $H x ^ { \star }$ is an equilibrium, then $\mathcal { T } _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) = x ^ { \star }$ , the vector field

$$
F _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ) : = \mathcal { T } _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ) - x
$$

is strongly monotone at $x ^ { \star }$ , and Corollary S.12.1 applies.

Proof. Since ${ \mathcal T } _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) \in K$ , the vector

$$
\mathcal { T } _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) - x ^ { \star }
$$

belongs to the tangent cone of K at $x ^ { \star }$ . Because $x ^ { \star }$ is an equilibrium,

$$
\Pi _ { K } \big ( x ^ { \star } , { \mathcal { T } } _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) - x ^ { \star } \big ) = 0 .
$$

It follows that

$$
T _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) = x ^ { \star } .
$$

For every $x \in K$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \langle F _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ) - F _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) , x - x ^ { \star } \rangle = \langle \mathcal T _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ) - \mathcal T _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) , x - x ^ { \star } \rangle - \| x - x ^ { \star } \| ^ { 2 } } \\ & { \qquad \leq \| \mathcal T _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ) - \mathcal T _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ( x ^ { \star } ) \| \| x - x ^ { \star } \| - \| x - x ^ { \star } \| ^ { 2 } } \\ & { \qquad \leq - ( 1 - \gamma ) \| x - x ^ { \star } \| ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

Thus $F _ { \mathrm { c t r } }$ is strongly monotone at $x ^ { \star }$ , with constant $1 - \gamma$ . The conclusion now follows from Corollary S.12.1. □

## S.13 Skorokhod problem

We recall the Skorokhod problem and the Lipschitz property of its solution map. This property is used to convert bounds between unconstrained driving paths into bounds between the corresponding projected paths.

Let $D ( [ 0 , \infty ) ; \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ denote the space of right-continuous functions with left limits from $[ 0 , \infty )$ to $\mathbb { R } ^ { d } . \mathrm { ~ I f ~ } v : [ 0 , \infty )  \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ , let $| \boldsymbol { v } | ( T )$ denote its total variation on $[ 0 , T ]$ . Let $D _ { B V } ( [ 0 , \infty ) ; \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ be the set of all $v \in D ( [ 0 , \infty ) ; \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ such that v has bounded variation on every compact interval. Let ∂Θ denote the boundary of Θ. For $x \in \partial \Theta$ , define the set of unit inward normals

$$
n _ { \Theta } ( x ) : = \left\{ \nu \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } : \left\| \nu \right\| = 1 , \ \langle \nu , y - x \rangle \geq 0 \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ } y \in \Theta \right\} .
$$

For $x \in$ int(Θ), set $n _ { \Theta } ( x ) : = \mathcal { O }$

Definition S.13.1 (Skorokhod problem). Let $v \in D ( [ 0 , \infty ) ; \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ with $\upsilon ( 0 ) \in \Theta$ be given. A pair $\left( \phi , \eta _ { s k } \right)$ solves the Skorokhod problem with respect to Θ if, for all $t \geq 0$

(i) $\phi ( t ) = v ( t ) + \eta _ { s k } ( t ) \ \mathrm { a n d } \ \phi ( 0 ) = v ( 0 ) ;$

(ii) $\phi ( t ) \in \Theta ;$

(iii) $| \eta _ { s k } | ( t ) < \infty ;$

(iv) $\begin{array} { r } { | \eta _ { s k } | ( t ) = \int _ { ( 0 , t ] } \mathbf { 1 } _ { \partial \Theta } \bigl ( \phi ( s ) \bigr ) d | \eta _ { s k } | ( s ) ; } \end{array}$

(v) there exists a measurable function $\nu : [ 0 , \infty ) \to \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ such that $\nu ( s ) \in n _ { \Theta } ( \phi ( s ) )$ for $d | \eta _ { s k } | \mathrm { - a . e }$ s, and

$$
\eta _ { s k } ( t ) = \int _ { ( 0 , t ] } \nu ( s ) d | \eta _ { s k } | ( s ) .
$$

The path v is called the unconstrained driving path, while $\phi$ is the projected path. When the solution is unique, write $\Gamma ( v ) : = \phi$ for its projected component. The following is Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 1] in the present notation.

Theorem S.13.1 (Lipschitz property of the Skorokhod map). Let $\Theta \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ be a convex polyhedron. For every $v \in D _ { B V } ( [ 0 , \infty ) ; \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ with $\upsilon ( 0 ) \in \Theta$ , there exists a unique solution to the Skorokhod problem. Moreover, the corresponding map Γ is Lipschitz, i.e., there exists a constant $L _ { \mathrm { S k } } > 0$ such that, for every $T < \infty$ and every $v _ { 1 } , v _ { 2 } \in D _ { B V } ( [ 0 , \infty ) ; \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ with ${ \boldsymbol { v } } _ { 1 } ( 0 ) , { \boldsymbol { v } } _ { 2 } ( 0 ) \in \Theta$

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq T } \| \Gamma ( v _ { 1 } ) ( t ) - \Gamma ( v _ { 2 } ) ( t ) \| \leq L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq T } \| v _ { 1 } ( t ) - v _ { 2 } ( t ) \| .
$$

Whenever a block step path $q ^ { ( m ) , \circ }$ , defined in Section 4, is used as an argument of Γ, extend it constantly beyond the block endpoint:

$$
q ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( r ) : = q _ { K _ { m } } ^ { ( m ) } , \qquad r \geq H _ { m } .
$$

This convention does not affect any estimate on $[ 0 , H _ { m } ]$

Lemma S.13.1 (Step-path Skorokhod comparison on a block). Fix $m \geq 0$ . Let

$$
\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } , \quad \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } , \quad v ^ { ( m ) , \circ } , \quad \tilde { v } ^ { ( m ) , \circ }
$$

be the right-continuous block step embeddings of the original slow sequence and of the sequences in $( 7 . 1 ) - ( 7 . 3 )$ , using the convention of Section $\it 4 .$ Then

$$
\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } = \Gamma \big ( \boldsymbol { v } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } \big ) , \qquad \tilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } = \Gamma \big ( \tilde { \boldsymbol { v } } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } \big ) .
$$

The Lipschitz constant $L _ { \mathrm { S k } }$ depends only on the geometry of Θ, not on m or the driving paths. Consequently, for every $0 \leq n \leq K _ { m }$

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| \leq L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| v _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \tilde { v } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| .
$$

Proof. For the first recursion, set $\eta _ { 0 } ^ { ( m ) } : = 0$ and $\eta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } : = \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - v _ { k } ^ { ( m ) }$ . For $0 \leq k < K _ { m }$ , write

$$
\Delta v _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } : = v _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } - v _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } , \qquad \Delta \eta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } : = \eta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } - \eta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } .
$$

Then, at the grid time $t ^ { ( m ) } ( k + 1 )$

$$
\theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \left( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } + \Delta v _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right) , \qquad \Delta \eta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } - \left( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } + \Delta v _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right) .
$$

Because Θ is closed and convex and $\theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } ( \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } + \Delta v _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } )$ , the first-order optimality condition for the Euclidean projection yields, for every $y \in \Theta$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left. { \theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { \left( m \right) } - \left( \theta _ { k } ^ { \left( m \right) } + \Delta v _ { k + 1 } ^ { \left( m \right) } \right) , y - \theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { \left( m \right) } } \right. \ge 0 . } \end{array}
$$

The first argument is $\Delta \eta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) }$ . Hence

$$
\left. \Delta \eta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } , y - \theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right. \geq 0 , \qquad y \in \Theta .
$$

Thus every nonzero regulator jump is an inward normal at $\theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) }$ , which must then lie on ∂Θ; its normalized direction belongs to $n _ { \Theta } ( \theta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } )$ . Because the block has only finitely many jumps and

both step embeddings are constant between grid points, the step regulator $\eta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } : = \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } - v ^ { ( m ) , \circ }$ has finite variation supported on times at which $\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } \in \partial \Theta$ . Hence

$$
\nu _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } : = \frac { \Delta \eta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } } { \left\| \Delta \eta _ { k + 1 } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| }
$$

at each nonzero jump defines a measurable direction process with

$$
\eta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) = \int _ { ( 0 , t ] } \nu ^ { ( m ) } ( s ) \mathrm { d } \vert \eta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } \vert ( s ) .
$$

Consequently,

$$
\left( \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } , \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } - v ^ { ( m ) , \circ } \right)
$$

solves the càdlàg Skorokhod problem driven by $v ^ { ( m ) , \circ }$ , and uniqueness gives $\theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } = \Gamma ( v ^ { ( m ) , \circ } )$ . The same argument yields $\widetilde { \theta } ^ { { ( m ) } , \circ } = \Gamma ( \widetilde { v } ^ { { ( m ) } , \circ } )$ . Applying Theorem S.13.1 on $[ 0 , t ^ { ( m ) } ( n ) ]$ therefore gives

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| \theta _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \widetilde { \theta } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| = \displaystyle \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq t ^ { ( m ) } ( n ) } \left\| \theta ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - \widetilde { \theta } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) \right\| } \\ { \displaystyle \leq L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \displaystyle \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq t ^ { ( m ) } ( n ) } \left\| v ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) - \widetilde { v } ^ { ( m ) , \circ } ( t ) \right\| } \\ { \displaystyle = L _ { \mathrm { S k } } \displaystyle \operatorname* { m a x } _ { 0 \leq k \leq n } \left\| v _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } - \widetilde { v } _ { k } ^ { ( m ) } \right\| . } \end{array}
$$

Lemma S.13.2 (Unconstrained path for the projected ODE). Fix $\tau \in \mathbb { R } _ { + }$ There exists an absolutely continuous path $w ^ { ( \tau ) } : [ 0 , \infty ) \to \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { \theta } }$ satisfying

$$
w ^ { ( \tau ) } ( 0 ) = z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( 0 ) = \theta ^ { \circ } ( \tau )
$$

and

$$
\dot { w } ^ { ( \tau ) } ( t ) = f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( t ) ; z ^ { ( \tau ) } ( t ) )
$$

for Lebesgue-a.e. $t \geq 0$ , such that

$$
z ^ { ( \tau ) } = \Gamma ( w ^ { ( \tau ) } ) .
$$

Proof. This follows from the Skorokhod representation of projected dynamical systems in Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 2]. □

## S.14 Step-size-related results

Lemma S.14.1. Let

$$
\alpha _ { n } = ( n + N _ { 0 } ) ^ { - { \mathfrak a } } , \qquad 0 < { \mathfrak a } < 1 ,
$$

and deine

$$
\chi ( m , n ) : = \left\{ \prod _ { \ell = m } ^ { n } ( 1 - \alpha _ { \ell } ) , ~ m \leq n , \right.
$$

Suppose that

$$
\left( N _ { 0 } + 1 \right) ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak { a } } } \geq 4 .
$$

Then, for every $n \geq 1$

$$
\sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } ,
$$

and

$$
\sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ^ { 2 } ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } .
$$

Proof. Let

$$
S _ { n } : = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) .
$$

Then $S _ { 1 } = \alpha _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } \le 2 \alpha _ { 1 }$ and, for $n \geq 2$

$$
S _ { n } = \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } + ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) S _ { n - 1 } .
$$

Assume inductively that $S _ { n - 1 } \leq 2 \alpha _ { n - 1 }$ and set $s _ { n } : = n + N _ { 0 }$ . Since $0 < \mathfrak { a } < 1$

$$
\frac { \alpha _ { n - 1 } } { \alpha _ { n } } = \left( \frac { s _ { n } } { s _ { n } - 1 } \right) ^ { \mathfrak { a } } \leq \frac { s _ { n } } { s _ { n } - 1 } \leq 1 + \frac { 2 } { s _ { n } } .
$$

Therefore,

$$
S _ { n } \leq \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } + 2 ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) \alpha _ { n } \left( 1 + \frac { 2 } { s _ { n } } \right)
$$

$$
\leq 2 \alpha _ { n } - \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } + \frac { 4 \alpha _ { n } } { s _ { n } } \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } ,
$$

where the last inequality uses $4 / s _ { n } \le s _ { n } ^ { - \mathfrak { a } } = \alpha _ { n }$ , which follows from $( N _ { 0 } + 1 ) ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak { a } } } \geq 4$ . This proves the first bound. Because $0 \leq \chi ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 1$ ，

$$
\sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ^ { 2 } ( k + 1 , n ) \leq \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } ,
$$

proving the second.

Lemma S.14.2. For $\alpha _ { n } = ( n + N _ { 0 } ) ^ { - { \mathfrak a } }$ and $0 < \mathfrak { a } < 1$ , for every $n \geq 2$

$$
\sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { n } | \alpha _ { k } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) - \alpha _ { k - 1 } \chi ( k , n ) | \leq \alpha _ { n } .
$$

Consequently,

$$
\left\| \sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { n } \{ \alpha _ { k } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) - \alpha _ { k - 1 } \chi ( k , n ) \} u _ { h } ^ { ( x _ { k - 1 } , \theta _ { k - 1 } ) } ( Y _ { k } ) \right\| \leq 2 \mathfrak { c } _ { 5 } \mathsf { B } _ { 6 } \alpha _ { n } ,
$$

almost surely.

Proof. Let $A _ { k } : = \alpha _ { k } \chi ( k + 1 , n )$ . Since $\chi ( k , n ) = ( 1 - \alpha _ { k } ) \chi ( k + 1 , n )$

$$
A _ { k } - A _ { k - 1 } = \{ \alpha _ { k } - \alpha _ { k - 1 } ( 1 - \alpha _ { k } ) \} \chi ( k + 1 , n ) .
$$

With $s = k + N _ { 0 }$ , the inequality $\alpha _ { k } \geq \alpha _ { k - 1 } ( 1 - \alpha _ { k } )$ is equivalent to

$$
\left( \frac { s - 1 } { s } \right) ^ { \mathfrak { a } } \geq 1 - s ^ { - { \mathfrak { a } } } ,
$$

which follows from subadditivity of $x \mapsto x ^ { \mathfrak { a } }$ on $\mathbb { R } _ { + }$ . Thus $A _ { k } - A _ { k - 1 } \geq 0$ , and

$$
\sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { n } | A _ { k } - A _ { k - 1 } | = A _ { n } - A _ { 1 } \leq \alpha _ { n } .
$$

The final bound follows from part (ii) of Lemma S.10.1.

## S.15 Miscellaneous results

Theorem S.15.1 (Azuma-Hoeffding). Suppose $\{ X _ { k } \} _ { k \ge 0 }$ is a scalar martingale and $\{ c _ { k } \} _ { k \ge 1 }$ is a deterministic nonnegative sequence such that $| X _ { k } - X _ { k - 1 } | \le c _ { k }$ almost surely. Then, for every $N \in  { \mathbb { N } }$ and every $\epsilon > 0$ 2

$$
\mathbb { P } ( | X _ { N } - X _ { 0 } | \ge \epsilon ) \le 2 \exp \left( - \frac { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } { 2 \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { N } c _ { k } ^ { 2 } } \right) .
$$

Lemma S.15.1 (Well-posedness of the projected ODE). For every $z _ { 0 } \in \Theta$ , the projected ODE

$$
\dot { z } ( t ) = \Pi _ { \Theta } \left( z ( t ) , f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( z ( t ) ; z ( t ) ) \right) , \qquad z ( 0 ) = z _ { 0 } ,
$$

has a unique solution, and this solution remains in Θ for all $t \geq 0$

Proof. Lemma 3.1 gives $\operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \| x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) \| \leq { \mathfrak { c } } _ { 8 }$ . Hence Assumption 3.5, applied on the ball of radius ${ \mathfrak { c } } _ { 8 } ,$ makes the diagonal field $F ( \theta ) : = f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . ) } } ( \theta ; \theta )$ Lipschitz on Θ. Define $F _ { \mathrm { e x t } } ( z ) : = F ( \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } z )$ Nonexpansiveness of projection makes $F _ { \mathrm { e x t } }$ globally Lipschitz; compactness of Θ makes it bounded, and it agrees with F on Θ. Existence follows from Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 3], while the Skorokhod representation and uniqueness follow from Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 2]. Hence the projected ODE is well posed and its solution remains in Θ □

Lemma S.15.2 (Perturbed power-drift recursion). Let $p > 1 , \kappa > 0 , \zeta > 0 , \ell \geq 0$ , and $C _ { e } \in [ 0 , \infty )$ Let $\{ u _ { m } \}$ and $\{ e _ { m } \}$ be nonnegative sequences such that $u _ { m } \to 0$ and, for all sufficiently large m,

$$
u _ { m + 1 } \leq u _ { m } - \kappa u _ { m } ^ { p } + e _ { m } ,
$$

and

$$
e _ { m } \leq C _ { e } ( m + 2 ) ^ { - \zeta } \{ \log ( m + 2 ) \} ^ { \ell } .
$$

Then there exists $C < \infty$ such that, for all sufficiently large m,

$$
u _ { m } \leq C \left\{ ( m + 2 ) ^ { - 1 / ( p - 1 ) } + ( m + 2 ) ^ { - \zeta / p } \{ \log ( m + 2 ) \} ^ { \ell / p } \right\} .
$$

Proof. Set

$$
w _ { m } : = ( m + 2 ) ^ { - 1 / ( p - 1 ) } , \qquad \eta _ { m } : = ( m + 2 ) ^ { - \zeta / p } \{ \log ( m + 2 ) \} ^ { \ell / p } ,
$$

and $h _ { m } : = w _ { m } + \eta _ { m }$ . These sequences are eventually decreasing. Because

$$
{ \frac { 1 } { p - 1 } } + 1 = { \frac { p } { p - 1 } } ,
$$

the mean-value theorem gives, eventually,

$$
0 \leq w _ { m } - w _ { m + 1 } \leq C _ { w } w _ { m } ^ { p } .
$$

It also gives

$$
0 \leq \eta _ { m } - \eta _ { m + 1 } \leq C _ { \eta } \frac { \eta _ { m } } { m + 2 } .
$$

${ \mathrm { I f ~ } } \zeta \leq p / ( p - 1 )$ , then

$$
\frac { \eta _ { m } } { m + 2 } = { \cal { O } } ( \eta _ { m } ^ { p } ) ,
$$

where the logarithmic factor at equality is harmless because $\ell \geq 0$ . If $\zeta > p / ( p - 1 )$ , then

$$
\frac { \eta _ { m } } { m + 2 } = O ( w _ { m } ^ { p } ) .
$$

Consequently, for some $C _ { h } < \infty$

$$
0 \leq h _ { m } - h _ { m + 1 } \leq C _ { h } ( w _ { m } ^ { p } + \eta _ { m } ^ { p } )\tag{S.15.1}
$$

eventually.

Choose $R > 0$ so that $\kappa p R ^ { p - 1 } \leq 1 / 2$ . Since $u _ { m } \to 0$ and $h _ { m } \to 0$ , choose $A _ { 0 } \geq 1$ and then a sufficiently large index M such that

$$
\kappa A _ { 0 } ^ { p - 1 } \geq 2 C _ { h } , \qquad \kappa A _ { 0 } ^ { p } \geq 2 C _ { e } , \qquad u _ { M } \leq R / 2 , \qquad A _ { 0 } h _ { M } \leq R / 2 .
$$

Set

$$
A : = \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ A _ { 0 } , \frac { u _ { M } } { h _ { M } } \right\} , \qquad v _ { m } : = A h _ { m } .
$$

Then $u _ { M } \leq v _ { M } \leq R / 2$ , and $v _ { m } \leq R / 2$ for every $m \geq M$ . Since $( w _ { m } + \eta _ { m } ) ^ { p } \ge w _ { m } ^ { p } + \eta _ { m } ^ { p }$ , (S.15.1) and the choice of A give

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { v _ { m } - \kappa v _ { m } ^ { p } + e _ { m } \leq A h _ { m } - \kappa A ^ { p } ( w _ { m } ^ { p } + \eta _ { m } ^ { p } ) + C _ { e } \eta _ { m } ^ { p } } \\ & { \qquad \leq A h _ { m } - A C _ { h } ( w _ { m } ^ { p } + \eta _ { m } ^ { p } ) } \\ & { \qquad \leq A h _ { m + 1 } = v _ { m + 1 } . } \end{array}
$$

The map $F ( u ) : = u - \kappa u ^ { p }$ is nondecreasing on $[ 0 , R ]$ . Therefore, if $u _ { m } \leq v _ { m }$ , then

$$
u _ { m + 1 } \leq F ( u _ { m } ) + e _ { m } \leq F ( v _ { m } ) + e _ { m } \leq v _ { m + 1 } .
$$

Induction proves $u _ { m } \leq v _ { m }$ for every $m \geq M$ , which is the claimed bound.

Lemma S.15.3 (Geometric convolution of the blockwise rate envelope). Suppose $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ Fix $\rho \in ( 0 , 1 )$ and $r > 0$ , and set

$$
\varphi _ { m } : = ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) } .
$$

Then, for every $m _ { 0 } \geq 0$ , there exist $m _ { 1 } \geq m _ { 0 }$ and $C _ { \mathrm { g e o } } < \infty$ such that, for every $m \geq m _ { 1 }$ 2

$$
\sum _ { j = m _ { 0 } } ^ { m - 1 } \rho ^ { m - 1 - j } \varphi _ { j } \leq C _ { \mathrm { g e o } } \varphi _ { m } .\tag{S.15.2}
$$

Moreover,

$$
\rho ^ { m - m _ { 0 } } \leq C _ { \mathrm { g e o } } \varphi _ { m } , \qquad m \geq m _ { 1 } .\tag{S.15.3}
$$

Consequently, if

$$
d _ { j } \leq C _ { r } \varphi _ { j } , \qquad j \geq m _ { 0 } ,
$$

then

$$
\sum _ { j = m _ { 0 } } ^ { m - 1 } \rho ^ { m - 1 - j } d _ { j } \leq C _ { r } C _ { \mathrm { g e o } } \varphi _ { m } , \qquad m \geq m _ { 1 } .
$$

Proof. By the minimality of $n _ { m }$

$$
T \leq t ( n _ { m } ) - t ( n _ { m - 1 } ) < T + \beta _ { n _ { m } - 1 } \leq T + \beta _ { n _ { 0 } } .
$$

This holds for $m \geq 1$ . Summing over the first m blocks gives

$$
m T \leq t ( n _ { m } ) - t ( n _ { 0 } ) \leq m ( T + \beta _ { n _ { 0 } } ) , \qquad m \geq 1 .\tag{S.15.4}
$$

Integral comparison for $\begin{array} { r } { t ( n ) = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n - 1 } ( N _ { 0 } + k ) ^ { - 6 } } \end{array}$ , together with (S.15.4), yields constants $c _ { T , - } , c _ { T , + } >$ 0 and $M < \infty$ such that

$$
c _ { T , - } ( m + 1 ) ^ { 1 / ( 1 - 6 ) } \leq N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } \leq c _ { T , + } ( m + 1 ) ^ { 1 / ( 1 - 6 ) } , \qquad m \geq M .\tag{S.15.5}
$$

Set

$$
p : = \frac { r } { 1 - { \mathfrak { b } } } , \qquad \psi _ { m } : = ( m + 2 ) ^ { - p } \sqrt { \log ( m + 2 ) } .
$$

Equation (S.15.5) gives constants $c _ { - } , c _ { + } > 0$ such that

$$
c _ { - } \psi _ { m } \leq \varphi _ { m } \leq c _ { + } \psi _ { m } , \qquad m \geq M .\tag{S.15.6}
$$

For $m \geq j \geq M$

$$
\frac { \psi _ { j } } { \psi _ { m } } = \left( \frac { m + 2 } { j + 2 } \right) ^ { p } \sqrt { \frac { \log ( j + 2 ) } { \log ( m + 2 ) } } \leq \left( \frac { m + 2 } { j + 2 } \right) ^ { p } \leq ( m - j + 1 ) ^ { p } .
$$

Hence, by (S.15.6),

$$
\varphi _ { j } \leq C ( m - j + 1 ) ^ { p } \varphi _ { m } , \qquad m \geq j \geq M .\tag{S.15.7}
$$

Putting $k = m - 1 - j$ in (S.15.7) gives

$$
\sum _ { j = M } ^ { m - 1 } \rho ^ { m - 1 - j } \varphi _ { j } \le C \varphi _ { m } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \rho ^ { k } ( k + 2 ) ^ { p } \le C _ { \mathrm { g e o } } \varphi _ { m } ,
$$

because $\rho \in ( 0 , 1 )$ . The finitely many terms with $m _ { 0 } \le j < M$ are $O ( \rho ^ { m - M } ) = O ( \varphi _ { m } )$ , since an exponential sequence decays faster than a polynomial-logarithmic one. This proves (S.15.2). The same comparison gives $\rho ^ { m - m _ { 0 } } = { \cal O } ( \varphi _ { m } )$ , proving (S.15.3). Finally, if $d _ { j } \leq C _ { r } \varphi _ { j }$ , multiply the first bound by $C _ { r }$ to obtain the stated consequence. □

Lemma S.15.4 (Block estimates for nonharmonic slow steps). Suppose $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ . There exist $C _ { T } < \infty$ and $m _ { T } < \infty$ such that, for every $m \geq m _ { T }$ 2

$$
D _ { m } \leq ( T + 1 ) \beta _ { n _ { m } } , \qquad K _ { m } \leq C _ { T } ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { \mathfrak { b } } ,
$$

and

$$
L _ { m } ^ { x } + L _ { m } ^ { \theta } \leq C _ { T } \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) .
$$

Proof. Set $X _ { m } : = N _ { 0 } + n _ { m }$ . Minimality of $n _ { m + 1 }$ gives

$$
a ( n _ { m } , n _ { m + 1 } ) < T + \beta _ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \leq T + \beta _ { n _ { 0 } } \leq T + 1 ,
$$

and hence

$$
D _ { m } \leq \beta _ { n _ { m } } \sum _ { k = n _ { m } } ^ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \beta _ { k } \leq ( T + 1 ) \beta _ { n _ { m } } .
$$

Let

$$
\overline { { K } } _ { m } : = \left\lceil 2 ^ { \mathfrak { b } } T X _ { m } ^ { \mathfrak { b } } \right\rceil .
$$

Because ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ , eventually $\overline { { K } } _ { m } \leq X _ { m }$ , and then

$$
\begin{array} { r } { t ( n _ { m } + \overline { { K } } _ { m } ) - t ( n _ { m } ) \geq \overline { { K } } _ { m } ( X _ { m } + \overline { { K } } _ { m } ) ^ { - \mathfrak { h } } \geq 2 ^ { - \mathfrak { h } } \overline { { K } } _ { m } X _ { m } ^ { - \mathfrak { h } } \geq T . } \end{array}
$$

Minimality therefore yields

$$
K _ { m } = n _ { m + 1 } - n _ { m } \leq \overline { { K } } _ { m } \leq C _ { T } X _ { m } ^ { \mathfrak { h } } .
$$

Finally, (S.15.5) implies $m + 2 \leq C _ { T } X _ { m } ^ { 1 - 6 }$ eventually, so

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { L _ { m } ^ { x } + L _ { m } ^ { \theta } = \log \{ 4 d _ { x } K _ { m } ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } \} + \log \{ 4 d _ { \theta } K _ { m } ( m + 2 ) ^ { 2 } \} } } \\ & { } & { \leq C + 2 \log K _ { m } + 4 \log ( m + 2 ) \leq C _ { T } \log X _ { m } . } \end{array}
$$

Increasing $m _ { T }$ and $C _ { T } ,$ if necessary, proves the result.

Lemma S.15.5 (Block estimates for harmonic slow steps). Suppose ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ , and set

$$
X _ { m } : = N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } , \qquad A _ { T } : = e ^ { T } .
$$

Then, for every $m \geq 0$

$$
( A _ { T } - 1 ) ( X _ { m } - 1 ) \leq K _ { m } < ( A _ { T } - 1 ) X _ { m } + 1 .
$$

Moreover, there exists $\xi _ { T } > 0$ such that

$$
X _ { m } = \xi _ { T } e ^ { m T } + { \cal O } _ { T } ( 1 ) \qquad a s m  \infty .
$$

Finally, there exist $C _ { T } < \infty$ and a deterministic integer $m _ { T } \ge 0$ such that, for every $m \geq m _ { T }$ 9

$$
\begin{array} { r } { D _ { m } \leq ( T + 1 ) X _ { m } ^ { - 1 } , \qquad L _ { m } ^ { x } + L _ { m } ^ { \theta } \leq C _ { T } \log X _ { m } , } \end{array}
$$

and, whenever $n _ { m } \leq n < n _ { m + 1 }$ ，

$$
X _ { m } \leq N _ { 0 } + n < X _ { m + 1 } < e ^ { T } X _ { m } + 1 \leq ( e ^ { T } + 1 ) X _ { m } .
$$

Proof. Minimality of $n _ { m + 1 }$ gives

$$
T \leq \sum _ { r = 0 } ^ { K _ { m } - 1 } \frac { 1 } { X _ { m } + r } < T + \frac { 1 } { X _ { m + 1 } - 1 } .
$$

Integral comparison, applied also to the same sum with its last term removed, gives

$$
T \leq \log \frac { X _ { m } + K _ { m } - 1 } { X _ { m } - 1 } , \qquad \log \frac { X _ { m } + K _ { m } - 1 } { X _ { m } } < T ,
$$

which proves the asserted bounds on $K _ { m }$ . Hence

$$
X _ { m + 1 } = e ^ { T } X _ { m } + \eta _ { m } , \qquad - ( e ^ { T } - 1 ) \leq \eta _ { m } < 1 .
$$

Thus

$$
e ^ { - m T } X _ { m } = X _ { 0 } + \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { m - 1 } e ^ { - ( j + 1 ) T } \eta _ { j } \longrightarrow \xi _ { T } ,
$$

and the tail of this absolutely convergent series gives $X _ { m } = \xi _ { T } e ^ { m T } + O _ { T } ( 1 )$ . Also, $X _ { m + 1 } - 1 \geq$ $e ^ { T } ( X _ { m } - 1 )$ , SO $\xi _ { T } \ge X _ { 0 } - 1 > 0$

Since $\beta _ { k } \leq X _ { m } ^ { - 1 }$ on block $m .$

$$
D _ { m } \leq X _ { m } ^ { - 1 } \sum _ { k = n _ { m } } ^ { n _ { m + 1 } - 1 } \beta _ { k } \leq ( T + 1 ) X _ { m } ^ { - 1 } .
$$

The preceding estimates give $K _ { m } = O _ { T } ( X _ { m } )$ and $m = O _ { T } ( \log X _ { m } )$ . Substitution in the definitions of $L _ { m } ^ { x }$ and $L _ { m } ^ { \theta }$ proves the logarithmic bound. The final within-block comparison follows from the upper bound on $K _ { m }$ □

Lemma S.15.6 (Geometric convolution for harmonic slow steps). Suppose ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ . Fix $\rho \in ( 0 , 1 )$ $r > 0$ , and $\ell \geq 0$ , and set

$$
\varphi _ { m } : = ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) \} ^ { \ell } , \qquad \lambda _ { \rho , T } : = - \frac { \log \rho } { T } .
$$

When $\rho = \rho _ { T }$ , the quantity $\lambda _ { \rho , T }$ coincides with $\lambda _ { T }$ in (8.7). For every fixed $m _ { 0 }$ , as $m \to \infty$

$$
\rho ^ { m - m _ { 0 } } + \sum _ { j = m _ { 0 } } ^ { m - 1 } \rho ^ { m - 1 - j } \varphi _ { j } = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { O ( \varphi _ { m } ) , } & { \lambda _ { \rho , T } > r , } \\ { O \left( ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - r } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) \} ^ { \ell + 1 } \right) , } & { \lambda _ { \rho , T } = r , } \\ { O \left( ( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) ^ { - \lambda _ { \rho , T } } \right) , } & { 0 < \lambda _ { \rho , T } < r . } \end{array} \right.
$$

The same bounds hold with $\varphi _ { j }$ replaced by any nonnegative sequence satisfying $d _ { j } \leq C \varphi _ { j }$

Proof. Lemma S.15.5 gives $N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } \asymp e ^ { m T }$ and log $( N _ { 0 } + n _ { m } ) \asymp m + 1$ . Therefore,

$$
\varphi _ { m } \asymp e ^ { - r T m } ( m + 1 ) ^ { \ell } .
$$

Up to constant factors, the convolution is

$$
\sum _ { j = m _ { 0 } } ^ { m - 1 } \rho ^ { m - 1 - j } e ^ { - r T j } ( j + 1 ) ^ { \ell } .
$$

If $\lambda _ { \rho , T } > r ,$ equivalently $\rho < e ^ { - r T }$ , factor out $e ^ { - r T ( m - 1 ) }$ and sum the convergent geometric series in

$$
( \rho e ^ { r T } ) ^ { m - 1 - j } .
$$

If $\lambda _ { \rho , T } = r$ , equivalently $\rho = e ^ { - r T }$ , sum $( j + 1 ) ^ { \ell } . \mathrm { ~ I f ~ } 0 < \lambda _ { \rho , T } < r$ , equivalently $\rho > e ^ { - r T }$ , factor out $\rho ^ { m - 1 }$ and sum the convergent series in

$$
\left( { \frac { e ^ { - r T } } { \rho } } \right) ^ { j } ( j + 1 ) ^ { \ell } .
$$

These give, respectively,

$$
O ( e ^ { - r T m } m ^ { \ell } ) , \qquad O ( e ^ { - r T m } m ^ { \ell + 1 } ) , \qquad O ( \rho ^ { m } ) .
$$

The initial transient has the same or smaller order, and converting back from m proves the result.

Lemma S.15.7 (Finite-state Kac cycle identity). Let $P$ be a transition matrix on a nite state space $\mathcal { V } ,$ with unique stationary distribution $\mu .$ Fix $y ^ { \star } \in \mathcal { V }$ , and let $( Y _ { n } ) _ { n \geq 0 }$ be the homogeneous Markov chain with transition matrix $P ,$ started from $Y _ { 0 } = y ^ { \star }$ . Let $\mathbb { E } _ { y } ,$ denote expectation under this law, and set

$$
\tau _ { y ^ { \star } } : = \operatorname* { i n f } \{ n \geq 1 : Y _ { n } = y ^ { \star } \} .
$$

$I f \mathbb { E } _ { y ^ { \star } } [ \tau _ { y ^ { \star } } ] < \infty$ , then, for every integer $d _ { g } \geq 1$ and every $g : \mathcal { V } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { g } }$ ，

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { y ^ { \star } } \left[ \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \tau _ { y ^ { \star } } - 1 } g ( Y _ { n } ) \right] = \frac { 1 } { \mu ( y ^ { \star } ) } \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ( y ) g ( y ) .
$$

Proof. For $j \in \mathcal { V }$ , define the finite cycle occupation measure

$$
\nu ( j ) : = \mathbb { E } _ { y ^ { \star } } \left[ \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \tau _ { y ^ { \star } } - 1 } \mathbf { 1 } \{ Y _ { n } = j \} \right] .
$$

It is finite because

$$
\sum _ { j \in \mathcal { y } } \nu ( j ) = \mathbb { E } _ { y ^ { \star } } [ \tau _ { y ^ { \star } } ] < \infty .
$$

The Markov property and $Y _ { 0 } = Y _ { \tau _ { u ^ { \star } } } = y ^ { \star }$ give, for every $j ,$

$$
\sum _ { i \in \mathcal { V } } \nu ( i ) P ( i , j ) = \mathbb { E } _ { y ^ { \star } } \left[ \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \tau _ { y ^ { \star } } } \mathbf { 1 } \{ Y _ { n } = j \} \right] = \nu ( j ) .
$$

Thus $\nu$ is a finite invariant measure. Uniqueness of the stationary distribution implies $\nu = c \mu$ while the first-return property gives $\nu ( y ^ { \star } ) = 1$ , and hence $c = 1 / \mu ( y ^ { \star } )$ . Therefore

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { y ^ { \star } } \left[ \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \tau _ { y ^ { \star } } - 1 } g ( Y _ { n } ) \right] = \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { V } } \nu ( j ) g ( j ) = \frac { 1 } { \mu ( y ^ { \star } ) } \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ( j ) g ( j ) ,
$$

as claimed.

## S.16 Step-size shift and the starting index of the analysis

This appendix describes two ways to ensure that the step-size conditions required by the analysis hold. We can choose $N _ { 0 }$ sufficiently large and begin the analysis at $n = 1$ , or take $N _ { 0 } = 1$ and begin the analysis at a sufficiently large index $n _ { 0 }$ . In both cases, the algorithm runs from $n = 1$

## S.16.1 Choosing $N _ { 0 }$ to begin the analysis at $n = 1$

This is the convention already adopted throughout the main paper. We first give a sufficient lower bound on $N _ { 0 }$ for the analysis to apply from $n = 1$ . For the blockwise fast analysis in Section $6 ,$ choose $N _ { 0 }$ so that

$$
\left( N _ { 0 } + 1 \right) ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak { a } } } \geq 4 .
$$

Equivalently, it is sufficient to take

$$
N _ { 0 } \geq \left\lceil 4 ^ { 1 / ( 1 - \mathfrak { a } ) } \right\rceil - 1 .
$$

The named domination inequality (S.5.1) then holds automatically because $A _ { x } ~ \geq ~ A _ { \theta } , ~ C _ { \theta } ~ =$ $C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { \theta }$ , and $\beta _ { 1 } \leq \alpha _ { 1 }$ . The small-step condition is used in the local martingale and Poissonresidual estimates. No additional infinite-horizon condition involving a fixed fast-martingale threshold is needed: the block-dependent thresholds will be chosen later so that their failure probabilities are summable.

## S.16.2 Beginning the analysis at $n = n _ { 0 }$ when $N _ { 0 } = 1$

Alternatively, take $N _ { 0 } = 1$ and define

$$
n _ { 0 } : = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ n \geq 1 : ( n + 1 ) ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak a } } \geq 4 \} .
$$

The algorithm runs from $n = 1$ , while the blockwise analysis begins at $n = n _ { 0 }$ , using the iterates generated by the algorithm up to that point. We retain the original iteration index and slow-time grid. For every $n \geq n _ { 0 }$

$$
\alpha _ { n } = ( n + 1 ) ^ { - 0 } , \qquad \beta _ { n } = ( n + 1 ) ^ { - 6 } , \qquad ( n + 1 ) ^ { 1 - 0 } \geq 4 .
$$

We now establish the analogue of Lemma S.14.1 for sums beginning at $n _ { 0 }$ . For every $n \geq n _ { 0 }$

$$
\sum _ { k = n _ { 0 } } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } , \quad \quad \sum _ { k = n _ { 0 } } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ^ { 2 } ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } .\tag{S.16.1}
$$

Indeed, at $n = n _ { 0 }$ , the first sum is $\alpha _ { n _ { 0 } } ^ { 2 } \leq 2 \alpha _ { n _ { 0 } }$ . If the first inequality holds with terminal index $n - 1$ , the product structure of $\chi \ \mathrm { g i }$ ves

$$
\begin{array} { c l } { \displaystyle \sum _ { k = n _ { 0 } } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) = \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } + ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) \displaystyle \sum _ { k = n _ { 0 } } ^ { n - 1 } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ( k + 1 , n - 1 ) } \\ { \leq \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } + 2 ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) \alpha _ { n - 1 } . } \end{array}
$$

Moreover,

$$
\frac { \alpha _ { n - 1 } } { \alpha _ { n } } = \left( \frac { n + 1 } { n } \right) ^ { \mathfrak { a } } \leq \frac { n + 1 } { n } \leq 1 + \frac { 2 } { n + 1 } ,
$$

and $4 / ( n + 1 ) \leq \alpha _ { n }$ because $( n + 1 ) ^ { 1 - { \mathfrak { a } } } \geq 4$ . Consequently,

$$
\sum _ { k = n _ { 0 } } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } - \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } + \frac { 4 \alpha _ { n } } { n + 1 } \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } .
$$

This proves the first inequality in (S.16.1) by induction. The second follows from $0 \leq \chi ^ { 2 } ( k + 1 , n ) \leq$ $\chi ( k + 1 , n )$

Every block entrance satisfies $n _ { m } \ge n _ { 0 }$ . Therefore, whenever the fast-tail or Poisson-residual proof invokes Lemma S.14.1, its relevant block-local sum is bounded instead by the corresponding suffix sum in (S.16.1). For example, for $n \geq n _ { m }$ ，

$$
\sum _ { k = n _ { m } } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ^ { 2 } ( k + 1 , n ) \leq \sum _ { k = n _ { 0 } } ^ { n } \alpha _ { k } ^ { 2 } \chi ^ { 2 } ( k + 1 , n ) \leq 2 \alpha _ { n } \leq 2 \alpha _ { n _ { m } } ,
$$

and the same argument applies without the square on $\chi .$ The weight-variation bound from Lemma S.14.2 is likewise valid on the suffix.

Under the large-shift convention used in the main paper, inequality (S.5.1), together with the monotonicity of $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n } ,$ is used in Appendix S.5, in the proof of Proposition S.5.1, immediately after the summation-by-parts decomposition of (S.5.2), to bound the Poisson-solution increments. When $N _ { 0 } = 1$ , the same inequality holds for every $n \geq n _ { 0 }$ , since

$$
C _ { \theta } \beta _ { n } = C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { \theta } \beta _ { n } \le C _ { h , \mathrm { P o i s . } } A _ { x } \alpha _ { n } , \qquad n \ge n _ { 0 } ,
$$

using $A _ { \theta } \leq A _ { x }$ and $\beta _ { n } \leq \alpha _ { n }$

Finally, the original slow-time grid is retained. The block construction gives

$$
T \leq t ( n _ { m } ) - t ( n _ { m - 1 } ) < T + \beta _ { n _ { m } - 1 } \leq T + \beta _ { n _ { 0 } } ,
$$

and hence

$$
m T \leq t ( n _ { m } ) - t ( n _ { 0 } ) \leq m ( T + \beta _ { n _ { 0 } } ) .
$$

Beginning the block construction at $t ( n _ { 0 } )$ changes only the constants in the block-geometry and geometric-convolution bounds, for both ${ \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ and ${ \mathfrak { b } } = 1$ Thus, every blockwise result assuming (3.3) applies to the blocks beginning at $n _ { 0 }$ , with (S.16.1) providing the required step-size estimate. The corresponding almost-sure rates hold for all sufficiently large original indices n. Since $N _ { 0 } = 1$ , these rates are expressed in terms of $n + 1$ , with the same polynomial and logarithmic exponents.

## S.17 Rates under contraction of the reduced slow map (Proof of Proposition 8.2)

This appendix proves Proposition 8.2. We first establish Lemma S.17.1, which bounds the distance between a noisy projected recursion and a projected auxiliary recursion driven by the same input.

The next lemma assumes that the noise $\xi _ { n + 1 }$ admits the decomposition (S.17.1) into a martingaledifference term $m _ { n + 1 }$ , a difference $R _ { n } - R _ { n + 1 }$ of bounded correction terms, and a small remainder $r _ { n }$ . These auxiliary sequences are introduced only for the analysis and are not computed by the algorithm. When applying the lemma to the slow recursion in the proof of Proposition 8.2, we take

$$
\xi _ { n + 1 } = f ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( y ) f ( y , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) + M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime } .
$$

Here $\xi _ { n + 1 }$ combines the Markov noise with the original martingale-difference noise $M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime }$ . We construct $m _ { n + 1 } , R _ { n }$ , and $r _ { n }$ using the Poisson equation, as shown below; the remainder $r _ { n }$ accounts for changes in the current pair $( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } )$

Lemma S.17.1. Let Θ be compact and convex, and consider adapted sequences

$$
\theta _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \big [ ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \theta _ { n } + \beta _ { n } G _ { n } + \beta _ { n } \xi _ { n + 1 } \big ] , \qquad z _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \big [ ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } G _ { n } \big ] ,
$$

where $G _ { n }$ is $\mathcal { F } _ { n }$ -measurable, $\theta _ { 1 } , z _ { 1 } \in \Theta$ , and $\beta _ { n } = B / t _ { n }$ , with $t _ { n } = N _ { 0 } + n , B > 1 / 2$ , and $0 < \beta _ { n } \leq 1$ Suppose that $G _ { n }$ and $\xi _ { n + 1 }$ are uniformly bounded by deterministic constants and

$$
\xi _ { n + 1 } = m _ { n + 1 } + R _ { n } - R _ { n + 1 } + r _ { n } , \qquad \mathbb { E } [ m _ { n + 1 } \mid \mathcal { F } _ { n } ] = 0 , \qquad \| m _ { n + 1 } \| \le K _ { \mathrm { m d } } ,\tag{S.17.1}
$$

where $R _ { n }$ is adapted and, for finite deterministic constants $P , C _ { r } \geq 0 , \| R _ { n } \| \leq P$ and $\left\| r _ { n } \right\| ^ { 2 } \leq C _ { r } \beta _ { n }$ almost surely for every n. Here $K _ { \mathrm { m d } } : = K _ { \xi } + 2 P + \sqrt { C _ { r } }$ , where $K _ { \xi } < \infty$ is a deterministic uniform bound on $\| \xi _ { n + 1 } \|$ . The bound on $m _ { n + 1 }$ follows from

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \| m _ { n + 1 } \| = \| \xi _ { n + 1 } - R _ { n } + R _ { n + 1 } - r _ { n } \| } } \\ & { } & { \leq K _ { \xi } + 2 P + \sqrt { C _ { r } \beta _ { n } } \leq K _ { \mathrm { m d } } , } \end{array}
$$

using $\beta _ { n } \leq 1$ . In particular, $K _ { \mathrm { m d } }$ is independent of n and of the block index. Then

$$
\left\| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( { \sqrt { \frac { \log t _ { n } } { t _ { n } } } } \right) .\tag{S.17.2}
$$

All norms in this lemma are Euclidean.

Proof. Nonexpansiveness of projection and boundedness of $\xi _ { n + 1 }$ give

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \| \theta _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n + 1 } \| ^ { 2 } \leq ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) ^ { 2 } \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } } \\ & { \qquad + 2 \beta _ { n } ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , \xi _ { n + 1 } \rangle + C \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}\tag{S.17.3}
$$

Here and below, C denotes a finite deterministic constant whose value may increase between inequalities. Since projection fixes every point of Θ, boundedness of the inputs also yields

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| { \theta } _ { n + 1 } - { \theta } _ { n } \| + \| z _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n } \| \leq C \beta _ { n } . } \end{array}
$$

To handle the difference $R _ { n } \mathrm { ~ - ~ } R _ { n + 1 }$ in (S.17.1), we work directly with the squared shifted distance $\lVert \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \rVert ^ { 2 }$ . Expand this square at indices n and $n + 1$ , and substitute (S.17.1) into (S.17.3). The terms involving $R _ { n } - R _ { n + 1 }$ cancel up to an error bounded by $C \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 }$ , giving

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \| \theta _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n + 1 } + \beta _ { n + 1 } R _ { n + 1 } \| ^ { 2 } } \\ & { \quad \leq ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) ^ { 2 } \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } } \\ & { \quad \quad + 2 \beta _ { n } ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , m _ { n + 1 } \rangle } \\ & { \quad \quad + 2 \beta _ { n } ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , r _ { n } \rangle + C \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}\tag{S.17.4}
$$

Indeed, the uncancelled terms are

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { 2 ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , R _ { n } \rangle } \\ & { \quad \quad + 2 \beta _ { n + 1 } \langle ( \theta _ { n + 1 } - \theta _ { n } ) - ( z _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n } ) , R _ { n + 1 } \rangle } \\ & { \quad \quad + 2 \{ \beta _ { n + 1 } - ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \beta _ { n } \} \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , R _ { n + 1 } \rangle } \\ & { \quad \quad + \beta _ { n + 1 } ^ { 2 } \| R _ { n + 1 } \| ^ { 2 } - ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) ^ { 2 } \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } \| R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

Their total absolute value is $O ( \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } )$ , by the preceding increment bound, compactness of $\Theta _ { i }$ boundedness of $R _ { n } .$ and $\beta _ { n + 1 } - ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \beta _ { n } = O ( \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } )$

Fix c with $1 < c < 2 B$ . Using

$$
\lVert \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } \rVert \leq \lVert \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \rVert + P \beta _ { n }
$$

and $\| r _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } \leq C _ { r } \beta _ { n }$ , Young's inequality gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { 2 \beta _ { n } ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \lvert \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , r _ { n } \rangle \rvert } \\ & { \quad \leq ( 2 - c / B ) \beta _ { n } \left. \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \right. ^ { 2 } + C \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

Since $\beta _ { n } = B / t _ { n }$ , inequality (S.17.4) therefore becomes

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \| \theta _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n + 1 } + \beta _ { n + 1 } R _ { n + 1 } \| ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle \quad \leq \left( 1 - \frac { c } { t _ { n } } + \frac { B ^ { 2 } } { t _ { n } ^ { 2 } } \right) \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad + 2 \beta _ { n } ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , m _ { n + 1 } \rangle + \frac { C } { t _ { n } ^ { 2 } } . } \end{array}\tag{S.17.5}
$$

Conditional Hoeffding's inequality, together with $\begin{array} { r } { \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } \leq 2 \left\| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \right\| ^ { 2 } + 2 P ^ { 2 } \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } } \end{array}$ , gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { E } [ \exp \{ 2 s \beta _ { n } ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \langle \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } , m _ { n + 1 } \rangle \} | \mathcal { F } _ { n } ] } \\ & { \quad \le \exp \Bigl \{ 4 K _ { \mathrm { m d } } ^ { 2 } s ^ { 2 } \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } \bigl ( \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } + P ^ { 2 } \beta _ { n } ^ { 2 } \bigr ) \Bigr \} , \qquad s \in \mathbb { R } . } \end{array}\tag{S.17.6}
$$

Choose $d > 0$ so that 4 $B ^ { 2 } K _ { \mathrm { m d } } ^ { 2 } d < c - 1$ . Exponentiate (S.17.5) after multiplying by $d t _ { n + 1 }$ , and apply (S.17.6) with $s = d t _ { n + 1 } = d ( t _ { n } + 1 )$ . The coefficient of $d t _ { n } \left\| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \right\| ^ { 2 }$ in the resulting exponent is

$$
\begin{array} { c } { \displaystyle \frac { t _ { n } + 1 } { t _ { n } } \left( 1 - \frac { c } { t _ { n } } + \frac { B ^ { 2 } } { t _ { n } ^ { 2 } } \right) + 4 B ^ { 2 } K _ { \mathrm { m d } } ^ { 2 } d \frac { ( t _ { n } + 1 ) ^ { 2 } } { t _ { n } ^ { 3 } } } \\ { = 1 - \displaystyle \frac { c - 1 - 4 B ^ { 2 } K _ { \mathrm { m d } } ^ { 2 } d } { t _ { n } } + O ( t _ { n } ^ { - 2 } ) . } \end{array}
$$

The remaining terms in that exponent are bounded by $C / t _ { n }$ . Consequently, there exists $\delta > 0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n ,$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb E \Bigl [ e ^ { d t _ { n + 1 } \| \theta _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n + 1 } + \beta _ { n + 1 } R _ { n + 1 } \| ^ { 2 } } \Bigr | \mathcal F _ { n } \Bigr ] } \\ & { \qquad \le e ^ { C / t _ { n } } \left( e ^ { d t _ { n } \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } } \right) ^ { 1 - \delta / t _ { n } } , \qquad 0 < 1 - \delta / t _ { n } < 1 . } \end{array}
$$

Taking expectations and applying Jensen's inequality to the concave power $u \mapsto u ^ { 1 - \delta / { t _ { n } } }  { y }$ ields

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \log \mathbb { E } \Big [ e ^ { d t _ { n + 1 } \| \theta _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n + 1 } + \beta _ { n + 1 } R _ { n + 1 } \| ^ { 2 } } \Big ] } } \\ & { } & { \leq ( 1 - \frac { \delta } { t _ { n } } ) \log \mathbb { E } \Big [ e ^ { d t _ { n } \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } } \Big ] + \frac { C } { t _ { n } } . } \end{array}
$$

The squared shifted distance is bounded by a deterministic constant, so its exponential moment is finite at every fixed index. Starting at an index where the preceding recursion holds, induction bounds the logarithm of the exponential moment by the larger of its initial value and $C / \delta$ . Including the finite prefix therefore gives

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { n \geq 1 } \mathbb { E } \Big [ e ^ { d t _ { n } \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } } \Big ] < \infty .
$$

Markov's inequality now implies

$$
\mathbb { P } \bigg ( \| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \| ^ { 2 } \geq K \frac { \log { t _ { n } } } { t _ { n } } \bigg ) \leq C t _ { n } ^ { - d K } .
$$

Choose $K > 1 / d$ and apply Borel-Cantelli. Finally,

$$
\left\| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } \right\| \leq \left\| \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } R _ { n } \right\| + { \frac { P B } { t _ { n } } } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( { \sqrt { \frac { \log t _ { n } } { t _ { n } } } } \right) ,
$$

which proves (S.17.2).

Proof of Proposition 8.2. The proof proceeds in four steps:

(i) We identify the projected equilibrium and establish almost-sure convergence of the original iterates under the logarithmically separated step sizes.

(ii) We construct Poisson decompositions for the fast and slow noise. For the fast recursion, we form the recursive weighted average $U _ { n }$ of the fast noise and analyze $p _ { n } = x _ { n } - U _ { n }$ . For the slow recursion, we construct the projected auxiliary sequence $z _ { n }$ and apply Lemma S.17.1 to bound $\lVert \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } \rVert$ . These auxiliary sequences are defined in (S.17.10) and are used only in the proof.

(iii) We show that the auxiliary slow iterate eventually remains in the face of Θ maximizing the inner product with the averaged drift at the equilibrium. On this face, that drift drops out of the projection objective, yielding a sharper bound on the auxiliary iterate's increments.

(iv) We use fast contraction and reduced slow-map contraction to derive coupled error bounds. An induction gives the rate $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) / \sqrt { N _ { 0 } + n } )$ , which we then transfer from the auxiliary sequences to the original iterates.

We use the original fast norm and Euclidean slow norm. Define the full averaged slow drift and its associated map by

$$
F ( x , \theta ) = \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { Y } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) f ( y , x , \theta ) , \qquad G ( x , \theta ) = \theta + F ( x , \theta ) .
$$

Thus $F ( x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) , \theta ) = f ^ { ( \mathrm { a v . } ) } ( \theta ; \theta )$ and $G ( x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) , \theta ) = \overline { { G } } ( \theta )$ . Constants below are deterministic unless stated otherwise; their values may increase from one estimate to the next.

Projected equilibrium and initial convergence. The map $\mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } \circ \overline { { G } } : \Theta  \Theta$ is a contraction, so it has a unique fixed point $\theta ^ { \star }$ . Put $c = 1 - \rho _ { s } > 0$ and $v = F ( x ^ { \star } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) , \theta ^ { \star } )$ . With the outward normal cone convention

$$
N _ { \Theta } ( \theta ) = \{ w : \langle w , y - \theta \rangle \leq 0 { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ e v e r y ~ } } y \in \Theta \} ,
$$

the projection characterization gives

$$
v \in N \Theta ( \theta ^ { \star } ) , \qquad \theta ^ { \star } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } [ \theta ^ { \star } + \beta v ] \quad ( \beta \geq 0 ) .\tag{S.17.7}
$$

At a boundary equilibrium, the averaged slow drift v may be nonzero and point outward. Projection nevertheless leaves $\theta ^ { \star }$ fixed, as expressed in (S.17.7). Contraction of $\overline { G }$ implies

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \langle \theta - \theta ^ { \star } , f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( \theta ; \theta ) - v \rangle \leq - c \| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \| ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

Both $v$ and the normal correction in the projected ODE have nonpositive contributions to the derivative of squared distance to $\theta ^ { \star }$ . Hence its flow satisfies

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| \mathsf { S } _ { t } \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \| \le e ^ { - c t } \| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \| , \qquad t \ge 0 . } \end{array}\tag{S.17.8}
$$

In particular, the equilibrium set is $\mathcal { E } \mathcal { Q } _ { \Theta } = \{ \theta ^ { \star } \}$ , and distance to $\theta ^ { \star }$ is a global Lipschitz Lyapunov function with a uniform one-block contraction.

The initial-convergence argument in Section S.7.6, through (S.7.5) and the subsequent slowblock comparison, applies to the present schedule. That part of the argument uses the standing assumptions, the step-size relations, and Lyapunov decrease; it does not use the box-face or common-target conditions. In the present case $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n } = B / ( A \log t _ { n } ) \to 0 , t _ { n } \alpha _ { n } \to \infty ,$ and both step-size sums diverge. The endpoint ratios on fixed fast-time blocks tend to one, while those on fixed slow-time blocks remain bounded. Thus the same Poisson and martingale comparisons, together with (S.17.8), give

$$
\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log t _ { n } } + \frac { \beta _ { n } } { \alpha _ { n } } \bigg )  0 , \qquad \theta _ { n }  \theta ^ { \star } \quad \mathrm { a l m o s t ~ s u r e l y . }\tag{S.17.9}
$$

The bounds in (S.17.9) follow from the block comparison estimates applied with the step sizes in (8.14).

Noise averaging within the feasible set. Center both noise streams at the actual pair $( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } )$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \xi _ { n + 1 } ^ { x } = h ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } ) + M _ { n + 1 } , } \\ & { \xi _ { n + 1 } ^ { \theta } = f ( Y _ { n } , x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - F ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) + M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime } . } \end{array}
$$

Both admit (S.17.1), with bounded martingale differences and correctors and with $\| r _ { n } \| \le C \alpha _ { n }$ To see this for either observation, let $u _ { x , \theta }$ solve its Poisson equation centered at its full average, and put $u _ { n } = u _ { x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } }$ . Then take

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { R _ { n } = u _ { n } ( Y _ { n } ) , } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { m _ { n + 1 } = M _ { n + 1 } ^ { a } + u _ { n } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) - \displaystyle \sum _ { y \in \mathcal { Y } } p ^ { ( \theta _ { n } ) } ( Y _ { n } , y ) u _ { n } ( y ) , } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { r _ { n } = u _ { n + 1 } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) - u _ { n } ( Y _ { n + 1 } ) , } } \end{array}
$$

where $M ^ { a }$ denotes M or $M ^ { \prime }$ , respectively. The transition rule for $Y _ { n + 1 }$ makes $m _ { n + 1 }$ a martingale difference with respect to the original filtration. The Poisson equation verifies the decomposition exactly, including its index $Y _ { n }$ . Lemma S.10.1 supplies the boundedness and sensitivity estimates for h. To obtain them for $f ,$ put $g _ { x , \theta } ( y ) = f ( y , x , \theta ) - F ( x , \theta )$ . On a bounded fast-state set this function is bounded and satisfies

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { y } \| g _ { x , \theta } ( y ) - g _ { x ^ { \prime } , \theta ^ { \prime } } ( y ) \| \leq C ( \| x - x ^ { \prime } \| + \| \theta - \theta ^ { \prime } \| ) ,
$$

by update Lipschitzness and Lemma 3.1. It is centered under $\mu ^ { ( \theta ) }$ . The return-time solution therefore vanishes at the reference state and equals $Z ^ { ( \theta ) } g _ { x , \theta }$ off that state, where $Z ^ { ( \theta ) }$ is the killed resolvent in (S.10.1). Its uniform bound and the resolvent identity give

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { y } \| u _ { x , \theta } ( y ) \| \leq C , \qquad \operatorname* { s u p } _ { y } \left\| u _ { x , \theta } ( y ) - u _ { x ^ { \prime } , \theta ^ { \prime } } ( y ) \right\| \leq C ( \| x - x ^ { \prime } \| + \| \theta - \theta ^ { \prime } \| ) .
$$

The actual increments are $O ( \alpha _ { n } )$ and $O ( \beta _ { n } )$ , so the sensitivity estimate gives the stated remainder bound. No independence between $M _ { n + 1 } ^ { a }$ and $Y _ { n + 1 }$ is needed.

Define the recursive weighted average $U _ { n }$ of the fast noise and the projected slow auxiliary sequence $z _ { n }$ by

$$
\begin{array} { l l } { { U _ { 1 } = 0 , } } & { { U _ { n + 1 } = ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) U _ { n } + \alpha _ { n } \xi _ { n + 1 } ^ { x } , } } \\ { { } } & { { { p _ { n } } = x _ { n } - U _ { n } , } } \\ { { z _ { 1 } = \theta _ { 1 } , } } & { { z _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } [ ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } G ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) ] , } } & { { \Delta _ { n } = \theta _ { n } - z _ { n } . } } \end{array}\tag{S.17.10}
$$

For completeness, the deterministic averaging weights

$$
w _ { n , i } = \alpha _ { i } \prod _ { j = i + 1 } ^ { n - 1 } ( 1 - \alpha _ { j } ) , \qquad 1 \leq i < n ,
$$

satisfy $\textstyle \sum _ { i < n } w _ { n , i } ^ { 2 } = O ( \alpha _ { n } )$ and $\textstyle \sum _ { i < n } w _ { n , i } \alpha _ { i } = O ( \alpha _ { n } )$ . These follow by applying a multiple of $\alpha _ { n }$ as a supersolution to the respective recursions $v _ { n + 1 } = ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) ^ { 2 } v _ { n } + \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 }$ and $b _ { n + 1 } = ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) b _ { n } + \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 }$ since $\alpha _ { n } - \alpha _ { n + 1 } = o ( \alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } )$ . The weights are eventually nondecreasing in $i ,$ because $1 / \alpha _ { i + 1 } - 1 / \alpha _ { i } \to 0 ;$ each fixed initial weight decays faster than every inverse power of $t _ { n }$ . Summation by parts therefore bounds the coboundary contribution by $O ( \alpha _ { n } )$ , as does the remainder contribution. Applying the Azuma-Hoeffding inequality coordinatewise to $\textstyle \sum _ { i < n } w _ { n , i } m _ { i + 1 }$ , using $\textstyle \sum _ { i < n } w _ { n , i } ^ { 2 } = O ( \alpha _ { n } )$ , gives

$$
\mathbb { P } \left( \left. \sum _ { i < n } w _ { n , i } m _ { i + 1 } \right. _ { 2 } > K \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log t _ { n } } \right) \leq C t _ { n } ^ { - 2 }
$$

for sufficiently large deterministic K. Since these probabilities are summable, the Borel-Cantelli lemma bounds the martingale contribution by $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( \sqrt { \alpha _ { n } \log t _ { n } } )$ . Norm equivalence gives the same order in the original fast norm.

The original slow recursion for $\theta _ { n }$ and the recursion for the projected auxiliary sequence $z _ { n }$ in (S.17.10) have the shared-input form required by Lemma S.17.1. Its remainder condition holds because $\alpha _ { n } ^ { 2 } = O ( \beta _ { n } )$ . Consequently,

$$
\left\| U _ { n } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \frac { \log t _ { n } } { \sqrt { t _ { n } } } \right) , \qquad \left\| \Delta _ { n } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \left( \sqrt { \frac { \log t _ { n } } { t _ { n } } } \right) .\tag{S.17.11}
$$

The inputs and actual iterates, together with the auxiliary sequences $U _ { n } , p _ { n }$ , and $z _ { n }$ , are bounded by deterministic constants. Thus all subsequent Lipschitz estimates may be taken on one enlarged deterministic fast-state ball

The face reached by the auxiliary. Set

$$
S = \mathop { \mathrm { a r g m a x } } _ { y \in \Theta } \langle v , y \rangle , \qquad e _ { n } = F ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - v + \Delta _ { n } .
$$

Equations (S.17.9) and (S.17.11) give $z _ { n }  \theta ^ { \star } , e _ { n }  0$ , and

$$
z _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } [ z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } ( v + e _ { n } ) ] .
$$

Suppose first that $S \neq \Theta$ . A polyhedron has only finitely many distinct normal cones, all closed. Therefore

$$
\delta = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ \mathrm { d i s t } ( v , C ) : C = N _ { \Theta } ( y ) { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ s o m e ~ } } y \in \Theta , \ v \notin C \} > 0 .
$$

Work after $\| e _ { n } \| \leq \delta / 4 . { \mathrm { ~ I f ~ } } z _ { n + 1 } \notin S$ , let $r = ( z _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n } ) / \beta _ { n }$ . Projection optimality gives $v + e _ { n } - r \in$ $N _ { \Theta } ( z _ { n + 1 } )$ , a cone excluding v, so $\| r \| \ge 3 \delta / 4$ . Testing the same normal inequality against $z _ { n }$ yields

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \langle v , r \rangle \geq \| r \| ^ { 2 } - \| e _ { n } \| \| r \| \geq 3 \delta ^ { 2 } / 8 . } \end{array}
$$

Every step ending outside S therefore increases $\langle v , z _ { n } \rangle$ by at least $3 \delta ^ { 2 } \beta _ { n } / 8$ Since $z _ { n } \in \Theta$ and Θ is compact, $\langle v , z _ { n } \rangle$ is bounded above by ma $\mathrm { x } _ { y \in \Theta } \langle v , y \rangle < \infty . \mathrm { ~ I f ~ } z _ { n }$ remained outside S, the preceding increment bound and $\textstyle \sum _ { n } \beta _ { n } = \infty$ would imply $\langle v , z _ { n } \rangle \to \infty$ , a contradiction. Thus $z _ { n }$ enters S after finitely many iterations. After entry, a step leaving S would require a strict increase from its maximum, which is impossible. Hence the auxiliary eventually remains in $S .$

For $z _ { n } , z _ { n + 1 } \in S$ , restrict the projection objective to S. The term involving v is constant there, giving

$$
z _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { S } [ z _ { n } + \beta _ { n } e _ { n } ] , \qquad \| z _ { n + 1 } - z _ { n } \| \leq \beta _ { n } \| e _ { n } \| .\tag{S.17.12}
$$

$\operatorname { I f } S = \Theta$ , the above relations hold from the start. The argument showing that $z _ { n }$ eventually enters and remains in S also applies when Θ is lower-dimensional. If ¿ $\imath \neq 0$ is orthogonal to every difference $y - y ^ { \prime }$ with $y , y ^ { \prime } \in \Theta$ , then $\langle v , y \rangle$ is constant over Θ, so $S = \Theta$ . Only the auxiliary sequence $z _ { n }$ must eventually remain in $S ;$ the noisy iterates $\theta _ { n }$ need not do so. No strict complementarity is required: $\theta ^ { \star }$ may lie on the relative boundary of S. Moreover, the fast equilibrium $x ^ { \star } ( \theta )$ need not be constant as θ varies over S.

Coupled contraction estimates. Recall $q = 1 - \gamma > 0$ , and let K be a Lipschitz constant of $x ^ { \star }$ and let $L _ { g }$ bound the Lipschitz dependence of G on its fast argument. Write

$$
e _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } : = \| p _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( z _ { n } ) \| , \quad \quad e _ { n } ^ { ( \theta ) } : = \| z _ { n } - \theta ^ { \star } \| , \quad \quad \rho _ { n } = \| U _ { n } \| + \| \Delta _ { n } \| , \quad \quad s _ { n } = \frac { \log t _ { n } } { \sqrt { t _ { n } } } .
$$

By (S.17.11), $\rho _ { n } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( s _ { n } )$ . Subtracting the update for $U _ { n + 1 }$ in (S.17.10) from the update for $x _ { n + 1 }$ cancels the noise term $\alpha _ { n } \xi _ { n + 1 } ^ { x }$ . Using $p _ { n } = x _ { n } - U _ { n }$ , we obtain

$$
p _ { n + 1 } = ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) p _ { n } + \alpha _ { n } h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } ) .
$$

Since $x _ { n } - p _ { n } = U _ { n }$ and $\theta _ { n } - z _ { n } = \Delta _ { n }$ , Lipschitz continuity gives, for a deterministic constant L,

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } & { } & { \left\| h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x _ { n } ; \theta _ { n } ) - h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( p _ { n } ; z _ { n } ) \right\| \leq L ( \| U _ { n } \| + \| \Delta _ { n } \| ) = L \rho _ { n } , } \\ & { } & { \| G ( x _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) - G ( p _ { n } , z _ { n } ) \| \leq L ( \| U _ { n } \| + \| \Delta _ { n } \| ) = L \rho _ { n } . } \end{array}
$$

Also,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| e _ { n } \| \le L _ { g } e _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } + ( 1 + \rho _ { s } ) e _ { n } ^ { ( \theta ) } + L \rho _ { n } , } \end{array}
$$

because $f ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( z _ { n } ; z _ { n } ) - v = \overline { { G } } ( z _ { n } ) - \overline { { G } } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) - ( z _ { n } - \theta ^ { \star } )$ . Fast contraction and (S.17.12) consequently give, eventually almost surely,

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { e _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( x ) } \leq ( 1 - q \alpha _ { n } + K L _ { g } \beta _ { n } ) e _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } + K ( 1 + \rho _ { s } ) \beta _ { n } e _ { n } ^ { ( \theta ) } } } \\ { { + \left( L \alpha _ { n } + K L \beta _ { n } \right) \rho _ { n } . } } \end{array}\tag{S.17.13}
$$

For the slow error, compare the auxiliary update with proje $\mathsf { \Omega } _ { 3 } [ ( 1 - \beta _ { n } ) \theta ^ { \star } + \beta _ { n } \overline { { G } } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) ] \ = \ \theta ^ { \star }$ , using (S.17.7). Nonexpansiveness and contraction of $\overline { G }$ give

$$
e _ { n + 1 } ^ { ( \theta ) } \leq ( 1 - c \beta _ { n } ) e _ { n } ^ { ( \theta ) } + L _ { g } \beta _ { n } e _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } + L \beta _ { n } \rho _ { n } .\tag{S.17.14}
$$

Thus the comparison retains the equilibrium normal v without assuming that the averaged drift vanishes at $\theta ^ { \star }$

To close these inequalities, note that

$$
\frac { s _ { n + 1 } } { s _ { n } } = 1 - \frac { 1 } { 2 t _ { n } } + \frac { 1 } { t _ { n } \log t _ { n } } + O ( t _ { n } ^ { - 2 } ) .
$$

Choose $K _ { x } > 2 L / q$ , and then choose $K _ { \theta }$ so large that

$$
c - \frac { L _ { g } K _ { x } + L } { K _ { \theta } } > \frac { 1 } { 2 B } ;
$$

this is possible since $B c \geq 2$ . On each almost-sure path, choose a sufficiently large index N after face identification and all eventual estimates. A finite random C can be chosen so that $\rho _ { n } \leq C s _ { n }$ for $n \ge N , e _ { N } ^ { ( x ) } \le C K _ { x } s _ { N }$ , and $e _ { N } ^ { ( \theta ) } \leq C K _ { \theta } s _ { N }$ . Assuming the latter two bounds at index n, (S.17.14) proves the slow bound at $n + 1$ . For (S.17.13), the decrement below $C K _ { x } s _ { n }$ , divided by $C s _ { n }$ , is at least

$$
\alpha _ { n } ( q K _ { x } - L ) - \beta _ { n } \{ K L _ { g } K _ { x } + K ( 1 + \rho _ { s } ) K _ { \theta } + K L \} .
$$

Since $\beta _ { n } / \alpha _ { n }  0$ and $t _ { n } \alpha _ { n }  \infty$ , this eventually exceeds $K _ { x } ( 1 - s _ { n + 1 } / s _ { n } )$ , proving the fast bound at $n + 1$ . Induction gives $e _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } + e _ { n } ^ { ( \theta ) } = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( s _ { n } )$ . Finally,

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } & { \| \theta _ { n } - \theta ^ { \star } \| \leq e _ { n } ^ { ( \theta ) } + \| \Delta _ { n } \| , \quad } & { \| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta _ { n } ) \| \leq e _ { n } ^ { ( x ) } + \| U _ { n } \| + K \left\| \Delta _ { n } \right\| . } \end{array}
$$

Together with (S.17.11), these inequalities prove (8.15). Lipschitzness of $x ^ { \star }$ gives the same bound for $\| x _ { n } - x ^ { \star } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) \|$ ; Lipschitzness of J and $J ( \theta ^ { \star } ) = 0$ give it for $| J ( \theta _ { n } ) |$ □

## S.18 Stochastic saddle-point learning without strong convexityconcavity

Consider the problem

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } _ { u \in U } \operatorname* { m a x } _ { v \in V } \mathcal { L } ( u , v ) , \qquad \mathcal { L } ( u , v ) = \varphi ( u ) + u ^ { \mathsf { T } } A v - \psi ( v ) ,\tag{S.18.1}
$$

where $U \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { u } }$ and $V \subset \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { v } }$ are nonempty compact convex polyhedra, $A \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { u } \times d _ { v } }$ , and $\varphi$ and $\psi$ are convex differentiable functions with Lipschitz gradients on neighborhoods of $U$ and $V .$ Write $\theta = ( u , v )$ and $\Theta = U \times V$ . Let $\theta ^ { \star } = ( u ^ { \star } , v ^ { \star } )$ be a saddle point, so that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal L ( u ^ { \star } , v ) \leq \mathcal L ( u ^ { \star } , v ^ { \star } ) \leq \mathcal L ( u , v ^ { \star } ) , \qquad u \in U , \ v \in V . } \end{array}
$$

Assumption S.18.1 (Radial power curvature). There exist $q > 2$ and $c _ { \mathrm { s p } } > 0$ such that

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } & { } & { \langle \boldsymbol { u } - \boldsymbol { u } ^ { \star } , \nabla \varphi ( \boldsymbol { u } ) - \nabla \varphi ( \boldsymbol { u } ^ { \star } ) \rangle + \langle \boldsymbol { v } - \boldsymbol { v } ^ { \star } , \nabla \psi ( \boldsymbol { v } ) - \nabla \psi ( \boldsymbol { v } ^ { \star } ) \rangle } \\ & { } & { \quad \ge c _ { \mathrm { s p } } \| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \| ^ { q } , \qquad \theta = ( u , v ) \in \Theta . } \end{array}\tag{S.18.2}
$$

All norms in this section are Euclidean. Condition (S.18.2) permits curvature to vanish at the saddle and does not require strong convexity-concavity. Convexity-concavity alone, however, does not guarantee convergence of the descent-ascent ODE, which can cycle even in bilinear games.

Define the descent-ascent field

$$
F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( u , v ) : = ( - \nabla \varphi ( u ) - A v , ~ A ^ { \mathsf { T } } u - \nabla \psi ( v ) ) .\tag{S.18.3}
$$

Suppose the finite controlled Markov chain satisfies Assumptions 3.1, 3.2, and 3.6. Let $\widehat { F } _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( y , \theta )$ be bounded and Lipschitz in $\theta ,$ uniformly in $y ,$ with stationary mean

$$
\sum _ { y \in \mathcal { V } } \mu ^ { ( \theta ) } ( y ) \widehat { F } _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( y , \theta ) = F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ) , \qquad \theta \in \Theta .\tag{S.18.4}
$$

Consider

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { x _ { n + 1 } = ( 1 - \alpha _ { n } ) x _ { n } + \alpha _ { n } \widehat { F } _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( Y _ { n } , \theta _ { n } ) , } \\ & { \theta _ { n + 1 } = \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta } ( \theta _ { n } + \beta _ { n } x _ { n } ) . } \end{array}\tag{S.18.5}
$$

The fast variable estimates the entire saddle field; both primal and dual variables evolve on the slow time scale. Initialize $\theta _ { 1 } \in \Theta$ and $\| x _ { 1 } \| \le R$ almost surely, where $R$ is a deterministic bound also satisfying sup ${ \it \Psi } _ { y , \theta } \left\| \widehat { F } _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( y , \theta ) \right\| \leq R$

Proposition S.18.1 (Saddle-point learning under power curvature). Under the preceding conditions, the reduced projected ODE has the unique equilibrium $\theta ^ { \star }$ , and $J ( \theta ) : = \| \theta - \theta ^ { \star } \|$ satisfies Assumption 8.2 with power $p _ { J } = q - 1$ . For recursion (S.18.5), choose

$$
\alpha _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 0 } , \qquad \beta _ { n } = ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 6 } , \qquad \mathfrak { a } = \frac { 2 ( q - 1 ) } { 4 q - 5 } , \quad \mathfrak { b } = \frac { 3 ( q - 1 ) } { 4 q - 5 } , \qquad \mathfrak { a } = \frac { 3 ( q - 1 ) } { 4 q - 5 } .\tag{S.18.6}
$$

with $N _ { 0 }$ satisfying (3.3). Then

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { \left\| \theta _ { n } - \theta ^ { \star } \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \frac { 1 } { 4 q - 5 } } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 ( q - 1 ) } } \bigg ) , } } \\ { { \left\| x _ { n } - F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta _ { n } ) \right\| = O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } \bigg ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - \frac { q - 1 } { 4 q - 5 } } \sqrt { \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) } \bigg ) . } } \end{array}\tag{S.18.7}
$$

The saddle may lie on the boundary of $\Theta ,$ :no strict-complementarity condition is imposed.

Proof. In (1.1)−(1.2), take $h ( y , x , \theta ) { } = \widehat { F } _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( y , \theta ) , f ( y , x , \theta ) { } = x$ and $M _ { n + 1 } = M _ { n + 1 } ^ { \prime } = 0$ . By (S.18.4), the averaged fast map is constant in x:

$$
h ^ { \mathrm { ( a v . ) } } ( x ; \theta ) = F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ) , \qquad x ^ { \star } ( \theta ) = F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ) .
$$

Thus Assumption 3.4 holds with contraction factor zero. The oracle regularity and $f ( y , x , \theta ) = x$ verify Assumption 3.5. Since $0 < \alpha _ { n } \leq 1$ ，

$$
\| x _ { n + 1 } \| \leq \left( 1 - \alpha _ { n } \right) \| x _ { n } \| + \alpha _ { n } R \leq R
$$

whenever $\| x _ { n } \| \leq R$ This proves Assumption 3.7; the martingale assumption holds trivially. The controlled-chain assumptions were imposed above.

The reduced slow field is $F _ { \mathrm { s p } }$ . Let $\theta ( t )$ solve its projected ODE, and use the outward normal cone

$$
N _ { \Theta } ( \theta ) : = \{ w : \langle w , z - \theta \rangle \leq 0 { \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ e v e r y ~ } } z \in \Theta \} .
$$

The saddle inequalities imply $F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) \in N _ { \Theta } ( \theta ^ { \star } )$ . Almost everywhere,

$$
\dot { \theta } ( t ) = F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ( t ) ) - \eta ( t ) , \qquad \eta ( t ) \in N _ { \Theta } ( \theta ( t ) ) .
$$

For $d = \theta ( t ) - \theta ^ { \star }$ , monotonicity of the normal cone gives $\langle d , \eta ( t ) - F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) \rangle \geq 0$ . Consequently, cancellation of the bilinear terms yields

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \frac { d } { d t } \frac { \| d \| ^ { 2 } } { 2 } \le \langle d , F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ( t ) ) - F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ^ { \star } ) \rangle } } \\ & { } & { \quad = - \langle u - u ^ { \star } , \nabla \varphi ( u ) - \nabla \varphi ( u ^ { \star } ) \rangle - \langle v - v ^ { \star } , \nabla \psi ( v ) - \nabla \psi ( v ^ { \star } ) \rangle } \\ & { } & { \quad \le - c _ { \mathrm { s p } } \| d \| ^ { q } . } \end{array}\tag{S.18.8}
$$

Applying the same inequality to a constant equilibrium trajectory shows that every equilibrium equals $\theta ^ { \star }$ . The function J is therefore 1-Lipschitz and has exactly the required zero set. Wherever $J ( \theta ( t ) ) > 0 , ( \mathrm { S } . 1 8 . 8 )$ gives

$$
\frac { d } { d t } J ( \theta ( t ) ) \leq - c _ { \mathrm { s p } } J ( \theta ( t ) ) ^ { q - 1 } .
$$

If a trajectory reaches $\theta ^ { \star }$ , it remains there by uniqueness of the projected flow. Integrating the preceding inequality gives, for each fixed $T > 0$ ，

$$
J ( \mathsf { S } _ { T } ( \theta ) ) \le J ( \theta ) \big [ 1 + c _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( q - 2 ) T J ( \theta ) ^ { q - 2 } \big ] ^ { - 1 / ( q - 2 ) } .\tag{S.18.9}
$$

In particular, J is a Lyapunov function, and

$$
\mathcal { D } _ { J } ( u ) : = u - u \big [ 1 + c _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( q - 2 ) T u ^ { q - 2 } \big ] ^ { - 1 / ( q - 2 ) }
$$

is continuous, vanishes at zero, and satisfies $0 < \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( u ) \le u$ for $u > 0$ . Moreover,

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { u \downarrow 0 } \frac { \mathcal { D } _ { J } ( u ) } { u ^ { q - 1 } } = c _ { \mathrm { s p } } T > 0 .
$$

This verifies the uniform one-block decrease and its power lower bound with $p _ { J } = q - 1$ . Since $q > 2$ , the exponents in (S.18.6) satisfy $1 / 2 < { \mathfrak { a } } < { \mathfrak { b } } < 1$ . Corollary 8.2 now gives (S.18.7). □

A family with vanishing curvature. Let $0 \in U \times V$ and take

$$
\varphi ( u ) = \frac { \| u \| ^ { q } } { q } , \qquad \psi ( v ) = \frac { \| v \| ^ { q } } { q } , \qquad q > 2 ,\tag{S.18.10}
$$

with arbitrary dimensions and arbitrary A. The origin is a saddle, and

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \langle u , \nabla \varphi ( u ) \rangle + \langle v , \nabla \psi ( v ) \rangle = \| u \| ^ { q } + \| v \| ^ { q } \geq 2 ^ { 1 - q / 2 } \| ( u , v ) \| ^ { q } . } \end{array}
$$

Thus (S.18.2) holds. The Hessians of both penalties vanish at zero, so on sets containing a neighborhood of zero neither strong convexity nor strong concavity is available. The proposition also applies when zero lies on the boundary. For example, $q = 4 { \mathrm { ~ g i v e s ~ } } { \mathfrak { a } } = 6 / 1 1 , 6 = 9 / 1 1$ , and slow-iterate rate $O _ { \mathrm { a . s . } } ( ( N _ { 0 } + n ) ^ { - 1 / 1 1 } \{ \log ( N _ { 0 } + n ) \} ^ { 1 / 6 } )$ . These are the rates supplied by the general power-decrease theorem; no optimality claim is made.

Remark S.18.1 (Comparison with existing frameworks). The obstruction in these applications is in the reduced slow dynamics; the fast averaged map is contractive. The comparisons below concern the hypotheses of the cited results, rather than possible extensions of their proof techniques.

For (S.18.10), suppose 0 is interior to Θ and $A \neq 0$ . Then

$$
\begin{array} { r } { D F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( 0 ) = R _ { A } : = \left( \begin{array} { c c } { 0 } & { - A } \\ { A ^ { \top } } & { 0 } \end{array} \right) . } \end{array}
$$

If $\sigma > 0$ is a nonzero singular value of A, the derivative of the forward map $\theta \mapsto \theta + h F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta )$ has eigenvalues $1 \pm i h \sigma$ , whose moduli exceed one for every $h > 0$ . Projection is inactive in a neighborhood of zero for each fixed h. Hence even the projected forward map is not locally nonexpansive in any fixed norm. This rules out the reduced-map contraction hypotheses of Chandak [6], Chandak et al. [9] and the nonexpansive-map hypothesis of Chandak [5] for this family. The nonconvex-gradient extension in Chandak [5] does not apply either: $R _ { A }$ is nonsymmetric, so $F _ { \mathrm { s p } }$ cannot be the negative Euclidean gradient of a scalar objective near zero. Its minimax specialization, which uses strong concavity in the fast player, also differs from (S.18.5), where the fast variable estimates the field and neither player requires strong curvature.

The contractive-map analysis of Chen et al. [10] likewise does not cover this reduced map. The more general drift condition in Chen et al. [11, Assumption 2.2] requires uniform quadratic dissipation toward an unprojected root. Here

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \langle \theta , F _ { \mathrm { s p } } ( \theta ) \rangle = - \| u \| ^ { q } - \| v \| ^ { q } , } \end{array}
$$

which cannot be bounded above by $- c \left\| \theta \right\| ^ { 2 }$ with $c > 0$ near zero. Nor can a positive-definite quadratic change of Lyapunov function restore local exponential dissipation: the linearization $R _ { A }$ has nonzero purely imaginary eigenvalues. The power-decrease condition (S.18.9) is sufficient for our result.

For the actor-critic recursion in Section 9, the lack of a nonexpansive slow map is already visible in a one-state, two-action MDP. Take costs 0 and 1, deterministic return to the same state, $B _ { \mathrm { a c } } = 1$ and write $p = ( 1 + e ^ { - ( \vartheta _ { 1 } - \vartheta _ { 2 } ) } ) ^ { - 1 }$ . At the exact critic, the averaged actor field is

$$
F _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( \vartheta ) = \bigl ( p ( 1 - p ) , - p ( 1 - p ) \bigr ) .
$$

Its Jacobian has eigenvalue $2 p ( 1 - p ) ( 1 - 2 p ) > 0$ in direction $( 1 , - 1 )$ when $p < 1 / 2$ At $\vartheta =$ $\left( - 1 / 2 , 1 / 2 \right)$ , both this point and $\vartheta + F _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( \vartheta )$ are interior to the logit box. Thus $\vartheta \mapsto \mathrm { p r o j } _ { \Theta _ { \mathrm { a c } } } ( \vartheta +$ $F _ { \mathrm { a c } } ( \vartheta ) )$ is not locally nonexpansive in any fixed norm. The same obstruction persists for sufficiently small positive forward steps. At the constrained optimum (1, -1), the actor field is nonzero and points outward, although the projected velocity is zero. Consequently, neither a reduced-map contraction/nonexpansiveness condition nor an unprojected-root condition follows from the hypotheses of our actor-critic application. The unprojected slow-gradient result in Chandak [5] does not provide a rate to this constrained equilibrium. In the full application, the controlled Markov sampling and value-based Lyapunov function must also be retained.

The actor-critic result concerns precisely the global-clock recursion stated in Section 9, motivated by Konda and Borkar [18, Algorithm 3]; it does not extend the present rate claim to the original visit-count or delayed implementation. Neither projection nor Markov noise alone distinguishes our analysis from all earlier work. The point of these two applications is that projected slow-ODE Lyapunov decrease can hold when the slow-map assumptions in the comparison results fail.
